# 1. PREOPERATIVE ASSESSMENT, PREMEDICATION AND PERIOPERATIVE DOCUMENTATION
<!-- EPA-1:K1 EPA-1:K2 EPA-1:K3 EPA-1:S1 EPA-1:EQ1 -->

## 1.1. PREAMBLE
<!-- EPA-1:K3:prehabilitation -->

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|Part A: Physiological Assessment]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|Part B: Preoperative Evaluation]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|Part C: Documentation Standards]]

**Exam Weighting:** High (Core knowledge for anesthesia practice)

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient Care, Professionalism

* Cornerstones of effective preop assessment are that of medical history and physical examination [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The evaluation may include investigations or referrals.
* An enhanced recovery may require "prehabilitation" with one or more of the following: smoking cessation, nutritional supplementation, an exercise regimen, and adjustment of medications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. **See Section 5.0 for comprehensive prehabilitation protocols including exercise prescription, nutritional optimization, psychological preparation, and behavioral modification strategies.**
* The preoperative evaluation will often guide the anesthetic plan [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Inadequate preoperative planning and incomplete patient preparation commonly lead to avoidable delays, cancellations, complications, and costs [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]].
* The preop evaluation has multiple purposes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]:
    * Identify patients whose outcomes likely will be improved by a specific medical treatment
    * Identify patients whose condition is so poor that proposed surgery might only hasten death without improving quality of life
    * Uncover findings that may change anaesthetic plan
    * Estimation of anaesthetic risk
    * Description of the proposed anaesthetic plan in the context of the overall surgical and postop plan; and its corresponding informed consent
    * Provision of psychological support

**Bottom Line:** Effective preoperative assessment combines thorough history, physical examination, appropriate investigations, and optimization strategies to guide the anesthetic plan and improve patient outcomes.

## 1.2. SAMPLE ANAESTHETIC PLAN

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|Part B: Anesthetic Planning]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|Part C: Documentation]]

**Competencies:** Patient Care, Professionalism 

1. Will sedative-hypnotic premedication be useful? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
2. What type(s) of anaesthesia will be employed? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * General
    * Airway management
    * Induction drugs
    * Need for/avoidance of muscle relaxation
    * Maintenance drugs
    * Regional
    * Technique
    * Agents
    * Sedation and MAC
    * Supplemental O2
    * Specific sedative drugs
3. Are there special intraoperative management issues [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Non standard monitors
    * Positions other than supine
    * Relative or absolute contraindications to specific anaesthetic drugs
    * Fluid management
    * Special techniques
    * Site (anaesthetizing location) concerns
4. How will the patient be managed postoperatively? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Management of acute pain
    * Intensive care
    * Postoperative ventilation
    * haemodynamic monitoring

**Bottom Line:** A comprehensive anesthetic plan should address premedication needs, type of anesthesia, specific intraoperative management issues, and postoperative care strategies including pain management and ICU needs if applicable.

## 1.3. RISK ASSESSMENT
<!-- EPA-1:K2:risk_stratification EPA-1:EQ1 EPA-1:EQ3 -->

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|Part A: Risk Principles]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|Part B: Risk Stratification]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|Part C: Patient Communication]]

**Exam Weighting:** High (Critical for safe patient care)

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient Care, Professionalism 

1. If stratifying based on comorbidities: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * The ASA physical status (ASA PS) is helpful but subjective and has intraoperative variability [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is also limited as it does not take into account preop physiological and functional status of patient, type of surgery and postop care.

    ![Untitled](PREOP_Untitled.png_86a956.png)

#### 1.3.1.1. DETAILED ASA PHYSICAL STATUS CLASSIFICATION SYSTEM [ASA 2020]

**Purpose and Clinical Application:**
The ASA Physical Status Classification System has been in use for over 60 years. The purpose of the system is to assess and communicate a patient's pre-anesthesia medical co-morbidities. The classification system alone does not predict the perioperative risks, but used with other factors (eg, type of surgery, frailty, level of deconditioning), it can be helpful in predicting perioperative risks.

**Clinical Decision Making:**
Assigning a Physical Status classification level is a clinical decision based on multiple factors. While the Physical Status classification may initially be determined at various times during the preoperative assessment of the patient, the final assignment of Physical Status classification is made on the day of anesthesia care by the anesthesiologist after evaluating the patient.

**Comprehensive ASA Classification with Examples (2020 Update):**

**ASA I - Normal Healthy Patient:**
* **Definition:** A normal healthy patient
* **Adult Examples:** Healthy, non-smoking, no or minimal alcohol use
* **Pediatric Examples:** Healthy (no acute or chronic disease), normal BMI percentile for age

**ASA II - Mild Systemic Disease:**
* **Definition:** A patient with mild systemic disease
* **Adult Examples:** Mild diseases only without substantive functional limitations. Current smoker, social alcohol drinker, pregnancy, obesity (30<BMI<40), well-controlled DM/HTN, mild lung disease
* **Pediatric Examples:** Asymptomatic congenital cardiac disease, well controlled dysrhythmias, asthma without exacerbation, well controlled epilepsy, non-insulin dependent diabetes mellitus, abnormal BMI percentile for age, mild/moderate OSA, oncologic state in remission, autism with mild limitations
* **Obstetric Examples:** Normal pregnancy*, well controlled gestational HTN, controlled preeclampsia without severe features, diet-controlled gestational DM

💡 **Pregnancy Classification Note:** Although pregnancy is not a disease, the parturient's physiologic state is significantly altered from when the woman is not pregnant, hence the assignment of ASA 2 for a woman with uncomplicated pregnancy.

**ASA III - Severe Systemic Disease:**
* **Definition:** A patient with severe systemic disease
* **Adult Examples:** Substantive functional limitations; One or more moderate to severe diseases. Poorly controlled DM or HTN, COPD, morbid obesity (BMI ≥40), active hepatitis, alcohol dependence or abuse, implanted pacemaker, moderate reduction of ejection fraction, ESRD undergoing regularly scheduled dialysis, history (>3 months) of MI, CVA, TIA, or CAD/stents.
* **Pediatric Examples:** Uncorrected stable congenital cardiac abnormality, asthma with exacerbation, poorly controlled epilepsy, insulin dependent diabetes mellitus, morbid obesity, malnutrition, severe OSA, oncologic state, renal failure, muscular dystrophy, cystic fibrosis, history of organ transplantation, brain/spinal cord malformation, symptomatic hydrocephalus, premature infant PCA <60 weeks, autism with severe limitations, metabolic disease, difficult airway, long term parenteral nutrition. Full term infants <6 weeks of age.
* **Obstetric Examples:** Preeclampsia with severe features, gestational DM with complications or high insulin requirements, a thrombophilic disease requiring anticoagulation.

**ASA IV - Severe Systemic Disease with Constant Threat to Life:**
* **Definition:** A patient with severe systemic disease that is a constant threat to life
* **Adult Examples:** Recent (<3 months) MI, CVA, TIA or CAD/stents, ongoing cardiac ischemia or severe valve dysfunction, severe reduction of ejection fraction, shock, sepsis, DIC, ARD or ESRD not undergoing regularly scheduled dialysis
* **Pediatric Examples:** Symptomatic congenital cardiac abnormality, congestive heart failure, active sequelae of prematurity, acute hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy, shock, sepsis, disseminated intravascular coagulation, automatic implantable cardioverter-defibrillator, ventilator dependence, endocrinopathy, severe trauma, severe respiratory distress, advanced oncologic state.
* **Obstetric Examples:** Preeclampsia with severe features complicated by HELLP or other adverse event, peripartum cardiomyopathy with EF <40, uncorrected/decompensated heart disease, acquired or congenital.

**ASA V - Moribund Patient:**
* **Definition:** A moribund patient who is not expected to survive without the operation
* **Adult Examples:** Ruptured abdominal/thoracic aneurysm, massive trauma, intracranial bleed with mass effect, ischemic bowel in the face of significant cardiac pathology or multiple organ/system dysfunction
* **Pediatric Examples:** Massive trauma, intracranial hemorrhage with mass effect, patient requiring ECMO, respiratory failure or arrest, malignant hypertension, decompensated congestive heart failure, hepatic encephalopathy, ischemic bowel or multiple organ/system dysfunction.
* **Obstetric Examples:** Uterine rupture.

**ASA VI - Brain-Dead Organ Donor:**
* **Definition:** A declared brain-dead patient whose organs are being removed for donor purposes

**Emergency Modifier (E):**
The addition of "E" denotes Emergency surgery: (An emergency is defined as existing when delay in treatment of the patient would lead to a significant increase in the threat to life or body part)

#### 1.3.1.2. ASA CLASSIFICATION CLINICAL CONSIDERATIONS [ASA GUIDELINES & EVIDENCE]

**Historical Context and Evolution:**
In 1941, the American Society of Anesthesiologists (ASA) tasked Meyer Saklad, Emery Rovenstine and Ivan Taylor with devising a method with which they could collect and tabulate outcomes from anaesthesia. This classification system has undergone several revisions since its inception, with its most recent amendment in 2020.

**Predictive Value and Limitations:**
Meta-analyses have shown that the ASA physical status classification has a sensitivity of approximately 0.74 (95% CI, 0.73 to 0.74), a specificity of approximately 0.67 (95% CI, 0.67 to 0.67), and a receiver operating curve area under the summary of around 0.736 (95% CI, 0.725 to 0.747) in predicting mortality postoperatively.

**Interrater Reliability Issues:**
Additional limitations in the ASA classification system include its nonbinary nature, which introduces high interrater variability when determining a range of disease severity rather than disease presence or absence. The same comorbidity may be present in more than one classification score, and judgment regarding comorbidity severity can vary significantly between healthcare providers, groups, or systems.

**Age Considerations:**
⚠️ Notably, age does not appear in the ASA physical status classification system, though neonates and older adults may be more sensitive to the effects of anesthesia than children and young adults, even in the absence of disease.

**Improved Accuracy with Examples:**
Adding examples to the ASA-Physical Status classification improves correct assignments to patients. The definitions and examples shown in the table below are guidelines for the clinician. To improve communication and assessments at a specific institution, anesthesiology departments may choose to develop institutional-specific examples to supplement the ASA-approved examples.

**Comprehensive Risk Assessment Requirements:**
For a robust and reliable preoperative risk assessment, it is essential to consider a range of additional factors discretely in addition to the information provided by a composite index classification, including:
* Type of surgery and urgency classification (elective, urgent, emergent)
* Patient frailty and functional status
* Need for blood products, volume and timing requirements
* Intended medications and timing requirements
* Expected postoperative care level

⚠️ **Misuse Concerns:** ASA PS classification system is incorrectly used for purposes beyond the original intent including billing modifiers and decisions regarding inpatient vs. ambulatory care for individual patients.

💡 **Clinical Utility:** Despite these limitations, the ASA physical status classification system remains one of the most widely used assessment and communication tools to describe a surgical patient's overall condition.

* For cardiac perioperative risk stratification (based on comorbidities), certain scoring systems are recommended [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These include the National Surgical Quality Improvement Programme (NSQIP) model, Charlson Comorbiditiy Index (CCI) or the revised cardiac risk index (RCRI)

2. If stratifying based on functional status/exercise tolerance: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Functional status is measured in metabolic equivalents (METS) [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|ⓘ]]. 1 MET is the energy consumed by the body at rest and is equivalent to 3.5 mL of oxygen consumed per kilogram of body weight per minute [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|ⓘ]]. The ability to climb a flight of stairs/ walk 600m/ 2 busstops without stopping equates to a moderate exercise capacity which corresponds to 4 METS - interpreted as lower risk of CVS complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
    * Another test includes that of the cardiopulmonary exercise testing (CPET) - It integrates the assessment of cardiac, respiratory, and metabolic variables in a condition simulating that of surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

3. If stratifying based on type of surgery: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * The prevalence of major CVS events (MACE) varies according to type of surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Minor surgery - i.e. cataract, cosmetic surgery - <1% risk for MACE. ENT, uro and ortho procedures have 1% risk. General abdominal or intrapertioneal surgery or NES procedures - >3% risk. Peripheral vascular surgery, thoracic and transplant surgery - >5%

### 1.3.2. *RISK STRATIFICATION TOOLS:*

* Physiological and operative severity score for the enumeration of mortality and morbidity (POSSUM) can be used in both elective and emergency cases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Over the years, POSSUM was modified to Portsmouth POSSUM (P‑POSSUM) which corrects for the tendency of POSSUM to overestimate surgical mortality in low-risk patients.
* Surgical APGAR score (SAS) is a 10‑point scale that was initially developed for evaluating patients after general surgery or vascular procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It helps in predicitng postop ICU admission.
* Acute physiology and chronic health evaluation (APACHE II) analysis is commonly used in the ICU [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Even though it is not a surgical or anaesthetic score, it is comprehensive. It estimates the patient's clinical and biological status accurately, allowing for mortality prediction.
* Various risk assessment methods have been described to assess perioperative complications related to the respiratory and cardiovascular systems. [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Risk indices for assessment of postop pulmonary complications include - ARISCAT risk index, arozullah respiratory failure index etc.
    * Risk indices for assessment of periop cardiac complications include Goldman cardiac index, revised cardiac risk index, ANESCARDIOCAT

#### 1.3.2.1. PRACTICAL ASA CLASSIFICATION APPLICATION [CLINICAL GUIDELINES]

**Day-of-Surgery Assessment Protocol:**
The final ASA Physical Status classification should be determined on the day of anesthesia care by the anesthesiologist after evaluating the patient. This final assessment may differ from preliminary classifications made during preoperative clinics due to:
* Changes in patient condition since initial assessment
* Additional information obtained from recent investigations
* Clinical findings on day of surgery examination
* Updated medication regimens or treatment responses

**Institutional Adaptations:**
To improve communication and assessments at a specific institution, anesthesiology departments may choose to develop institutional-specific examples to supplement the ASA-approved examples. This helps address:
* Local patient populations and common comorbidity patterns
* Subspecialty-specific considerations (cardiac, pediatric, obstetric)
* Regional disease prevalence and treatment protocols

**Documentation and Communication:**
* **Clear Documentation:** Record the rationale for ASA classification, especially for borderline cases
* **Multidisciplinary Communication:** Use ASA status as part of broader risk communication with surgical teams
* **Patient Discussion:** Include ASA status in informed consent discussions about anesthetic risk
* **Quality Improvement:** Track ASA classification accuracy and outcomes for institutional learning

**Common Classification Challenges:**
* **Age-Related Changes:** Consider functional rather than chronological age impact
* **Multiple Comorbidities:** Evaluate cumulative vs. individual disease severity
* **Well-Controlled vs. Poorly-Controlled Disease:** Distinguish between stable chronic conditions and active disease processes
* **Emergency Cases:** Apply emergency modifier appropriately based on time-sensitive nature of condition

💡 **Best Practice:** Use ASA classification as part of a comprehensive risk assessment rather than as a standalone predictor. Combine with functional assessment, surgical factors, and other validated risk tools for optimal perioperative planning.

#### 1.3.2.2. ASA CLASSIFICATION IN SPECIAL POPULATIONS [SUBSPECIALTY CONSIDERATIONS]

**Pediatric ASA Classification Considerations:**
* **Age-Specific Factors:** Full term infants <6 weeks of age are automatically classified as ASA III due to physiologic immaturity
* **Developmental Considerations:** Include autism spectrum disorders with functional limitations in classification
* **Congenital Conditions:** Distinguish between corrected, stable uncorrected, and symptomatic congenital abnormalities
* **Prematurity Impact:** Premature infants with PCA <60 weeks are classified as ASA III minimum
* **Growth Parameters:** Consider BMI percentiles for age rather than absolute BMI values

**Obstetric ASA Classification Specifics:**
* **Normal Pregnancy:** Uncomplicated pregnancy is classified as ASA II due to physiologic changes
* **Gestational Complications:** Distinguish severity of gestational diabetes, hypertension, and preeclampsia
* **Peripartum Conditions:** Include specific obstetric emergencies and complications
* **Cardiac Considerations:** Peripartum cardiomyopathy with EF <40 warrants ASA IV classification

**Cardiac Surgery Considerations:**
* **Temporal Factors:** Recent (<3 months) cardiac events significantly impact classification
* **Functional Status:** Consider ejection fraction and current cardiac symptoms
* **Device Dependencies:** Pacemaker dependence and AICD presence affect classification
* **Procedural Urgency:** Emergency cardiac procedures often involve ASA IV-V patients

**Trauma and Emergency Surgery:**
* **Multiple Injury Assessment:** Consider cumulative impact of multiple system involvement
* **Hemodynamic Stability:** Shock state elevates classification regardless of underlying condition
* **Time-Sensitive Nature:** Apply emergency modifier appropriately
* **Pre-injury Status:** Consider baseline health status where possible

**Chronic Disease Management:**
* **Control vs. Presence:** Well-controlled chronic diseases may warrant lower classification than poorly controlled conditions
* **Functional Impact:** Assess actual functional limitations rather than just disease presence
* **Multi-morbidity:** Consider cumulative effect of multiple chronic conditions
* **End-Organ Damage:** Include complications and sequelae in classification

⚠️ **Documentation Requirements:** For complex cases, document specific factors contributing to ASA classification to improve communication and reduce inter-rater variability.

#### 1.3.2.3. ASA CLASSIFICATION OUTCOMES AND EVIDENCE [RESEARCH FINDINGS]

**Mortality Prediction Accuracy:**
Research demonstrates clear correlation between ASA classification and perioperative outcomes:
* **Progressive Risk:** There is a varying trend of increasing likelihood of complications and mortality with increasing ASA class across different procedure types
* **Statistical Performance:** Meta-analyses show sensitivity ~74%, specificity ~67%, with area under ROC curve of 0.736 for mortality prediction
* **Outcome Utility:** Despite limitations, ASA classification remains valuable for outcome prediction and quality improvement initiatives

**Interrater Reliability Studies:**
* **Variability Issues:** Studies show significant variation in ASA assignment between different anesthesiologists, especially for borderline cases
* **Improvement with Examples:** Addition of specific examples and institutional guidelines improves agreement rates
* **Training Impact:** Standardized training and calibration exercises reduce inter-rater variability
* **Subspecialty Differences:** Agreement rates vary between different anesthesia subspecialties

**Modern Validation Studies:**
* **2017 Study (Hurwitz et al.):** Adding examples to ASA Physical Status classification improves correct assignments to patients (Anesthesiology 2017; 126:614-22)
* **2019 Review (Mayhew et al.):** Comprehensive review of historical perspectives and modern developments (Anaesthesia 2019; 74:373-9)
* **Pediatric Studies:** Mixed results for pediatric-specific examples on improving classification accuracy

**Quality Improvement Applications:**
* **Risk Stratification:** Use for case mix adjustment in outcome reporting
* **Resource Allocation:** Guide perioperative resource planning and staffing
* **Audit and Monitoring:** Track outcomes by ASA class for quality improvement
* **Research Stratification:** Standard tool for research patient stratification

**Limitations in Contemporary Practice:**
* **Disease Complexity:** Modern patients often have multiple well-controlled chronic conditions that don't fit neatly into single categories
* **Medical Advances:** Current treatments may render previously severe conditions more manageable
* **Population Changes:** Aging population and obesity epidemic create classification challenges
* **Emergency vs. Elective:** Distinction between patient's baseline status and acute presentations

💡 **Evidence-Based Practice:** While ASA classification has limitations, it remains the most widely validated and used preoperative assessment tool. Its utility is maximized when combined with other risk assessment methods and used consistently within institutions.

**Bottom Line:** Risk assessment should incorporate multiple factors including ASA status, functional capacity, comorbidities, and type of surgery. The ASA Physical Status Classification System serves as a standardized communication tool to assess preoperative comorbidities but should not be used alone for risk prediction. Current ASA classification (updated 2020) includes comprehensive examples for adult, pediatric, and obstetric patients across all six classes. Validated tools like RCRI, NSQIP, and POSSUM can help quantify risk, though each has limitations. Exercise tolerance remains one of the most important predictors of perioperative outcomes. For optimal risk assessment, combine ASA classification with functional status evaluation, surgery type, and other validated risk indices.

## 1.4. ELEMENTS OF THE PREOPERATIVE HISTORY

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to comprehensive preoperative assessment]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Communication skills and documentation requirements]]

**Exam Weighting:** High (Fundamental to anesthesia practice)

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient Care* Patients presenting for elective surgery and anesthesia typically require the recording of a focused medical history emphasizing abnormalities of exercise tolerance; nutritional and functional status; cardiac, pulmonary, endocrine, kidney, or liver function; electrolytes or metabolism; and anatomic issues relevant to airway management or regional anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Prev anaesthetic hx should include [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Easy/Difficult airway/intubation, ETT/LMA size, larynx grade
    * Postop nausea vomiting
    * Other complications - i.e. malignant hyperthermia

### 1.4.1. CARDIOVASCULAR ISSUES
<!-- EPA-1:K1:cardiovascular EPA-1:EQ3 -->

* The focus of preoperative cardiac assessment should be on determining whether the patient would benefit from further cardiac evaluation or interventions prior to the scheduled surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, the same approach is not appropriate for all patients.
* The prudent approach to a patient undergoing elective knee arthroplasty will differ from that for a patient needing resection of pancreatic cancer, given the benign results of a delay in the former procedure and the possible deadly effects of a delay in the latter procedure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* In general, the indications for cardiovascular investigations are the same in elective surgical patients as in any other patient with a similar medical condition [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Put another way, the fact that a patient is scheduled to undergo elective surgery does not change the indications for testing to diagnose coronary artery disease.
* Summarized tips for individual diseases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * IHD
    * Symptoms (unstable angina) and classification (IHD is ACS, unstable angina, STEMI or NSTEMI?) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Latest ix (TTE, MPI, Coros) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Intervention done (PCI/CABG/Medical therapy; ?incomplete revascularization) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Cardiac stent - DAPT/SAPT duration varies acc to DES (6-12m) vs bare metal stent (4-6 weeks) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * *Indication for stent placement predicts periop cardiac complication more than exact type of stent [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * General recc [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * ⚠️ Avoid surgery within 1m of BMS placement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Immediately after stenting, such patients are at increased risk of acute myocardial infarction if clopidogrel (or related agents) and aspirin are abruptly discontinued [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * If recent <6w BMS req. surgery, continue DAPT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * ⚠️ Avoid non-urgent surgery for at least 12m following DES placement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * If surgery cannot be deferred after DES [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Consult cardiology [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Continue DAPT if stent within 6m [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * If stent >6m ago, stop clopidogrel for 5d, continue aspirin [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Cardiology referral only for high risk pts, changes in cardiac status (angina, palpitation, syncope, dyspnea, poor effort tolerance, new changes in ECG, new murmur) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * ⚠️ **Perioperative ACE-I/ARB Management:** Complex decision requiring individualized approach
    * **Current Controversy:** Conflicting international guidelines exist regarding perioperative management
    * **2014 ACC/AHA:** Recommend continuation (Level B evidence)
    * **2017 CCS:** Recommend withholding 24 hours before surgery (Strong recommendation, low-quality evidence)  
    * **2014 ESC/ESA:** Continue for heart failure patients, consider transient discontinuation for hypertensive patients
    * **Evidence Summary:**
    * **VISION Study:** 20% relative risk reduction in intraoperative hypotension when withheld 24h preop
    * **Meta-analyses:** 30% relative risk increase in hypotension if continued, but no association with MACE or mortality
    * **Limited RCT data:** Only 6 RCTs with methodological limitations and mixed results
    * **Clinical Decision Framework:**
    * **Consider withholding if:** Anticipated hypotensive events (non-supine positioning, major blood loss, neuraxial anesthesia), polypharmacy (especially with diuretics), altered neurohormonal feedback (elderly, diabetic autonomic neuropathy)
    * **Consider continuing if:** Strong cardiac indications (LV dysfunction, previous MI, high RCRI), low-risk procedures, uncontrolled hypertension with target organ damage
    * **Drug-specific timing:** Longer half-life drugs (enalapril, lisinopril, losartan) - hold 24 hours; shorter-acting (captopril) - hold >10 hours
    * **Special Considerations:**
    * **Refractory hypotension:** May require vasopressin or angiotensin II for treatment
    * **Renal effects:** Conflicting evidence on acute kidney injury risk
    * **Anesthetic type:** Higher risk with neuraxial anesthesia due to sympathectomy
    * **Resume early:** Restart postoperatively when clinically feasible
    * Heart failure
    * Symptoms (NYHA, orthopnea) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * EF (and when it was taken; if taken in acute episode may not be true reflection) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Frequency and last decompensation/ fluid overload [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Valvular lesions
    * Symptoms [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Severity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Arrhythmias
    * Rate control/ Rhythm control [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * **CIED and latest device check status** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * **Basic Assessment:** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Determine indication, type and mode [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Determine dependence on pacing function - any symptomatic bradyarrhythmia, ECG (look for pacing spikes/capture) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Determine function [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Device interrogation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Battery check [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Whether there is a need to convert to asynchronous mode for surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * 💡 **Comprehensive CIED Management:** For detailed information on CIED physics, device interrogation, EMI management, and perioperative protocols, see **Section 8: CARDIAC IMPLANTABLE ELECTRONIC DEVICE (CIED) MONITORING** in 4.4. CARDIOVASCULAR MONITORING notes `[Bryant2016]`
    * **Key Preoperative Considerations:** `[Bryant2016]`
    * **Device types:** PPM, ICD, CRT-P, CRT-D, ILR `[Bryant2016]`
    * **Pacing dependency assessment critical** for perioperative planning `[Bryant2016]`
    * **Interrogation timing:** Within 12 months for PPM, 6 months for ICD preferred `[Bryant2016]`
    * **EMI risk stratification:** Based on surgical site (above/below umbilicus) and diathermy type `[Bryant2016]`
    * **Medication optimization:** Continue anti-arrhythmics, correct electrolyte abnormalities (especially Mg2+) `[Bryant2016]`
    * **Document:** European Pacemaker Patient Identification Card information `[Bryant2016]`
    * HTN
    * **Perioperative Risk Assessment Based on Latest Evidence:**
    * **Risk thresholds:** Recent studies show no clinically significant association between hypertension (defined as systolic <180 mm Hg and diastolic <110 mm Hg) and perioperative complications in noncardiac surgery
    * **⚠️ Severe hypertension:** Systolic ≥180 mm Hg or diastolic ≥110 mm Hg requires careful evaluation - insufficient evidence for optimal management threshold
    * **Cardiovascular procedures:** Hypertension poses significant risk in cardiac surgery, with systolic hypertension (>140 mm Hg), systolic hypervariability, and pulse pressure hypertension (>80 mm Hg) being risk factors for perioperative death, stroke, LV dysfunction, and renal failure
    * **Vascular procedures:** Perioperative hypertension is significant risk factor for neurologic deficit in carotid endarterectomy and carotid stenting
    * **LV hypertrophy complications:** Presence of LV hypertrophy adds significant perioperative cardiovascular risk even in noncardiac surgery
    * **Clinical Assessment:**
    * ⚠️ Severe or DBP >100mmHg may be a/w increased mortality and cardiac complications perioperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Some may have altered autoregulation ie. cerebral [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * If not urgent, send home because it takes at least a week, ideally want cardiac remodelling (above treating the numbers) which can take weeks to months [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * **Preop evaluation:**
    * Evaluate control [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Exclude secondary cause [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Evaluate end-organ comorbidities - CVS, CVD, Renal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * **Peri-operative targets:**
    * 10-20% above or below baseline (MAP), DBP should not exceed 100mmHg [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Continue antiHTN even after NBM to prevent rebound hypertension but most will hold off ACEI or ARB, or reduce dose if on single agent on the morning of operation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * **Enhanced ACE-I/ARB Perioperative Management Based on Recent Evidence:**
    * **Current Clinical Controversy:** One of the most controversial topics in contemporary anesthesia practice
    * **Conflicting International Guidelines:**
    * **2014 ACC/AHA:** Recommend continuation (Class IIa, Level B evidence)
    * **2017 CCS:** Recommend withholding 24 hours before surgery (Strong recommendation, low-quality evidence)
    * **2014 ESC/ESA:** Continue for heart failure patients, consider discontinuation for hypertensive patients
    * **Key Evidence Summary:**
    * **VISION Study (n=14,687):** 20% relative risk reduction in intraoperative hypotension when withheld 24h preop, 18% relative risk reduction in composite outcome of death/stroke/myocardial injury
    * **Meta-analyses:** 30% relative risk increase in hypotension if continued, but no association with MACE or mortality
    * **Limited RCT data:** Only 6 RCTs with methodological limitations and mixed results
    * **PREOP-ACEI Trial:** Less intraoperative hypotension with withdrawal (55% vs 69%) but increased postoperative hypertension
    * **Pathophysiology of Perioperative Risk:**
    * **Intact RAS dependence:** Surgery and anesthesia increase reliance on renin-angiotensin system for hemodynamic stability
    * **Alternative pathways:** ACE-I allows conversion to vasodilatory angiotensin 1-7 via alternative enzymes
    * **Anesthetic interactions:** Volatile agents and neuraxial anesthesia enhance vasodilation in RAS-blocked patients
    * **Catecholamine-refractory hypotension:** May require vasopressin or terlipressin for treatment
    * **Clinical Decision Framework:**
    * **Consider withholding if:**
    * Anticipated hypotensive events (non-supine positioning, major blood loss, neuraxial anesthesia)
    * Polypharmacy concerns (especially concurrent diuretics)
    * Altered neurohormonal feedback systems (elderly, diabetic autonomic neuropathy)
    * **Consider continuing if:**
    * Strong cardiac indications (LV dysfunction, previous MI, high RCRI score)
    * Low-risk procedures with minimal expected hemodynamic changes
    * Uncontrolled hypertension with target organ damage
    * **Drug-Specific Timing Considerations:**
    * **Longer half-life drugs:** (enalapril, lisinopril, losartan) - omit 24 hours before surgery
    * **Shorter-acting agents:** (captopril) - omit night before (>10 hours)
    * **Intraoperative Management Strategies:**
    * **If therapy continued:** Tailored induction, volume expansion, early vasopressor use, alternative agents for refractory hypotension
    * **If therapy withheld:** Monitor for rebound hypertension, early resumption postoperatively
    * **Special Considerations:**
    * **Renal effects:** Conflicting evidence on acute kidney injury risk
    * **Anesthetic type influence:** Higher risk with neuraxial anesthesia due to sympathectomy
    * **Refractory hypotension treatment:** Vasopressin or terlipressin more effective than high-dose catecholamines
    * **Current Expert Recommendations:** Individualized approach based on patient factors, surgical factors, and institutional capabilities rather than universal protocols

💡 **ESC Guidelines** - [2022 ESC Guidelines on cardiovascular assessment and management of patients undergoing non-cardiac surgery](https://academic.oup.com/eurheartj/article/43/39/3826/6675076?login=true#393966606) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Cardiovascular assessment should be targeted and appropriate to the specific patient and procedure. Key elements include evaluating IHD status, heart failure symptoms, valvular disease, arrhythmias including CIED management, and blood pressure control. The urgency of surgery must be weighed against the need for cardiac optimization.

### 1.4.2. PULMONARY ISSUES
<!-- EPA-1:K1:pulmonary EPA-1:EQ2 -->

* The risk of postoperative respiratory complications is closely associated with these factors, and with the following: ASA physical status 3 and 4, cigarette smoking, surgeries lasting longer than 4 h, certain types of surgery (abdominal, thoracic, aortic aneurysm, head and neck, and emergency surgery), and general anesthesia (compared with cases in which general anesthesia was not used) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Summarized tips for specific diseases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Asthma/COPD
	    * Existing COPD increases risk of postop pulmonary complications - atelectasis, pneumonia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Evaluate severity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Frequency and severity of exacerbations, last attack date [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Response to meds [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Steroids can also cause pt to have adrenal insufficiency [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Hx of hospital admissions and ventilatory support [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Effort tolerance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * RHF [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Presence of complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Infections [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Hx of PTX [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Is there carcinoma [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Treat reversible causes and optimise therapy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Predictors of severe attack intraop: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * using >3cans salbutamol/yr [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * multiple prednisolone courses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * 1 severe exacerbation in last 1yr [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * prev ICU stay [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * smoker [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Approach [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * ⚠️ Postpone nonurgent surgery if there is exacerbation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Smoking - ideally 8w cessation allowing lung mucosa to recover and to reduce airway reactivity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * **Vaping/E-Cigarettes** - emerging perioperative concern with distinct considerations:
<!-- EPA-1:EQ2:vaping -->
		    * **Always ask specifically about vaping** - patients often do not equate vaping with "smoking" and may not volunteer this information
		    * **Respiratory Effects:**
			    * Throat irritation, chronic cough, sputum production
			    * Erythematous and irritable airway mucosa with potential bronchial injuries
			    * Nicotine triggers bronchoconstriction via parasympathetic activation
			    * Reduced ciliary function and impaired cough reflex
			    * Increased airway hyperreactivity and bronchospasm risk during anaesthesia
		    * **Cardiovascular Effects:**
			    * Acute increases in HR and BP (nicotine-mediated catecholamine release)
			    * Endothelial dysfunction and oxidative stress
			    * Increased thrombosis risk
			    * Unfavourable shift in myocardial oxygen supply-demand ratio
			    * Increased risk of myocardial arrhythmias
		    * **EVALI (E-cigarette/Vaping Use-Associated Lung Injury):**
			    * Emerged in 2019 outbreak: 2,807 hospitalised cases, 68 confirmed deaths (USA)
			    * **Vitamin E acetate** in THC-containing products is primary causative agent
			    * Symptoms: dyspnoea, chest pain, cough, fever, nausea, weight loss
			    * Diagnosis of exclusion requiring: (1) e-cigarette use within 90 days, (2) pulmonary infiltrates on imaging, (3) absence of infection/alternative diagnosis
			    * CT findings: bilateral ground-glass opacities with basilar predominance
			    * >50% require ICU admission; 26% require mechanical ventilation
			    * Treatment: supportive care, corticosteroids (methylprednisolone 0.5-1 mg/kg)
			    * ⚠️ **Consider EVALI in any vaping patient with acute postoperative respiratory symptoms**
		    * **Wound Healing Concerns:**
			    * E-cigarettes impair wound healing similarly to traditional smoking
			    * Nicotine causes vasoconstriction and impairs collagen remodelling
			    * Studies show higher hospital readmission rates (9.1%) than traditional smokers
			    * Longer operative times and length of stay reported in joint arthroplasty patients
		    * **Comparison with Traditional Smoking:**
			    * No significant carbon monoxide exposure (unlike cigarettes)
			    * Comparable nicotine delivery to experienced smokers
			    * Estimated health risk <5% of cigarette smoking (but NOT zero risk)
			    * Less cytochrome P450 enzyme induction (different drug metabolism effects)
			    * ⚠️ **E-cigarettes are NOT a safe perioperative substitute for smoking cessation**
		    * **Preoperative Assessment Recommendations:** [Source: BJA Education 2021]
			    * Directly question ALL patients about vaping (separate from smoking history)
			    * Document: frequency of use, last use, substances (nicotine vs THC)
			    * THC-containing products carry higher EVALI risk
			    * Consider underlying cardiovascular disease in long-term users
			    * ⚠️ Nicotine concentration labelling often inaccurate (products labelled "0 mg/ml" contained up to 23.9 mg/ml in studies)
		    * **Preoperative Cessation:**
			    * Ideal: 6-8 weeks before surgery (treat like traditional smokers)
			    * Minimum: 2-4 weeks before surgery
			    * Even 12 hours cessation provides some benefit
			    * No specific vaping cessation guidelines exist - apply smoking cessation recommendations
		    * **Preoperative Optimisation Protocol:**
			    * Screen for EVALI symptoms: dyspnoea, cough, chest pain, fever
			    * Consider lung function testing in symptomatic patients
			    * Prophylactic bronchodilator (albuterol 2-4 puffs) pre-procedure for patients with signs of reversible airways disease
		    * 💡 **Clinical Pearl:** Unlike cigarette smokers, vapers may not have elevated carboxyhaemoglobin levels, but nicotine-related cardiovascular and airway effects remain significant perioperative concerns.
		    * **E-Liquid Composition and Harmful Substances:** [Source: BJA Education 2021]
			    * Base components: propylene glycol, glycerol, flavourings, nicotine (up to 36 mg/mL in some products)
			    * Harmful substances detected in vapour:
				    * Carcinogens: aldehydes, polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons, tobacco-specific nitrosamines
				    * Heavy metals: tin, chromium, silver, copper, lead, arsenic, nickel
				    * Organic compounds: glycols, acetone, volatile organic compounds
				    * Microbial contaminants: bacterial endotoxins, fungal glucans
			    * Risk estimate: <5% of cigarette smoking health risks (but NOT zero)
		    * **Youth Vaping Epidemic and Gateway Effect:**
			    * 27.5% of US high school students (mean age 16.1 years) are current users
			    * 10.5% of US middle school students (mean age 12.7 years) are current users
			    * ⚠️ **Gateway effect**: Adolescent e-cigarette users are 3-5× more likely to progress to cigarette smoking
			    * Paediatric nicotine poisoning: exposures in US children <6 years increased 1398% (2012-2015)
		    * **COVID-19 and Vaping Connection:**
			    * Nicotine induces increased ACE2 expression in bronchial epithelial cells
			    * ACE2 is the SARS-CoV-2 binding site, potentially increasing infection susceptibility
			    * 2020 study (n=4,351): COVID-19 diagnosis 5× more likely in young e-cigarette users
			    * Dual users (e-cigarettes + combustible cigarettes): 7× increased COVID risk
		    * **E-Cigarette Device Injuries:**
			    * 3,369 explosion/burn injuries reported to US emergency departments (2015-2019)
			    * Mechanism: thermal runaway of lithium-ion batteries
			    * Common injury locations: face, hands, thigh, groin
			    * Ask patients about any prior device-related injuries during preoperative assessment
		    * **E-Cigarettes as Smoking Cessation Tool:**
			    * 2020 Cochrane review: moderate certainty evidence ENDS increased quit rates vs NRT
			    * 2019 RCT (n=886): 1-year abstinence rate 18% (e-cigarette) vs 9.9% (NRT)
			    * Mechanism: retention of smoking ritual may assist craving reduction
			    * 💡 **Clinical Pearl:** In patients switching from cigarettes to e-cigarettes for cessation, acknowledge reduced harm but counsel on ideal complete cessation before elective surgery
		    * **Intraoperative Case Reports (APSF 2020):**
			    * Case: patient with EVALI history rapidly desaturated to SpO2 51% after induction despite adequate preoxygenation
			    * Persistent increased airway reactivity and impaired diffusion capacity may persist weeks after EVALI
			    * V/Q mismatching can worsen immediately post-induction in daily vapers
			    * ⚠️ **Consider extended preoxygenation and prepare for potential rapid desaturation**
		    * **Paediatric-Specific Guidelines (ASA/SPA/AAP 2021):**
			    * Preoperative screening for e-cigarette use mandatory in adolescents
			    * Identify THC-containing product use (significantly higher EVALI risk)
			    * Encourage both preoperative and long-term postoperative cessation
			    * Consider referral to tobacco cessation programs
			    * Family education on vaping risks is essential
		    * **Drug Interactions and Pharmacological Considerations:**
			    * Less CYP450 enzyme induction vs traditional smoking (PAHs largely absent from vapour)
			    * Volatile organic compounds (toluene) may cause CNS depression and reduce anaesthetic requirements
			    * THC-containing products: standard cannabinoid-related anaesthetic implications apply
			    * PONV protection: unclear (unlike traditional smoking)
			    * Effects on postoperative pain: unknown
	    * Optimise airway function [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Bronchodilator - Ensure patient brings inhalers to OT/check that OT has inhaler [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Corticosteroids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Ab if infection suspected [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Chest PT, sputum clearance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Incentive spirometry [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Perioperative pt education - ie. pain management so they can take deep breaths and cough well [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * In asthma pts - to check if can tolerate NSAIDs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * OSA
	    * Risk of perioperative airway obstruction and respiratory failure while under effects of sedation/ GA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Preop op assessment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * STOPBANG - screening for patients with high BMI >30 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Sleep polysomnography - AHI 6-20, 21-40, >40 (severe) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Severe cases may req. nocturnal CPAP to continue in periop period - ask them to bring to hospital if they have [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * Usually will attempt RA if possible, avoid long acting opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
	    * May need SHD postop; monitor in PACU > 1hr [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

#### 1.4.2.1. ENHANCED OSA ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT [ASA OSA GUIDELINES]

**Preoperative Evaluation Protocol:** Anesthesiologists should work with surgeons to develop a protocol whereby patients in whom the possibility of OSA is suspected on clinical grounds are evaluated long enough before the day of surgery to allow preparation of a perioperative management plan. This evaluation may be initiated in a preanesthesia clinic (if available) or by direct consultation from the operating surgeon to the anesthesiologist.

**Comprehensive Assessment Components:**
* **Medical Records Review** should include (but not be limited to) checking for:
    * History of airway difficulty with previous anesthetics
    * Hypertension or other cardiovascular problems
    * Other congenital or acquired medical conditions
    * Review of sleep studies is encouraged
* **Patient and Family Interview** should include focused questions related to:
    * Snoring, apneic episodes
    * Frequent arousals during sleep (e.g., vocalization, shifting position, extremity movements)
    * Morning headaches and daytime somnolence
* **Physical Examination** should include evaluation of:
    * Airway, nasopharyngeal characteristics
    * Neck circumference, tonsil size, tongue volume

**OSA Risk Assessment Example:**
* **Identification Criteria** (Table 1 from ASA Guidelines):
    * Sleep apnea (documented by sleep study or clinical signs/symptoms)
    * Predisposing physical characteristics: BMI ≥35 kg/m², neck circumference >17" (M) or >16" (F), craniofacial abnormalities affecting airway
    * History of airway obstruction problems with anesthesia/sedation

**Perioperative Risk Scoring:** The severity of the patient's OSA, the invasiveness of the diagnostic or therapeutic procedure, and the requirement for postoperative analgesics should be taken into account in determining whether a patient is at increased perioperative risk from OSA.

**Clinical Decision Making:** If any characteristics noted during the preoperative evaluation suggest that the patient has OSA, the anesthesiologist and surgeon should jointly decide whether to:
1. Manage the patient perioperatively based on clinical criteria alone, or
2. Obtain sleep studies, conduct a more extensive airway examination, and initiate indicated OSA treatment in advance of surgery

⚠️ **Day of Surgery Evaluation:** If this evaluation does not occur until the day of surgery, the surgeon and anesthesiologist together may elect for presumptive management based on clinical criteria or a last-minute delay of surgery.

💡 **Patient and Family Education:** The patient and his or her family as well as the surgeon should be informed of the potential implications of OSA on the patient's perioperative course.

    * URTI
    * Higher risk of respiratory complications e.g. bronchospasm, laryngospasm, hypoxia under GA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * ⚠️ Usually recommend elective surgery at least 2-4 weeks after resolution of symptoms [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * ⚠️ If pneumonia – postpone 6wks after resolution [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Pulmonary assessment should focus on identifying respiratory disease severity, exacerbation status, and optimization strategies. For asthma/COPD, evaluate medication requirements and recent exacerbations. For OSA, comprehensive evaluation should include clinical screening (STOPBANG), sleep study review if available, and multi-disciplinary planning for perioperative management. Elective surgery should be postponed for active respiratory infections.

#### 1.4.2.2. OSA PREOPERATIVE PREPARATION [ASA OSA GUIDELINES]

**Preoperative Initiation of CPAP:** Preoperative initiation of CPAP should be considered, particularly if OSA is severe. For patients who do not respond adequately to CPAP, NIPPV should be considered.

**Alternative Therapies:** The preoperative use of mandibular advancement devices or oral appliances and preoperative weight loss should be considered when feasible.

**Post-Surgical Patients:** A patient who has had corrective airway surgery (e.g., uvulopalatopharyngoplasty, surgical mandibular advancement) should be assumed to remain at risk of OSA complications unless a normal sleep study has been obtained and symptoms have not returned.

**Airway Management Considerations:** Patients with known or suspected OSA may have difficult airways and therefore should be managed according to the "Practice Guidelines for Management of the Difficult Airway: An Updated Report."

#### 1.4.2.3. OSA INTRAOPERATIVE MANAGEMENT [ASA OSA GUIDELINES]

**Medication Selection:** Because of their propensity for airway collapse and sleep deprivation, patients at increased perioperative risk from OSA are especially susceptible to the respiratory depressant and airway effects of sedatives, opioids, and inhaled anesthetics; therefore, the potential for postoperative respiratory compromise should be considered in selecting intraoperative medications.

**Anesthetic Technique Options:**
* **For superficial procedures:** Consider the use of local anesthesia or peripheral nerve blocks, with or without moderate sedation
* **If moderate sedation is used:** Ventilation should be continuously monitored by capnography or another automated method if feasible because of the increased risk of undetected airway obstruction in these patients
* **For patients previously treated with CPAP:** Consider administering CPAP or using an oral appliance during sedation
* **General anesthesia:** General anesthesia with a secure airway is preferable to deep sedation without a secure airway, particularly for procedures that may mechanically compromise the airway
* **Major conduction anesthesia:** Spinal/epidural should be considered for peripheral procedures

**Airway Management:**
* ⚠️ **Extubation:** Unless there is a medical or surgical contraindication, patients at increased perioperative risk from OSA should be extubated while awake
* **Neuromuscular Block:** Full reversal of neuromuscular block should be verified before extubation
* **Positioning:** When possible, extubation and recovery should be carried out in the lateral, semiupright, or other nonsupine positions

#### 1.4.2.4. OSA POSTOPERATIVE MANAGEMENT [ASA OSA GUIDELINES]

**Risk Factors for Complications:** Risk factors for postoperative respiratory depression may include the underlying severity of the sleep apnea, systemic administration of opioids, use of sedatives, site and invasiveness of surgical procedure, and the potential for apnea during rapid eye movement (REM) sleep on the third or fourth postoperative day (i.e., "REM rebound").

**Analgesia Management:**
* **Regional analgesic techniques** should be considered to reduce or eliminate the requirement for systemic opioids in patients at increased perioperative risk from OSA
* **Neuraxial analgesia:** If planned, weigh the benefits (improved analgesia, decreased need for systemic opioids) and risks (respiratory depression from rostral spread) of using an opioid or opioid–local anesthetic mixture rather than a local anesthetic alone
* **Patient-controlled opioids:** If used, continuous background infusions should be avoided or used with extreme caution
* **Multimodal analgesia:** Nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory agents and other modalities (e.g., ice, transcutaneous electrical nerve stimulation) should be considered if appropriate
* ⚠️ **Sedatives:** Clinicians are cautioned that the concurrent administration of sedative agents (e.g., benzodiazepines, barbiturates) increases the risk of respiratory depression and airway obstruction

**Oxygenation and Ventilatory Support:**
* **Supplemental oxygen** should be administered continuously to all patients who are at increased perioperative risk from OSA until they are able to maintain their baseline oxygen saturation while breathing room air
* **CPAP/NIPPV:** When feasible, CPAP or NIPPV (with or without supplemental oxygen) should be continuously administered to patients who were using these modalities preoperatively, unless contraindicated by the surgical procedure
* **Equipment Compliance:** Compliance with CPAP or NIPPV may be improved if patients bring their own equipment to the hospital

**Positioning and Monitoring:**
* **Positioning:** If possible, patients at increased perioperative risk from OSA should be placed in nonsupine positions throughout the recovery process
* **Continuous monitoring:** Hospitalized patients who are at increased risk of respiratory compromise from OSA should have continuous pulse oximetry monitoring after discharge from the recovery room
* **Monitoring settings:** Continuous monitoring may be provided in a critical care or stepdown unit, by telemetry on a hospital ward, or by a dedicated, appropriately trained professional observer in the patient's room
* **Duration:** Continuous monitoring should be maintained as long as patients remain at increased risk

**Emergency Interventions:** If frequent or severe airway obstruction or hypoxemia occurs during postoperative monitoring, initiation of nasal CPAP or NIPPV should be considered.

**Discharge Criteria:** Patients at increased perioperative risk from OSA should not be discharged from the recovery area to an unmonitored setting (i.e., home or unmonitored hospital bed) until they are no longer at risk of postoperative respiratory depression. To establish that patients are able to maintain adequate oxygen saturation levels while breathing room air, respiratory function may be determined by observing patients in an unstimulated environment, preferably while asleep.

💡 **Special Pediatric Considerations:** For children undergoing tonsillectomy for OSA, the Task Force cautions that repeated hypoxemia may alter μ-opioid receptors, making these children sensitive to opioids and therefore requiring a reduced opioid dose (i.e., approximately half the usual dose).

#### 1.4.2.5. OSA INPATIENT VS OUTPATIENT DETERMINATION [ASA OSA GUIDELINES]

Before patients at increased perioperative risk from OSA are scheduled to undergo surgery, a determination should be made regarding whether a surgical procedure is most appropriately performed on an inpatient or outpatient basis.

**Factors to Consider:**
1. Sleep apnea status
2. Anatomical and physiologic abnormalities
3. Status of coexisting diseases
4. Nature of surgery
5. Type of anesthesia
6. Need for postoperative opioids
7. Patient age
8. Adequacy of postdischarge observation
9. Capabilities of the outpatient facility

**Facility Requirements:** The availability of emergency difficult airway equipment, respiratory care equipment, radiology facilities, clinical laboratory facilities, and a transfer agreement with an inpatient facility should be considered in making this determination.

### 1.4.3. ENDOCRINE AND METABOLIC ISSUES
<!-- EPA-1:K1:endocrine EPA-1:EQ4 -->

* Summarized tips for individual conditions [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * DM
    * Impt to avoid hyperglycaemia and even more so, hypoglycaemia; Stress hyperglycaemia will worse DM control [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Poorly controlled DM likely to have autonomic dysfunction and swinging BP intra-op. Higher risk of CVA/AMI -> need referral to endocrine for optimization (early preop if HbA1c >9%) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Evaluate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * T1/T2 DM [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Treatment - insulin/OHGA treatment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Control A1C, HC trend [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Evaluate End organ - CVS, renal, Neuro, Airway (stiff joint syndrome) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Preop ix [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * FBC, RP, A1C [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Urine analysis, proteins, ketones [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * 12 lead ECG [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * +/- CXR [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Special ix for end organ [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * If [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * ⚠️ Pt in DKA or HHS or hypoglycaemia - postpone surgery if non urgent; but if urgent may need 2-3hr to work on glucose control [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * NIDDM/ T2DM on diet control - HC 6hrly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * NIDDM on OHGA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * stop long acting agent prior to fasting, [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * consider hydration w low dose dextrose containing infusion, [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * stop OHGA on operation day [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * HC 4-6hrly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * IDDM/ T1DM [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Admit 1 day before, usually should be done first case of the day [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Req. baseline insulin therapy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Maintain hydration w dextrose-containing solution once NBM [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Regular HC at least 4hrly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Inv. endocrinologist [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Thyroid disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Assess [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * hormonal status (clinical and lab) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Complications from mass effect - SOB, dysphagia, hoarseness of voice [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Ix: FBC, RP, TFT (within 6m if on stable meds), Ca, CXR (retrosternal goitre - look for previous US), 12 lead ECG [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Adrenal insufficiency [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Hypocortisolism or long term steroid/TCM use [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Steroids and their indication (>10mg Prednisolone OM should be stressed dose) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Patients with possible adrenal insufficiency may need stress dose IV hydrocortisone100mg on induction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Endocrine assessment should focus on disease control status and end-organ complications. For diabetes, evaluate type, control (HbA1c), and treatment regimen. For thyroid disease, assess hormonal status and potential airway implications. For patients on steroids, evaluate for potential adrenal insufficiency and plan for stress-dose steroids if needed.

### 1.4.4. COAGULATION ISSUES
<!-- EPA-1:K1:haematology EPA-1:K3:blood_management -->

* 3 Impt coagulation issues to address during preop evaluation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * How to manage pts on warfarin or other long acting anticoagulants on a long term basis? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * How to manage pts w CAD on plavix or related agents? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Can I safely provide neuraxial anaesthesia to pts on long term anticoagulation therapy or those who will receive them perioperatively? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Controversial [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* most patients undergoing anything more involved than minor surgery will require discontinuation of anticoagulation in advance of surgery to avoid excessive blood loss [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * How far in advance to stop? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Is bridging required? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* In patients deemed at high risk for thrombosis (eg, those with certain mechanical heart valve implants or with atrial fibrillation and a prior thromboembolic stroke), chronic anticoagulants should be replaced by intramuscular low molecular weight heparins (eg, enoxaparin) or by intravenous unfractionated heparin. [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * In patients with a high risk of thrombosis who receive bridging therapy, the risk of death from excessive bleeding is an order of magnitude lower than the risk of death or disability from stroke if the bridging therapy is omitted. [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Patients at lower risk for thrombosis may have their anticoagulant drug discontinued preoperatively and then reinitiated after successful surgery. [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

    * Children 5.0-11.99 yr: 11.5 g/dl
    * Children 12.0-14.99 yr: 12.0 g/dl
    * Nonpregnant women ≥15.0 yr: 12.0 g/dl
    * Pregnant women: 11.0 g/dl
    * Men ≥15.0 yr: 13.0 g/dl

**Anticoagulation Management:**
* **Discontinuation:** In consultation with appropriate specialist, discontinue anticoagulation therapy (e.g., warfarin, anti-Xa drugs, antithrombin agents) for elective surgery
* **Bridging therapy:** Transition to shorter acting drug (e.g., heparin, low-molecular-weight heparin) may be appropriate in selected patients
* **Antiplatelet agents:** If clinically possible, discontinue nonaspirin antiplatelet agents (e.g., thienopyridines such as clopidogrel, ticagrelor, or prasugrel) for sufficient time in advance of surgery, except for patients with history of percutaneous coronary interventions
* **Aspirin:** May be continued on case-by-case basis
* **Risk-benefit analysis:** The risk of thrombosis versus the risk of increased bleeding should be considered when altering anticoagulation status

**Blood Product Availability:**
* Assure that blood and blood components are available for patients when significant blood loss or transfusion is expected
* **Autologous donation:** When autologous blood is preferred, patient may be offered opportunity to donate blood before admission only if there is adequate time for erythropoietic reconstitution

**Perioperative Blood Conservation Strategies:**

**Restrictive Transfusion Strategy:**
* A restrictive red blood cell transfusion strategy may be safely used to reduce transfusion administration
* Determination of whether hemoglobin concentrations between 6 and 10 g/dl justify red blood cell transfusion should be based on:
    * Potential or actual ongoing bleeding (rate and magnitude)
    * Intravascular volume status
    * Signs of organ ischemia
    * Adequacy of cardiopulmonary reserve
* Red blood cells should be administered unit-by-unit when possible, with interval reevaluation

**Antifibrinolytic Therapy:**
* **Cardiopulmonary bypass:** Use antifibrinolytic therapy for prophylaxis of allogeneic blood transfusion
* **Orthopedic procedures:** Consider using antifibrinolytic therapy for prophylaxis in certain procedures such as knee replacement surgery
* **High-risk procedures:** Consider antifibrinolytic therapy for prophylaxis in liver surgery and other clinical circumstances at high risk for excessive bleeding

**Acute Normovolemic Hemodilution (ANH):**
* Consider ANH to reduce allogeneic blood transfusion in patients at high risk for excessive bleeding (e.g., major cardiac, orthopedic, thoracic, or liver surgery), if possible

**Intraoperative Blood Conservation:**
* **Cell salvage:** Reinfuse recovered red blood cells as blood-sparing intervention in intraoperative period when appropriate
* **Leukocyte reduction:** Leukocyte-reduced blood may be used for transfusion to reduce complications associated with allogeneic blood transfusion

**Coagulation Monitoring and Management:**
* **Viscoelastic testing:** If coagulopathy is suspected, obtain standard coagulation tests (e.g., INR, aPTT, fibrinogen concentration) or viscoelastic assays (e.g., TEG and ROTEM), if available, as well as platelet count
* **Pharmacologic interventions for bleeding:**
    * **Desmopressin:** May be used in patients with excessive bleeding and platelet dysfunction
    * **Topical hemostatics:** Consider fibrin glue or thrombin gel
    * **Prothrombin complex concentrates:** May be used in patients with excessive bleeding and increased INR
    * **Fibrinogen concentrate:** May be used for hypofibrinogenemia

**Transfusion Reaction Monitoring:**
* During and after transfusion, periodically check for signs including hyperthermia, hemoglobinuria, microvascular bleeding, hypoxemia, respiratory distress, increased peak airway pressure, urticaria, hypotension, and signs of hypocalcemia
* If signs of transfusion reaction apparent, immediately stop transfusion and initiate supportive care

💡 **Enhanced Recovery Benefits:** Implementation of comprehensive blood management strategies can reduce transfusion requirements, minimize complications, and improve patient outcomes through coordinated preoperative optimization, intraoperative conservation techniques, and evidence-based transfusion practices.

![Untitled](PREOP_Untitled_5.png_c92d7d.png)

**Bottom Line:** Management of perioperative anticoagulation requires careful risk-benefit analysis between thromboembolism and bleeding. For patients at high risk of thrombosis (e.g., mechanical valves), bridging therapy is often recommended. For neuraxial anesthesia, specific guidelines regarding timing of anticoagulant cessation should be followed.

### 1.4.5. GASTROINTESTINAL ISSUES
<!-- EPA-1:K1:liver -->

* Risk of aspiration is increased in certain groups of patients: pregnant women in the second and third trimesters, those whose stomachs have not emptied after a recent meal, and those with serious gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD). [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Summarized tips for individual condition [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * GERD [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Our approach is to treat patients who have only occasional symptoms like any other patient without GERD, and to treat patients with consistent symptoms (multiple times per week) with medications (eg, nonparticulate antacids such as sodium citrate) and techniques (eg, tracheal intubation rather than laryngeal mask airway) as if they were at increased risk for aspiration. [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * ⚠️ Patients with severe GERD may need RSI as high aspiration risk -advise 1' team/prescribe PPI 10pm night of op and 6am morning of [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Chronic liver disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Calculate CPS, MELD score [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Previous complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Varices - contraindication for NGT, TEE [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Encephalopathy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Thromboyctopenia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Ascites [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * If Child C: counsel for postop hepatic encephalopathy KIV ICU [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** GI issues assessment should focus on aspiration risk and hepatic function. For significant GERD, consider medications and RSI to reduce aspiration risk. For liver disease, calculate severity scores (CPS, MELD) and evaluate for complications that may affect anesthetic management.

### 1.4.6. RENAL DISEASES
<!-- EPA-1:K1:renal -->

* Multisystemic, screen for anaemia and IHD [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Preop [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Determine cause, previous surgery inc. transplantation, medications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * RU per day [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * type and freq. of dialysis, determine when was last successful dialysis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
