    * Refer to renal team to assist in fluid management and dialysis - use NaCl 0.9% for maintenance and avoid fluids containing K [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * ⚠️ Ideally HD should be done 24hr before CKD [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Min. allow 4-6hr to elapse after HD to allow fluid compartment equilibration and metabolism of residual heparin; heparin free dialysis would be better [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Preop evaluation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Check fluid status [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Check dialysis line, peritoneal catheter or AVF - no blood taking, BP cuff, IV canulae [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Indications for urgent dialysis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Summarized tips for individual condition [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * CKD
    * HD/PD: frequency, last HD and duration, any intra-dialytic events, dry weight [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Patients on PD will need to drain PD fluid before op (aspiration risk with increased intraabdominal pressure) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * ⚠️ Surgery should ideally be within 24h after hemodialysis, otherwise minimally 4h after last HD (to allow fluid and electrolytes to equilibrate) - do post dialysis blds if random blds are deranged (ie dialysis dependent e-homeostasis) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Confirm which limb cannot be used for BP/ vascular access [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Highlight if U > 30, HCO3 < 18, K > 5 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Renal disease evaluation requires assessment of disease type, dialysis status, fluid status, and electrolyte abnormalities. Ideally, schedule surgery within 24 hours after dialysis, but at least 4-6 hours after to allow equilibration. Confirm which extremity has vascular access and avoid its use for BP measurement or IV access.

### 1.4.7. NEUROLOGICAL
<!-- EPA-1:K1:neurology -->

* To document baseline GCS, neurology [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Especially if going for regional anaesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* CVA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Time since CVA (best to be >9mths but minimally 3mths) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Ischemic/ haemorrhagic? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Functional recovery? Any residual neuro deficit? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Parkinson's [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Severity, level of function [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Presence of autonomic dysfunction, concomitant cognitive impairment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Neck mobility may be affected [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Timing of Madopar [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * 💡 Family to bring madopar for periop dosing via NGT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Epilepsy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Control [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * AED regimen and compliance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Dementia/ Cognitive impairment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Cooperability for RA/Spinal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * If dementia/ cognitive impairment, may need to speak to family for anaesthesia counselling, may not be suitable for MAC if not cooperative/ keeps moving [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Any use of antiAcetylcholine - affects NMB and reversal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Neurological assessment should document baseline status, especially before regional anesthesia. For stroke patients, consider timing since event (ideally >3 months). For Parkinson's patients, maintain medication regimen perioperatively. For patients with cognitive impairment, assess ability to cooperate with regional anesthesia or MAC.

### 1.4.8. OTHERS
<!-- EPA-1:K1:obesity EPA-1:K1:others EPA-1:EQ6 -->

* MSK and other conditions affecting airway [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Cervical spondylosis, ankylosing spondylitis, previous RT for NPC [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Hematological [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Anaemia - any role for nontransfusion based preop optimization? - IV iron, Iron tablets, EPO [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Arrange for transfusion standby? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Obesity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Weight/Height² = BMI [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Obesity = >28kg/m²; Morbid = >35kg/m² [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Morbid obese has 2x higher risk of dying from major op [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Association w multiple comorbids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Preop assessment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * BMI [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * GERD [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Snoring, OSA - lie supine and see if they can saturate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * BP control, signs of R/L HF [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Inform anaesthetist if there is logistics arrangements, booking postop HD or ICU bed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Social [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Alcohol, Smoking [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Heavy smokers have more secretions, higher risk of bronchospasm [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Advise to stop smoking >2d preop [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Counsel chronic alcoholics for higher risk of intraop awareness [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Women's menses, pregnancy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * If pregnant for non obs abdo surgery, counsel for early labour and miscarriage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Additional assessment areas include MSK conditions affecting airway management, hematological issues requiring optimization, obesity-related complications, and social factors like smoking and alcohol use. For pregnant patients undergoing non-obstetric surgery, counsel about potential risks.

## 1.5. PREOP LAB TESTING

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to laboratory testing]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Cost-effective and evidence-based practice]]

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient Care* In 2022, the Indian Society of Anaesthesiologists (ISA) published guidelines for preoperative investigations [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It vaires depending upon the type and urgency of surgery (elective, semi‑elective, emergency), the patient's current physiological status, associated comorbidities, and the medications. It also uniquely mentions the acceptable validity time for previously performed ix. Ix like normal FBC RP LFT and coag profile - are valid for 2 months. ECG and CXR is valid for 12 months provided clinical condition of patient has not changed.
* Coagulation studies are not routine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In patients with bleeding disorders, renal and liver dysfunction, and the type and invasiveness of the procedure, coagulation studies should be considered.
* Pulmonary function tests (PFTs) are not needed in the majority of patients undergoing extra thoracic surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In most cases, PFTs confirm the clinical impression without adding to the estimation of risk. PFTs may be useful in patients undergoing thoracic surgery, patients with known or suspected respiratory disease.
* According to the European Society of Anaesthesiologists (ESA), the assessment of cardiac troponins should be done prior to surgery and then 48 to 72 hours after surgery in high-risk patients like cardiac patients, critically ill cases, those posted for highly complex surgery, and morbidly obese [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* NUH guidelines [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * ASA 1 patients <50 years old: no investigations required [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * All patients >50 years old: FBC, RP with glucose, ECG [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Patients with medical problems: FBC, RP, ECG ± ask for repeat bloods if electrolytes are deranged [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * GXM ± standby blood products: if anticipated or potential for major blood loss/ Hb or plt low and may need transfusion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Routine CXR and PT/PTT not required [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Valid for 6/12 if no change in condition [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Specific to patient's medical condition: e.g. TFT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

#### 1.5.1.1. ASA-BASED TESTING RECOMMENDATIONS [EVIDENCE-BASED APPROACH]

**ASA Class-Specific Testing Guidelines:**

**ASA I (Healthy Patients):**
* **Age <40:** Generally no routine testing required for minor/intermediate surgery
* **Age 40-50:** Consider ECG; other tests based on surgical factors
* **Age >50:** ECG recommended; consider basic metabolic panel for major surgery
* **Pregnancy:** As per obstetric guidelines

**ASA II (Mild Systemic Disease):**
* **Baseline Testing:** FBC, comprehensive metabolic panel, ECG
* **Disease-Specific:** Additional tests based on specific comorbidities
    * Diabetes: HbA1c, glucose
    * Hypertension: ECG, basic metabolic panel
    * Mild lung disease: Consider chest X-ray if symptomatic
* **Validity:** Results valid for 3-6 months if condition stable

**ASA III (Severe Systemic Disease):**
* **Comprehensive Testing:** FBC, comprehensive metabolic panel, ECG mandatory
* **Additional Studies:** Based on specific conditions
    * Cardiac: Echo, stress testing as indicated
    * Renal: Creatinine, BUN, electrolytes
    * Hepatic: LFTs, coagulation studies
    * Pulmonary: Chest X-ray, consider PFTs
* **Cardiac Biomarkers:** Consider troponin in high-risk cardiac patients
* **Validity:** Results valid for 1-3 months, shorter for unstable conditions

**ASA IV (Constant Threat to Life):**
* **Extensive Testing:** Comprehensive laboratory and diagnostic workup
* **Recent Results:** Tests should be current within days to weeks
* **Specialist Consultation:** Often requires subspecialty input for optimization
* **Continuous Monitoring:** May require ICU-level monitoring capabilities

**ASA V (Moribund Patient):**
* **Emergency Testing:** Focused on immediate life-threatening issues
* **Point-of-Care:** Utilize rapid testing when possible
* **Minimal Delays:** Avoid unnecessary testing that delays urgent surgery

**Special Considerations:**
* **Emergency Surgery:** Limit testing to essential studies that will change management
* **Outpatient vs. Inpatient:** Consider discharge planning and monitoring capabilities
* **Resource Availability:** Ensure necessary monitoring and support available postoperatively

💡 **Personalized Approach:** Testing should be individualized based on ASA class, type of surgery, and specific patient factors rather than following rigid protocols.

![Untitled](PREOP_Untitled_6.png_60bb3a.png)

**Bottom Line:** Preoperative laboratory testing should be selective rather than routine, based on patient factors, type of surgery, and potential impact on management. For healthy patients under 50, tests may not be needed. Valid results can typically be used for several months if the patient's condition is stable.

## 1.6. AIRWAY ASSESSMENT
<!-- EPA-1:S1:clinical_assessment EPA-1:EQ6:obesity_airway -->

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|ⓘ|Part A: Anatomy of the airway]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical assessment of the airway]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Documentation and communication of difficult airway]]

**Exam Weighting:** High (Critical for anesthesia safety)

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient Care* The anesthesiologist should examine the patient's airway before every anesthetic is administered [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Any loose or chipped teeth, caps, bridges, or dentures should be noted [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Poor fit of the anesthesia mask should be expected in edentulous patients and those with significant facial abnormalities [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Micrognathia(a short distance between the chin and the hyoid bone), prominent upper incisors, a large tongue, limited range of motion of the temporomandibular joint or cervical spine, or a short or thick neck suggest that difficulty may be encountered in direct laryngoscopy for tracheal intubation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

![Untitled](PREOP_Untitled_7.png_656400.png)

* Steps [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    1. Mallampati score: ask patient to sit up and open mouth as wide as possible and stick out tongue, don't say AH [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    2. Mobility of temporomandibular joint: any clicks when opening mouth? Previous jaw dislocation? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    3. Thyromental distance: at least 6cm/4 finger-breadths (FB) with neck extended / 3FB chin to hyoid in neutral [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    4. Range of motion of cervical spine (also note any neurological symptoms, e.g. finger paraesthesia/ numbness that changes on at extremes of neck movement that might suggest cervical myelopathy) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Risk of difficult airway if neck extension <90deg [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    5. Mouth opening: at least 4cm/3 FB [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    6. Dentition: loose/missing teeth (can put on glove and assess teeth mobility via direct palpation), prosthesis e.g. dentures, crowns, caps, bridges (including condition of prosthesis) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Incisor prosthesis at higher risk esp with LMA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    7. Others e.g. facial hair [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Neck circumference: A neck circumference of greater than 17 inches is associated with difficulties in visualization of the glottic opening [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **What are the sensitivities, specificities and positive predictive values of the commonly used tests described above?** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * **Mallampati score:** This is arguably the most recognized and most performed test for preoperative airway assessment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Depending on which validation study you read, sensitivity is between 4080%, specificity is between 50 - 85%, and the positive predictive value (PPV) is between 520% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
    * Class I: The entire palatal arch, including the bilateral faucial pillars, is visible down to the bases of the pillars [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
    * Class II: The upper part of the faucial pillars and most of the uvula are visible [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
    * Class III: Only the soft and hard palates are visible [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
    * Class IV: Only the hard palate is visible [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

    ![Untitled](PREOP_Untitled_8.png_c412b5.png)

    * **Inter-incisor gap (IIG):** Mouth opening has a sensitivity of around 2545%, a specificity of around 95%, and a PPV of up to 25% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
    * **Thyromental distance (TMD):** Depending on the study, sensitivity is quoted to be between around 5% and 90%, specificity ranges from roughly 25% to 80%, and PPVs from about 15% to 40% have been found [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
    * **TMJ mobility:** Sensitivity is up to 30% at best, specificity is 85% to 90%. Like for the other tests, the PPV is low being around 20% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* It is immediately obvious that many tests have relatively **low sensitivity** although they are largely fairly specific. Even more concerning though is the fact that their **positive predictive value is generally very low** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. This is because truly difficult intubations are comparatively rare. The PPV of a test always suffers when the condition it aims to predict is rare [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

![](https://aam.ucsf.edu/sites/g/files/tkssra4826/f/wysiwyg/56.jpg)

* The positive predictive value for airway assessment tests; 'Tests predicting difficult intubation' encompass true positives plus false positives [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Combinations of tests** perform somewhat better [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, the Mallampati score together with the thyromental distance achieved around 80% sensitivity, 98% specificity and a PPV of almost 65% in one study (the PPV was so much higher because of the high specificity of this test combo) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* One example of a scoring systems for preoperative airway assessment is the **Wilson risk sum score.** Others include the **Arne risk index** or the **El-Ganzouri risk index.** Neither are widely used in clinical practice but apply more in research settings to describe patient or study populations [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]:
* Even with this combination of tests, while the sensitivity and specificity of the Wilson risk score are up to 55% and 90% respectively, the PPV is still only about 10% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

![Untitled](PREOP_Untitled_9.png_5375c5.png)

* NUH ED Physiological airway assessment: CRAAASH [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Consumption of oxygen (hypoxia, affects preoxygenation) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Right ventricular failure (impact of PPV on preload) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Agitation (affects preoxygenation) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Asthma (affects ketamine use and ventilator settings) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Acidosis (affects ventilator settings) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Shock (affects preinduction haemodynamics) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * HyperKalaemia (affects use of succinylcholine) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Comprehensive airway assessment should include Mallampati score, thyromental distance, neck mobility, mouth opening, dentition evaluation, and neck circumference. Individual tests have limited predictive value, but combinations improve accuracy. Remember that even with thorough assessment, unexpected difficult airways may still occur.

## 1.7. PREANAESTHESIA INSTRUCTIONS

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical guidelines for preoperative fasting and medication management]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Patient communication and perioperative safety]]

**Exam Weighting:** High (Essential for patient safety)

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient Care### 1.7.2. FASTING

* Fasting guidelines [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * The truth is that there are no good data to support restricting fluid intake (of any kind or any amount) more than 2 h before induction of general anesthesia in healthy patients undergoing elective procedures; moreover, there is strong evidence that nondiabetic patients who drink fluids containing carbohydrates and protein up to 2 h before induction of anesthesia suffer less perioperative nausea and dehydration than those who are fasted longer [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
    * IV maintenance fluids if there is a prolonged period of fasting [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Adults and older children [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * 8 hours for heavy meal (e.g. oily food) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * 6 hours for light meal (e.g. white bread with jam) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * 2 hours for clear feeds (<10ml/kg)- water, coffee or tea without milk, juice without pulp [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * In practice: usually NBM 12mn for morning surgeries before 12pm and NBM 6am after light breakfast for surgeries after 12pm. Can continue regular oral meds with sips of water [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Younger children & babies [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Formula milk 6 hrs; Breastmilk 4 hrs; Clear feeds 2 hrs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

#### 1.7.1.1. UPDATED ASA FASTING GUIDELINES 2023 [2023 ASA GUIDELINES]

**Enhanced Recommendations for Clear Liquids:**

**Carbohydrate-Containing Clear Liquids - Strong Recommendation:**
We recommend healthy adults drink carbohydrate-containing clear liquids until 2 h before elective procedures requiring general anesthesia, regional anesthesia, or procedural sedation. The carbohydrates may be simple or complex.
* **Volume:** Up to 400 ml of clear liquids is considered an appropriate volume
* **Benefits:** Patients drinking carbohydrate-containing clear liquids experienced less hunger and thirst compared to fasting and less hunger compared to drinking noncaloric clear liquids
* **Types:** Simple carbohydrates (clear fruit juices, glucose solutions) or complex carbohydrates (maltodextrin-based solutions) are equally effective
* **Safety:** No incidence of aspiration or regurgitation was reported in studies

**Protein-Containing Clear Liquids:**
There is insufficient evidence to recommend protein-containing clear liquids preferentially over other clear liquids 2 h before elective procedures. All studied protein-containing clear liquids also contained carbohydrates.

**Chewing Gum:**
* We suggest not delaying elective procedures in healthy adults who are chewing gum
* ⚠️ **Important:** Chewing gum should be removed before any sedative/anesthetic is administered
* Chewing gum had a minimally increased residual gastric volume but no difference in gastric pH
* **Clinical Practice:** Confirm gum removal before anesthetic administration, but do not delay surgery for gum chewing during fasting period

**Enhanced Pediatric Fasting Considerations:**
* **Current Recommendation:** Clear liquids may be continued until 2 h before procedures in children
* **Research Gap:** There is insufficient evidence to recommend reducing clear liquid fasting to 1 h in pediatric patients, though several pediatric anesthesia practices use this approach
* **Best Practice:** To avoid prolonged fasting in children, efforts should be made to allow clear liquids in children at low risk of aspiration as close to 2 h before procedures as possible
* **Clinical Judgment:** In children with shorter clear liquid fasting duration, exercise clinical judgment

**Key Practice Points:**
* **Prolonged Fasting:** Fasting duration is often substantially longer than recommended and has well-described adverse consequences
* **Patient Population:** These guidelines apply to healthy patients undergoing elective procedures
* **Contraindications:** Guidelines may not apply to patients with coexisting diseases affecting gastric emptying (pregnancy, obesity, diabetes, hiatal hernia, gastroesophageal reflux disease, ileus, emergency procedures)
* **Implementation:** Robust local education and policy updates are essential for all patient care teams

**Updated Summary of Fasting Recommendations:**
* **Carbohydrate-containing clear liquids:** 2 h (STRONGLY RECOMMENDED)
* **Clear liquids:** 2 h
* **Breast milk:** 4 h  
* **Infant formula/Nonhuman milk:** 6 h
* **Light meal:** 6 h
* **Heavy/Fatty Foods:** 8 h

💡 **Clinical Benefits:** Encouraging carbohydrate-containing clear liquids until 2 hours preoperatively can minimize potential harms of prolonged fasting, including hunger, thirst, dehydration, and hypoglycemia, while maintaining safety.

### 1.7.2. MEDICATIONS

* CVS [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Continue BB, CCB [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Omit ACEI/ARB +/- diuretics - avoids risk of intraop hypotension [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    ![[Pasted image 20250720111823.png]]
* Respiratory [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * To administer rescue inhaler on call to OT or bring w pt to OT (connected to spacer) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Can be administered in cases of intraop bronchospasm [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Neurology [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Continue AED and Parkinson meds (take strictly as per regime) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Endocrine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * For T2DM, omit all OHGA and insulin on op day, kiv start sliding scale (Refer to DM NBM guidelines on
    intranet). Check H/C on arrival (SDA) / before sending down to OT (inpt) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * ⚠️ SGLT2-inhibitors (e.g. dapaglifozin, empaglifozin) should be stopped 2 days before and on the operation day (risk of euglycemic DKA periop) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, if the operation date is on 3 July, the SGLT2-i should be stopped on 1 July, 2 July and 3 July [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
    * Continue all thyroid med [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Antiplatelet/Anticoag [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Usually decision left to surgeons, but also influence our decision for RA (Refer to guideline on intranet) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Rule of thumb: RA (incl spinal) ok if only on aspirin. If anything else, will need adequate cessation duration or RA contraindicated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * **For IHD patients with coronary stents:** See comprehensive antiplatelet management in section 3.1.7.1 (DAPT timing, aspirin continuation, P2Y12 cessation timing, bridging strategies)
* Steroids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Do NOT need stress dosing if: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * any dose <3wks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * 5mg Prednisone/day or equivalent [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * 10mg Prednisone every other day or equivalent [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Presumed to have suppressed HPA axis: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * > 20mg Prednisone/day or equivalent for >3wks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Any patient with clinical Cushing's syndrome [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * HPA axis testing indicated for intermediate risk (5-20mg >3wks) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Continue usual dose for minor procedures or procedures under local anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Sources very for optimal dose of "stress dose steroid" but often 50-100mg Hydrocortisone can be given with induction if there is concern [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Continue stress dosing perioperatively should be done with Endocrinology assistance (often 25-50mg q8hrs x24hrs) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * In general, patients are probably given stress dose steroids more often than is needed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
    * (4mg Dexa = 27mg Prednisone = 107mg Hydrocortisone) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Others [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Continue PPI [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
    * Continue immunosuppression. Supplement peri-op with IV hydrocortisone if taking prednisolone>10mg/day in last 3 months (risk of adrenal insufficiency with LT steroids) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

💡 **For IHD Patients:** See section 3.1.8 for comprehensive preoperative optimization strategies including:
* Beta-blocker management (continue existing, do NOT routinely initiate - POISE trial)
* Statin continuation (VISION trial evidence)
* Smoking cessation timing (>4 weeks ideal, any duration better than none)
* Anemia management (target Hb >8 g/dL, consider IV iron if ≥2 weeks available)
* Evidence against routine coronary revascularization (CARP trial)

### 1.7.3. COMPREHENSIVE ACE-I/ARB PERIOPERATIVE MANAGEMENT [EVIDENCE-BASED GUIDELINES]

**Background and Epidemiology:**
* **Prevalence:** More than one-third of surgical patients have hypertension and are taking ACE-I or ARB therapy
* **Global burden:** Hypertension affects 50% of men >45 years, making ACE-I/ARB management a critical perioperative issue
* **Clinical uncertainty:** Despite decades of use, optimal perioperative management remains controversial

**Mechanism of Perioperative Risk:**

**Renin-Angiotensin System (RAS) Physiology:**
* **Normal response:** Renin released from juxtaglomerular cells → Angiotensin I → ACE → Angiotensin II → vasoconstriction and aldosterone release
* **ACE-I mechanism:** Block conversion of Angiotensin I to Angiotensin II, limiting vasoconstriction
* **ARB mechanism:** Competitively block AT1 receptors, preventing Angiotensin II effects
* **Perioperative implications:** Impaired compensatory vasoconstriction during hypotensive episodes

**Pathophysiology of Perioperative Hypotension:**
* **Primary mechanism:** Inability to mount appropriate RAS-mediated vasoconstrictor response
* **Alternative pathways:** ACE-I allows conversion to vasodilatory angiotensin 1-7 via alternative enzymes
* **Anesthetic interactions:** Enhanced hypotensive effects with volatile agents, neuraxial anesthesia
* **Timing factors:** Effects persist beyond drug half-life due to tissue-based RAS effects

**Current Evidence Base:**

**Major Studies:**
* **VISION Study (n=14,687):**
  * **Design:** Large international prospective cohort study
  * **Primary finding:** 20% relative risk reduction in intraoperative hypotension with 24-hour withholding
  * **Secondary findings:** 18% relative risk reduction in death/stroke/myocardial injury
  * **Limitations:** Crude hypotension definition (SBP <90 mmHg), observational design
* **Meta-Analysis (Hollmann et al., 2018):**
  * **Finding:** 30% relative risk increase in intraoperative hypotension with continuation
  * **No MACE correlation:** No association between hypotension and major adverse cardiac events
  * **Study quality:** High heterogeneity, small sample sizes in component studies
* **PREOP-ACEI Trial:**
  * **Largest RCT:** Most robust randomized evidence available
  * **Hypotension reduction:** Less intraoperative hypotension with withdrawal (55% vs 69%)
  * **Rebound effect:** Increased postoperative hypertension with withdrawal
  * **Safety:** No increased acute renal failure or MACE with either strategy

**Conflicting Guidelines:**

**2014 ACC/AHA Guidelines:**
* **Recommendation:** Continue ACE-I/ARB perioperatively (Class IIa, Level B)
* **Rationale:** Based on Rosenman meta-analysis showing no adverse outcomes
* **Resumption:** Restart postoperatively when clinically feasible (Level C)

**2017 CCS Guidelines:**
* **Recommendation:** Withhold 24 hours before surgery (Strong recommendation, low-quality evidence)
* **Rationale:** Based primarily on VISION study results
* **Target:** All patients on chronic ACE-I/ARB therapy undergoing noncardiac surgery

**2014 ESC/ESA Guidelines:**
* **Heart failure:** Continue in patients with systolic dysfunction (Class IIa, Level C)
* **Hypertension:** Consider transient discontinuation (Class IIa, Level C)
* **New initiation:** Consider starting within week of surgery for appropriate candidates

**Clinical Decision-Making Framework:**

**Patient Risk Assessment:**
* **High-risk for hypotension:**
  * Elderly patients (>75 years)
  * Diabetic autonomic neuropathy
  * Heart failure with preserved ejection fraction
  * Concurrent multiple antihypertensives
  * History of intraoperative hypotension
* **High-risk for MACE:**
  * Heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (EF <40%)
  * Recent myocardial infarction (<6 months)
  * High RCRI score (≥3 risk factors)
  * Severe valvular disease

**Surgical Risk Assessment:**
* **High-risk procedures:**
  * Major vascular surgery (open AAA, carotid endarterectomy)
  * Cardiac surgery
  * Major abdominal surgery (hepatectomy, Whipple)
  * Thoracic surgery
  * Emergency surgery
* **Positioning considerations:**
  * Beach chair position (shoulder surgery)
  * Prone position (spine surgery)
  * Anti-Trendelenburg position
* **Expected blood loss:** >500 mL or >15% estimated blood volume

**Anesthetic Risk Assessment:**
* **Neuraxial anesthesia:** Increased risk due to sympathetic blockade
* **Combined general-neuraxial:** Highest risk for severe hypotension
* **Volatile agents:** Enhanced hypotensive effects vs. TIVA
* **Long procedures:** >3 hours increase cumulative risk

**Pharmacokinetic Considerations:**

**ACE Inhibitor Half-Lives:**
* **Captopril:** 1.7 hours (short-acting, hold >10 hours)
* **Enalapril:** 11 hours (hold 24 hours)
* **Lisinopril:** 12 hours (hold 24 hours)
* **Benazepril:** 10-11 hours (hold 24 hours)
* **Fosinopril:** 12 hours (hold 24 hours)

**ARB Half-Lives:**
* **Losartan:** 2 hours (active metabolite 7 hours, hold 24 hours)
* **Valsartan:** 9 hours (hold 24 hours)
* **Irbesartan:** 11-15 hours (hold 24-48 hours)
* **Candesartan:** 3.5-4 hours (hold 12-24 hours)
* **Telmisartan:** 24 hours (hold 48 hours)

**Clinical Decision Algorithm:**

**Step 1: Assess Indication Strength**
* **Strong indications for continuation:**
  * Heart failure with EF <40%
  * Recent MI with incomplete revascularization
  * Diabetic nephropathy with proteinuria
  * High-grade CKD with cardiovascular protection benefit
* **Moderate indications:**
  * Well-controlled hypertension monotherapy
  * Stable CAD without recent events
  * Low-grade CKD

**Step 2: Assess Hypotension Risk**
* **High risk:** Major surgery + neuraxial anesthesia + elderly patient
* **Moderate risk:** One or two risk factors present
* **Low risk:** Minor surgery + general anesthesia + young patient

**Step 3: Apply Decision Matrix**
* **Strong indication + Low risk:** Continue therapy
* **Strong indication + High risk:** Individualized decision with enhanced monitoring
* **Moderate indication + High risk:** Consider withholding
* **Weak indication + Any risk:** Withhold therapy

**Practical Implementation Strategies:**

**If Continuing Therapy:**
* **Preoperative optimization:**
  * Ensure adequate hydration status
  * Hold other hypotensive agents (diuretics, additional vasodilators)
  * Consider dose reduction (50% of usual dose)
* **Intraoperative management:**
  * Avoid large propofol boluses (use slow induction)
  * Optimize intravascular volume before induction
  * Prepare vasopressors (phenylephrine, norepinephrine ready)
  * Consider arterial line for continuous BP monitoring
  * Have vasopressin available for refractory hypotension
* **Enhanced monitoring:**
  * Continuous invasive blood pressure monitoring
  * Consider goal-directed fluid therapy
  * Frequent hemodynamic assessments

**If Withholding Therapy:**
* **Timing considerations:**
  * Stop 24 hours before surgery (most agents)
  * Stop 48 hours for very long-acting agents (telmisartan)
  * Document exact timing of last dose
* **Bridge therapy options:**
  * Short-acting agents (nicardipine, clevidipine) for severe hypertension
  * Avoid rebound hypertension with abrupt cessation
  * Monitor BP closely in preoperative period
* **Restart planning:**
  * Resume within 24 hours postoperatively when stable
  * Monitor for rebound hypertension
  * Ensure adequate oral intake before PO resumption

**Special Populations:**

**Heart Failure Patients:**
* **Strong evidence:** Continue ACE-I/ARB in systolic heart failure (Class I recommendation)
* **Risk mitigation:** Enhanced hemodynamic monitoring, aggressive fluid optimization
* **Alternative agents:** Consider short-acting alternatives if withholding necessary

**Renal Disease Patients:**
* **Conflicting evidence:** Some studies show increased ARF, others show protection
* **Mechanism considerations:** Balance renal protective effects vs. hypotension-induced AKI
* **Individualized approach:** Consider baseline renal function and procedure-specific risk

**Diabetic Patients:**
* **Autonomic neuropathy:** Higher risk for hemodynamic instability
* **Renal protection:** Strong indication for continuation in diabetic nephropathy
* **Glycemic effects:** Monitor for interactions with perioperative glucose management

**Emergency Surgery:**
* **Time constraints:** Limited ability to withhold therapy appropriately
* **Risk acceptance:** Focus on hemodynamic management rather than medication adjustment
* **Enhanced monitoring:** Increased vigilance for hypotensive episodes

**Complications and Management:**

**Intraoperative Hypotension:**
* **First-line treatment:**
  * Fluid bolus (250-500 mL crystalloid)
  * Direct-acting vasopressors (phenylephrine 50-100 mcg IV)
  * Norepinephrine (2-10 mcg/min infusion)
* **Refractory hypotension:**
  * Vasopressin 0.5-2 units IV bolus
  * Terlipressin 0.5-2 mg IV (if available)
  * Angiotensin II 5-20 ng/kg/min infusion (if available)
* **Avoid:** High-dose indirect sympathomimetics (ephedrine >1 mg/kg total)

**Postoperative Complications:**
* **Rebound hypertension:** More common with withdrawal strategy
* **Management:** Restart home medications, consider short-acting alternatives
* **Acute kidney injury:** Monitor creatinine, optimize volume status
* **Cardiac events:** No clear difference between strategies in RCT data

**Quality Improvement and Monitoring:**

**Institutional Protocols:**
* **Develop clear guidelines:** Based on local expertise and resources
* **Multidisciplinary input:** Anesthesia, surgery, cardiology, pharmacy
* **Regular updates:** Incorporate new evidence as it becomes available
* **Staff education:** Ensure consistent application across providers

**Outcome Monitoring:**
* **Primary metrics:** Intraoperative hypotension rates, vasopressor use
* **Secondary metrics:** MACE, AKI, length of stay, patient satisfaction
* **Stratified analysis:** Compare outcomes by patient risk, procedure type, strategy used
* **Continuous improvement:** Use data to refine protocols and decision-making

**Research Priorities:**
* **Ongoing trials:** SPACE trial (UK) results eagerly awaited
* **Needed studies:** Large RCTs with standardized protocols and outcome definitions
* **Personalized approaches:** Biomarker-guided therapy, genetic factors
* **Long-term outcomes:** Effects on cardiovascular events beyond 30 days

**Patient Education and Communication:**
* **Shared decision-making:** Involve patients in perioperative medication decisions
* **Risk communication:** Explain potential benefits and harms of each strategy
* **Expectation setting:** Discuss possible perioperative BP changes
* **Compliance planning:** Ensure clear instructions for medication management

💡 **Current Best Practice:** Given conflicting guidelines and evidence, an individualized approach incorporating patient risk factors, surgical complexity, and institutional capabilities provides the most rational strategy for ACE-I/ARB perioperative management.

**Bottom Line:** ACE-I/ARB perioperative management requires individualized decision-making balancing cardiovascular benefits against hypotension risk. Key considerations include indication strength (heart failure vs. hypertension), procedure risk (major vs. minor surgery), anesthetic technique (neuraxial vs. general), and patient factors (age, autonomic function). For high-risk procedures or neuraxial anesthesia, consider withholding 24 hours preoperatively. For strong cardiac indications, continuation with enhanced monitoring may be preferred. Prepare for potential refractory hypotension with vasopressin regardless of strategy chosen.

#### 1.7.3.1. ENHANCED MEDICATION MANAGEMENT STRATEGIES [PERIOPERATIVE GUIDELINES]

**Cardiovascular Medications - Evidence-Based Approach:**

**Beta-Blockers:**
* **Continue existing therapy:** Beta-blockers should be continued in patients already receiving them for appropriate conditions (angina pectoris, symptomatic arrhythmias, hypertension)
* **New initiation considerations:** Initiation of perioperative beta-blockers should be based on clinical judgment evaluating patient's risk factors for cardiovascular complications and type of surgery
* **Timing:** If new beta-blockade initiated, should ideally begin 2 or more days prior to surgery for optimal effect
* ⚠️ **Caution:** Fixed, high-dose beta-blocker therapy begun on day of surgery associated with increased risk of stroke, death, hypotension, and bradycardia

**ACE Inhibitors/ARBs:**
* **Controversy:** The perioperative management remains controversial
* **Evidence:** Withholding ACEI/ARB on day of surgery associated with significantly less intraoperative hypotension
* **No outcome difference:** No difference in mortality or major adverse cardiovascular events whether continued or held
* **Recommendation:** Either continuation or holding on day of surgery is reasonable; if held, should be restarted as soon as clinically feasible
* **Enhanced Management Strategies:**
    * **Risk Assessment Framework:** Individualized decision-making based on:
    * **Patient factors:** Cardiac comorbidities, autonomic function, age
    * **Surgical factors:** Anticipated blood loss, positioning, duration
    * **Anesthetic factors:** Neuraxial vs. general anesthesia, anticipated vasodilator use
    * **Evidence-Based Timing:**
    * **If withholding:** Stop 24 hours before surgery for long-acting agents (enalapril, lisinopril, losartan, valsartan, irbesartan, telmisartan)
    * **Short-acting agents:** Stop night before surgery (>10 hours) for captopril, candesartan
    * **Combination therapy:** Consider additional precautions with concurrent diuretics or multiple antihypertensives
    * **High-Risk Scenarios for Withholding:**
    * **Positioning:** Beach chair, prone, anti-Trendelenburg positions
    * **Major surgery:** Vascular, cardiac, thoracic, major abdominal procedures
    * **Neuraxial anesthesia:** Increased sympathectomy risk
    * **Anticipated blood loss:** >500mL or >10% blood volume
    * **Patient factors:** Elderly, diabetic autonomic neuropathy, heart failure with preserved EF
    * **Indications for Continuation:**
    * **Heart failure with reduced EF:** Strong evidence for continuation in systolic dysfunction
    * **Recent MI:** Within 6 months, especially if incomplete revascularization
    * **High RCRI score:** ≥3 risk factors may benefit from continuation
    * **Low-risk surgery:** Superficial, endoscopic, or outpatient procedures
    * **Intraoperative Management:**
    * **If continued:** Prepare for hypotension with vasopressors, consider reduced-dose induction
    * **Refractory hypotension:** Use vasopressin 0.5-2 units IV or terlipressin for AT-II resistant hypotension
    * **Volume status:** Optimize intravascular volume before induction
    * **Postoperative Considerations:**
    * **Early resumption:** Restart within 24 hours when hemodynamically stable
    * **Monitor closely:** Watch for rebound hypertension if withheld
    * **Document rationale:** Clear documentation of perioperative management decisions

**Statins:**
* **Continue perioperatively:** Strong evidence supports continuation of statin therapy perioperatively
* **Benefits:** Vasodilatory, anti-inflammatory, and antithrombotic effects beyond lipid lowering
* **Withdrawal risks:** Perioperative withdrawal increases morbidity
* **Restart early:** Should be restarted postoperatively as soon as possible
* **New initiation:** Consider starting in high-risk patients 30 days to 1 week before surgery when feasible

**Aspirin:**
* **Not routinely recommended:** For most noncardiac surgery patients
* **Case-by-case basis:** May be appropriate for certain high-risk patients (CAD, cerebrovascular disease) if ischemic risks outweigh bleeding risk
* **Continue for specific indications:** Patients with recent stents or high cardiovascular risk

**Advanced Anticoagulation Management:**

**Warfarin Reversal:**
* **Urgent reversal:** For emergency surgery, administer prothrombin complex concentrates (PCCs) in consultation with appropriate specialist, or fresh frozen plasma
* **Non-urgent reversal:** Administer vitamin K except when rapid restoration of anticoagulation after surgery required

**Novel Oral Anticoagulants (NOACs):**
* **Timing of cessation:** Based on drug half-life, renal function, and bleeding risk of procedure
* **Bridging considerations:** Generally not required for most procedures
* **Reversal agents:** Specific reversal agents available for some NOACs (idarucizumab for dabigatran, andexanet alfa for factor Xa inhibitors)

**Enhanced Diabetes Management:**

**Perioperative Glucose Control:**
* **Target range:** Avoid both hyperglycemia and hypoglycemia
* **Stress response:** Surgery and anesthesia increase insulin resistance and glucose levels
* **Monitoring frequency:** More frequent glucose monitoring required perioperatively

**SGLT2 Inhibitor Management:**
* **Extended cessation:** Stop 2-3 days before surgery
* **Risk mitigation:** Prevents euglycemic diabetic ketoacidosis
* **Alternative agents:** Consider short-acting alternatives if glucose control needed

**Insulin Management:**
* **Basal insulin:** May continue at reduced dose
* **Sliding scale:** Implement for acute glucose management
* **IV insulin protocols:** For major surgery or poor glucose control

**Herbal and Supplement Management:**

**Bleeding Risk Supplements:** (Discontinue 1-2 weeks before surgery)
* **Antiplatelet effects:** Garlic, ginger, ginkgo biloba, feverfew
* **Anticoagulant effects:** Fish oil, vitamin E
* **Multiple mechanisms:** Ginseng, saw palmetto

**Safe to Continue:**
* **Melatonin:** May reduce anxiety and delirium
* **Valerian:** Mild sedative, considered safe

**Drug Interactions and Metabolism:**

**Cytochrome P450 Considerations:**
* **Inducers:** May affect anesthetic drug metabolism
* **Inhibitors:** May prolong anesthetic effects
* **Common culprits:** St. John's wort, grapefruit juice

**Perioperative Pharmacokinetic Changes:**
* **Altered protein binding:** Due to acute phase response
* **Volume of distribution changes:** Due to fluid shifts
* **Hepatic metabolism:** May be altered by surgery and anesthesia

💡 **Clinical Decision Making:** Medication management should be individualized based on patient's medical conditions, type of surgery, anesthetic plan, and risk-benefit analysis. Coordination with other specialists often required for optimal outcomes.

### 1.7.4. POSTOP DISPOSITION

* Some indications: severe OSA for respiratory monitoring, major op in patient with ESRF on HD, IHD EF 20% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

### 1.7.5. COUNSELLING OF RISKS

* GA: AMI/CVA/death, Sore throat, dental/ oral trauma, PONV, drug reactions [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]] [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]]
* Central Neuraxial Block (CNB): spinal/epidural: Infection, bleeding, hematoma, PDPH (1%), paralysis (1:200,000), failure and need for conversion to GA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]] [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]]
* Peripheral Nerve Block (PNB): truncal/upper/lower limb blocks: Infection, bleeding, hematoma, neurological injury, block failure, conversion to GA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]] [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]]
* Invasive lines: IA/CVC: Infection, bleeding, hematoma, thrombosis, pneumothorax, hand perfusion affected [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]] [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]]
* Postop disposition: HD/ICU and risk of cancelling if bed unavailable [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]] [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]]
* PCA: sedation, respiratory depression, giddiness, nausea/vomiting [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]] [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ]]

#### 1.7.5.1. ASA-BASED RISK COMMUNICATION [PATIENT COUNSELING]

**Risk Communication Framework by ASA Class:**

**ASA I-II Patients:**
* **Low Risk Profile:** Emphasize generally low risk while acknowledging that no procedure is without risk
* **Common Concerns:** Address specific patient anxieties about anesthesia awareness, nausea, sore throat
* **Reassurance:** Highlight excellent safety profile for healthy patients
* **Specific Risks:** Focus on procedure-specific rather than patient-specific risks

**ASA III Patients:**
* **Moderate Risk Profile:** Acknowledge increased risk due to medical conditions while emphasizing risk mitigation strategies
* **Disease-Specific Risks:** Discuss how specific comorbidities may affect perioperative course
* **Optimization Benefits:** Explain how preoperative optimization can reduce risks
* **Monitoring Plans:** Describe enhanced monitoring and postoperative care plans

**ASA IV-V Patients:**
* **High Risk Profile:** Honest discussion of significantly elevated risks while maintaining appropriate hope
* **Multidisciplinary Care:** Explain team approach and specialized care requirements
* **Alternative Options:** Discuss whether less invasive alternatives exist
* **Goals of Care:** Ensure alignment between patient/family expectations and realistic outcomes

**Informed Consent Considerations:**
* **ASA Classification Explanation:** Explain what ASA class means in patient-friendly terms
* **Risk Stratification:** Use ASA class as framework for discussing relative risk levels
* **Personalized Risk Assessment:** Combine ASA classification with surgical and patient-specific factors
* **Documentation:** Record ASA class and risk discussion in informed consent documentation

**Family Communication:**
* **Understandable Language:** Avoid medical jargon when explaining ASA classification
* **Risk Context:** Provide perspective on risks compared to everyday activities or other medical procedures
* **Shared Decision Making:** Include family in decision making for high-risk patients
* **Support Resources:** Provide information about available support services

💡 **Communication Best Practice:** Use ASA classification as a starting point for risk discussion but personalize the conversation based on individual patient concerns, values, and specific surgical factors.

**Bottom Line:** Preoperative instructions should cover fasting requirements (with 2023 updated guidelines favoring carbohydrate-containing clear liquids until 2h preoperatively), medication management (continue most cardiac medications except ACEI/ARB, hold oral hypoglycemic agents, manage anticoagulants based on risk), planned postoperative disposition, and thorough counseling about anesthetic risks for informed consent. Use ASA Physical Status Classification as framework for risk communication while personalizing discussions based on individual patient factors and concerns.

# 2. APPROACH TO THE HEALTHY PATIENT




## 2.1. PATIENT IDENTIFICATION, DIAGNOSIS, AND PLANNED PROCEDURE

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to preoperative assessment]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Documentation standards and patient safety]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareIdentifying the patient is the first step when evaluating a patient preoperatively, typically done with legal name, date of birth, and medical record number [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. This also includes documenting the patient's sex assigned at birth, gender identity, preferred name, and preferred pronouns [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Although EMRs may not have a clear format to display this information, it is important for the anesthesiologist to respectfully address their patient in the manner which they prefer [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Assessment of vital signs, height, weight, and body mass index (BMI) provides information regarding physical characteristics, which will be necessary for planning anesthetic dosages, positioning, and evaluation of coexisting diseases as well as airway management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is also important to ascertain the nature of the illness or injury necessitating surgery, as it will determine both the clinical urgency of the proposed operation and influence the available time and depth of the preoperative evaluation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. True emergency procedures require a more abbreviated evaluation and are associated with higher anesthetic morbidity and mortality [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The approach to urgent procedures is less well defined. The anesthesiologist and surgeon must weigh the risk of morbidity of operative delay against the benefits of establishing associated diagnoses that can influence patient management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Table 23-2 shows one classification of operative urgency, though individual hospitals may have their own definitions [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The indication for the surgical procedure may also have implications for other aspects of perioperative management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, the presence of a small bowel obstruction has implications regarding the risk of aspiration and the need for a rapid sequence induction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Similarly, the extent of a lung resection will dictate the need for further pulmonary testing and perioperative monitoring [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Patients undergoing carotid endarterectomy may require a more extensive neurologic examination as well as additional testing to rule out coronary artery disease (CAD) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The planned procedure also dictates patient positioning and whether blood products will be necessary [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Frequently, obtaining such information will require communication with the surgeon and the OR team, enhancing both patient safety and OR efficiency [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A patient's advanced directive should be reviewed and addressed directly with the patient or family, especially when the surgery is for palliation of a terminal illness [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. While "do not resuscitate or intubate" orders are frequently rescinded for procedures, patients or families should be able to express their wishes or engage in a conversation regarding cardiopulmonary resuscitation and administration of life saving efforts [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Classification of Urgency of Surgical Procedures:**
1. **Emergency** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
   * Life, limb, or organ-saving
   * Optimal timing: <6 h
   * Examples: Ruptured aortic aneurysm, Major trauma to thorax/abdomen, Acute increase in intracranial pressure

2. **Urgent** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
   * Conditions threaten life, limb, or organ
   * Optimal timing: 6–24 h
   * Examples: Perforated bowel, Compound fracture, Eye injury

3. **Time sensitive** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
   * Stable, but requires intervention
   * Optimal timing: Days to weeks
   * Examples: Tendon/nerve injuries, Cancer procedures

4. **Elective** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
   * Procedure planned at patient or surgeon convenience
   * Optimal timing: Up to 1 yr
   * Examples: All other procedures that can be planned in advance

**Bottom Line:** Proper patient identification, including preferred name and pronouns, is fundamental to safety and respect. The urgency of surgery dictates the depth of preoperative evaluation and should be classified clearly. The specific surgical procedure informs many aspects of anesthetic planning, from airway management to monitoring requirements.

## 2.2. RESPONSE TO PREVIOUS ANESTHETICS

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to assessing previous anesthetic experiences]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Patient safety and quality improvement]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareThe ability to review previous anesthetic records is particularly helpful for detecting the presence of a difficult airway, a history of malignant hyperthermia (MH), and the individual's response to surgical stress and specific anesthetics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The patient should be questioned regarding any previous personal or familial difficulties with anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A patient's report of an "allergy" to anesthesia should raise suspicion for MH [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A diagnosis of MH susceptibility will affect the anesthetic regimen and bring into question the appropriateness of outpatient surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Although not life threatening, persistent nausea and vomiting after a previous surgery may be the patient's most negative and lasting memory [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It also has the potential to cause a prolonged recovery room stay or an unplanned hospital admission [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There are multiple predictors of postoperative nausea and vomiting (PONV), including type of surgical procedure, anesthetic agents, and patient factors [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Risk Factors for PONV in Adults With Positive Overall Evidence:**
* Female sex [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* History of PONV or motion sickness [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Nonsmoking [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Younger age (<50 yr) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* General versus regional anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Volatile anesthetics, nitrous oxide [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Postoperative opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Duration of anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Type of surgery: cholecystectomy, laparoscopic, gynecologic [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

The Apfel simplified risk score predicts PONV with 0, 1, 2, 3, or 4 risk factors as 10%, 20%, 40%, 60%, and 80%, respectively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Society guidelines propose dual prophylactic antiemetic therapy when 1 to 2 risk factors are present and use of 3 to 4 agents for patients with more than 2 risk factors [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, armed with this knowledge preoperatively, the anesthesiologist is also able to tailor the anesthetic or possibly avoid the most likely causes of PONV: volatile anesthetics, nitrous oxide, postoperative opioids, and an unnecessarily prolonged NPO time altogether [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

In children, independent predictors of postoperative vomiting (POV) include: duration of surgery longer than 30 minutes, age above 3 years, history of POV in patient or family, and strabismus surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Based on the presence of 0, 1, 2, 3, and 4 factors, the risk of POV was 9%, 10%, 30%, 55%, and 70%, respectively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Risk Factors for Pediatric Postoperative Nausea and Vomiting (PONV):**
* Age ≥3 years [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Strabismus surgery, adenotonsillectomy, otoplasty [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* History of PONV/motion sickness [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Surgery duration ≥30 min [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Family history of PONV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Volatile anesthetics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Post-pubertal female [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Anticholinesterases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Long-acting opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

Most recent guidelines suggest pediatric patients with 1 to 2 risk factors receive dual antiemetic therapy with a 5HT3 antagonist and dexamethasone, while those with 3 or more risk factors should receive dual antiemetic therapy as well as consideration for TIVA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Reviewing previous anesthetic records helps identify potential issues including difficult airways, malignant hyperthermia, and PONV risk. For PONV risk assessment, use validated tools like the Apfel score for adults and specific pediatric risk factors. This allows for prophylactic strategies including multimodal antiemetics and anesthetic technique modifications.

## 2.3. MEDICATIONS/ALLERGIES

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to medication assessment and allergy evaluation]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Patient safety and medication management]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareThe history should include a complete list of medications, including over-the-counter and herbal products, to define a preoperative medication regimen, anticipate potential drug interactions, and provide clues to underlying disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A complete list of drug allergies or reactions should be obtained, as well as any history of reaction to latex [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

For the patient who presents on the day of surgery, the anesthesiologist should determine when the patient last ate solids and drank clear liquids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Once the general issues are completed, the preoperative history and physical examination can focus on specific systems [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Herbal/Dietary Supplements and Drug Interactions:**

Many herbal supplements have pharmacologic effects and drug interactions relevant to anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]:

* **Cannabinoids**: Can cause sympathetic hyperactivity, impaired temperature regulation, airway irritability, increased risk of coronary vasospasm, interaction with anesthesia drugs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Discontinue at least 3 days but ideally 2 weeks before surgery (except FDA-approved uses) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Ephedra**: Increases heart rate and blood pressure, enhances sympathomimetic effects, potential arrhythmias with digoxin or halothane, hypertension with oxytocin [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Not recommended for use [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Feverfew**: Inhibits platelet activity and increases bleeding, causes rebound headache with sudden cessation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hold 2 weeks before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Garlic**: Inhibition of platelet aggregation, potential for increased bleeding [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hold 2 weeks before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Ginger**: Potent inhibitor of thromboxane synthetase; may increase bleeding time [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hold 2 weeks before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Ginkgo**: May enhance bleeding in patients on anticoagulant or antithrombotic therapy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hold 2 weeks before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Ginseng**: May inhibit platelet aggregation and increase bleeding, decreases blood glucose [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hold 1 week before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Kava-kava**: Potentiates sedative effects of anesthetics, may cause hepatotoxicity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hold 2 weeks before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **St. John's Wort**: May prolong effects of anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hold 2 weeks before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Melatonin**: May reduce anxiety, may decrease delirium in hospitalized elderly patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Can continue [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Valerian**: Mild sedative, considered safe [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Can continue [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Vitamin E**: May increase bleeding, especially with other anticoagulants [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hold 2 weeks before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** A thorough medication history, including prescription drugs, over-the-counter medications, and herbal supplements, is essential for identifying potential drug interactions and complications. Many herbal supplements affect coagulation or interact with anesthetics and should be discontinued 1-2 weeks before surgery. Document all allergies with the specific reaction noted.

## 2.4. SCREENING PATIENTS USING A SYSTEMS APPROACH

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to organ system-based evaluation]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient Care### 2.4.2. AIRWAY

One of the most critical aspects of the preoperative evaluation is the ability of the anesthesiologist to recognize the potential for difficulty in maintaining a patent airway with a mask, a laryngeal mask airway, or placement of an endotracheal tube when the patient is under general anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A review of previous anesthetic records is especially useful in uncovering an unsuspected "difficult airway" or to confirm previous uneventful tracheal intubations, noting whether the patient's body habitus or airway anatomy has changed in the interim [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Patients should be questioned about their ability to breathe through their nose, whether there is suspected or diagnosed obstructive sleep apnea (OSA), and whether they have orthopnea [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Evaluation of the airway involves examination of the oral cavity, including dentition, determination of the thyromental distance, assessment of the size of the patient's neck and potential tracheal deviation or masses, as well as evaluation of their ability to flex the base of the neck and extend the head [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For trauma patients or patients with severe rheumatoid arthritis (RA) or Down syndrome, assessment for signs or symptoms of cervical cord compression is critical [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In some instances, radiographic examination may also be required [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The Mallampati classification is the standard for assessing the relationship of the tongue size relative to the oral cavity, although by itself the Mallampati classification has a low positive predictive value in identifying patients who are difficult to intubate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Intubation involves multiple steps: flexion of the lower neck, extension of the head on the neck, opening the mouth to insert the laryngoscope, and displacing the tongue forward and down into the submandibular space to expose the glottis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Therefore, a multifactorial approach to predicting intubation difficulty has proven to be more helpful [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. One must distinguish factors that predict a difficult intubation and factors that predict a difficult mask airway [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, the absence of teeth clearly makes laryngoscopy less difficult, but at the same time can make mask ventilation more challenging [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Modified Mallampati Airway Classification System:**
* **Class I:** Full view of soft palate, uvula, tonsillar pillars [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Class II:** Soft palate and upper portion of uvula [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Class III:** Soft palate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Class IV:** Hard palate only [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Components of the Airway Examination That Suggest Difficult Tracheal Intubation:**
1. Long upper incisors [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
2. Prominent "overbite" [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
3. Inability to protrude the mandibular incisors anterior to maxillary incisors [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
4. Distance between incisors is <3 cm when mouth is fully opened [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
5. Uvula not visible when tongue is protruded with patient sitting [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
6. Shape of palate highly arched or very narrow [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
7. Mandibular space noncompliant [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
8. Thyromental distance <3 fingerbreadths [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
9. Neck short or thick [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
10. Limited range of motion of head and neck [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

### 2.4.1. PULMONARY SYSTEM

A screening evaluation should include questions regarding the history of tobacco use, dyspnea, exercise tolerance, cough, wheezing, bronchodilator or steroid use, recent upper respiratory tract infection, stridor, and snoring or sleep apnea [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
