Physical examination should assess respiratory rate, chest excursion, use of accessory muscles, nail color, and the patient's ability to carry on a conversation or to walk without dyspnea [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Auscultation can detect decreased breath sounds, wheezing, stridor, or crackles [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 2.4.2. CARDIOVASCULAR SYSTEM

When screening a patient for cardiovascular disease prior to surgery, the anesthesiologist is most interested in recognizing signs and symptoms of uncontrolled hypertension and unstable cardiac disease such as myocardial ischemia, congestive heart failure, valvular heart disease, and significant cardiac dysrhythmias [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. One should assess for symptoms of dyspnea, chest pain, syncope, or decreased exercise tolerance that would require further evaluation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Certain populations of patients, such as the elderly, women, or diabetics, may present with more atypical features [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The presence of unstable angina has been associated with a high perioperative risk of myocardial infarction (MI) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The perioperative period is associated with a hypercoagulable state and surges in endogenous catecholamines, both of which may exacerbate the underlying process in unstable angina, increasing the risk of acute infarction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The patient should be questioned about symptoms of clinically important valvular disease, such as angina, dyspnea, syncope, or congestive heart failure, that would require further evaluation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Importantly, the anesthesiologist must identify patients who have undergone placement of a coronary artery stent, have an implantable cardiac device, or are taking an antiplatelet or anticoagulant regimen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The perioperative care of these patients should be coordinated with a cardiologist [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The anesthesiologist should be familiar with the American Heart Association (AHA) website (<http://www.heart.org/>) and its links to the latest AHA statements and guidelines for health professionals [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Here one can find the most recent recommendations regarding specific patients and procedures that require subacute bacterial endocarditis prophylaxis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The examination of the cardiovascular system should include blood pressure evaluation, measuring both arms when appropriate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The anesthesiologist should consider the effects of preoperative anxiety and review resting blood pressure measurements [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. During auscultation of the heart, one should specifically listen for a murmur radiating to the carotids suggestive of aortic stenosis, abnormal rhythms, or a gallop suggestive of heart failure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The presence of bruits over the carotid arteries warrants further workup to determine the risk of stroke [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The extremities should be examined for peripheral pulses to exclude peripheral vascular disease or congenital cardiovascular disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 2.4.3. NEUROLOGIC SYSTEM

Neurologic assessment in the apparently healthy patient can be accomplished through simple observation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The patient's ability to answer health history questions practically indicates a normal mental status [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Questions can be directed regarding a history of stroke, symptoms of cerebrovascular disease, seizures, pre-existing neuromuscular disease, or nerve injuries [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The neurologic examination may be cursory in healthy patients or extensive in patients with coexisting disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Testing of strength, reflexes, and sensation may be important in patients for whom the anesthetic plan or surgical procedure may result in a change in condition [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 2.4.4. ENDOCRINE SYSTEM

Each patient should be questioned for a history or symptoms of endocrine diseases that may affect the perioperative course: diabetes mellitus, thyroid disease, parathyroid disease, endocrine-secreting tumors, and adrenal cortical suppression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** A systems-based screening approach allows for comprehensive yet focused evaluation. For airway assessment, combine multiple predictive factors rather than relying solely on Mallampati score. For cardiovascular screening, focus on unstable conditions, stents, and cardiac devices. For pulmonary assessment, note exercise tolerance, recent infections, and risk factors for complications. Neurologic and endocrine screening should identify conditions requiring specific perioperative management.

# 3. EVALUATION OF THE PATIENT WITH KNOWN SYSTEMIC DISEASE





## 3.1. CARDIOVASCULAR DISEASE

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|ⓘ|Part A: Cardiovascular physiology]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to cardiovascular disease management]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Evidence-based practice guidelines]]

**Exam Weighting:** High (Critical for safe anesthesia practice)

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareGlobally, more than 300 million surgical procedures are performed annually [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. With an aging population, the incidence of comorbid conditions in patients undergoing noncardiac surgery has risen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Nearly half of patients over 45 years of age undergoing noncardiac surgery have multiple cardiovascular risk factors such as hypertension, hyperlipidemia, diabetes mellitus, obesity, and chronic kidney disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Almost 25% of these patients have a history of atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These risk factors place them at higher risk of perioperative cardiovascular complications such as death, MI, and stroke, which are a major source of morbidity and mortality, and occur at a rate of 3% for noncardiac surgeries [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The incidence of major adverse cardiovascular events (MACEs) is related to the risk factors present in each individual patient prior to surgery, as well as the risk-profile of the surgery itself [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The preoperative evaluation of the patient with known or suspected cardiovascular disease is focused on two areas: identification of clinical risk factors and preoperative cardiac testing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The goals are to define risk, identify which patients will benefit from further testing, determine whether perioperative β-blockade, interventional therapy, or even surgery would be beneficial before the planned procedure, and form an appropriate anesthetic plan [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

#### 3.1.1.1. SYSTEMATIC PREOPERATIVE EVALUATION FRAMEWORK [PART B - CLINICAL APPROACH]

**Three-Pillar Risk Assessment for IHD Patients:**

The preoperative evaluation of IHD patients going for non-cardiac surgery should systematically address three key areas `[PDF:3]`:

**1. Rule Out Active/Unstable Cardiac Conditions** `[PDF:3,4]`

⚠️ **Critical Assessment - These require immediate attention and potential surgery postponement:**

* **Acute Coronary Syndrome:**
  * **History:** Assess for symptoms of unstable angina, rest pain, crescendo angina
  * **Examination:** New onset mitral regurgitation (papillary muscle dysfunction), signs of heart failure
  * **Investigations:** ECG (ST changes, new Q waves), troponin elevation

* **Decompensated Heart Failure:**
  * **History:** Orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea, recent fluid indiscretion, weight gain
  * **Examination:** Lung crackles, pedal edema, elevated JVP, S3 gallop
  * **Investigations:** CXR (pulmonary edema, cardiomegaly), NT-proBNP elevation

* **Significant Arrhythmias:**
  * **Tachyarrhythmias:** New onset or poorly controlled atrial fibrillation, ventricular tachycardia
  * **Bradyarrhythmias:** High-grade AV block, symptomatic bradycardia
  * **Assessment:** Vitals (HR, BP), ECG, electrolytes (K, Mg, Ca)

💡 **Clinical Pearl:** The presence of any active/unstable cardiac condition represents an extremely high perioperative risk and should prompt cardiology referral and treatment of the acute condition before proceeding with elective surgery.

**2. Evaluate Current Cardiac Function** `[PDF:3,5]`

**Comprehensive Functional Assessment:**
* **Functional capacity:** See detailed METS assessment (section 3.1.2.1)
* **Coronary revascularization status:**
  * Previous PCI/stenting: timing, type of stent (BMS vs DES), indication (stable CAD vs ACS)
  * Previous CABG: timing, number of grafts, completeness of revascularization
  * See section 3.1.7.1 for detailed antiplatelet management
* **Cardiac investigations:** `[PDF:5]`
  * **2D Echocardiography:** LVEF, regional wall motion abnormalities (RWMAs), valvular function, pulmonary artery pressure
  * **MIBI/Nuclear imaging:** Perfusion defects, stress-induced ischemia, stress EF
  * **Coronary angiogram:** If available, extent and severity of CAD
* **Current medications:** Especially antiplatelets, beta-blockers, ACE-I/ARB, statins
* **Recent general anesthetics:** Previous perioperative complications, hemodynamic stability

**3. Assess Surgical Risk Factors** `[PDF:3]`

**Procedure-Specific Considerations:**
* Type and duration of surgery
* Invasiveness of surgery (neuroendocrine stress response, fluid shifts/blood loss, thrombogenicity)
* Open vs minimally invasive/laparoscopic approach
* Special considerations (e.g., ability to tolerate pneumoperitoneum, positioning requirements)
* See section 3.1.4 for detailed surgical risk stratification

**Strongest Independent Predictors of Adverse Perioperative Cardiac Outcomes:** `[PDF:3]`
1. **Active cardiac conditions** (highest priority)
2. **Poor exercise tolerance** (<4 METS)
3. **High-risk surgery** (vascular, intraperitoneal, intrathoracic)

**Bottom Line:** A systematic three-pillar approach to preoperative cardiac evaluation ensures no critical risk factors are missed. Active/unstable conditions require immediate attention, current cardiac function guides perioperative monitoring and management, and surgical risk assessment determines the overall probability of adverse events.

### 3.1.2. IDENTIFYING CLINICAL RISK FACTORS

A focused history and cardiovascular physical examination is the first step in estimating perioperative cardiovascular risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Comorbid conditions associated with MACE should be identified [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These include a history of ischemic heart disease, presence of coronary stents, heart failure, dysrhythmias, valvular heart disease, systemic and pulmonary hypertension, chronic kidney disease, and diabetes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Exercise tolerance remains one of the most important predictors of perioperative risk for noncardiac surgery and helps define the need for further testing and invasive monitoring [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. An excellent exercise tolerance, even in patients with stable angina, suggests that the myocardium can be stressed without failing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Alternatively, if patients experience dyspnea associated with chest pain during minimal exertion, the probability of extensive CAD is high, and is associated with greater perioperative risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In addition, these patients are at risk for developing hypotension with ischemia and, therefore, may benefit from preoperative coronary intervention therapy, revascularization, or more intensive intraoperative monitoring [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Exercise tolerance can be assessed with formal treadmill testing or with a questionnaire that assesses activities of daily living [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Patient-reported poor exercise tolerance (i.e., inability to walk four blocks or climb two flights of stairs) is independently associated with a twofold risk of serious perioperative complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The likelihood of a serious adverse event is inversely related to the number of blocks that can be walked [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Therefore, there is good evidence to suggest that minimal additional testing is necessary if the patient is able to describe a good exercise tolerance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Estimated Energy Requirement for Various Activities:**
* **1 MET:** Daily self-care (eat, dress, toilet), walk indoors, walk 2-3 blocks on level ground at 2-3 mph, light housework [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **4 METs:** Climb a flight of stairs, walk on level ground at 4 mph, run a short distance, heavy housework, moderate recreational activities (golf, bowling, dancing, doubles tennis) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **>10 METs:** Strenuous sports (swimming, singles tennis, football, basketball, skiing) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

#### 3.1.2.1. ENHANCED FUNCTIONAL CAPACITY ASSESSMENT [PART B - CLINICAL APPLICATION]

**Comprehensive Methods for Functional Evaluation:**

**Subjective Assessment Methods:**
* **METS (Metabolic Equivalents):** Most commonly used in daily practice `[PDF:6]`
  * 1 MET = 3.5ml O2/kg/min = O2 consumption at rest
  * Cutoff for surgery = 4 METS (climbing stairs)
  * **Detailed Activity Classification:**
    * 1 MET: Energy expenditure at rest (3.5ml O2/kg/min)
    * 2 METs: Walking on level ground, light housework
    * 4 METs: Climbing a flight of stairs, walking uphill, heavy housework
    * 6 METs: Leisure swimming/cycling
    * 10 METs: Strenuous sports (competitive football, moderate to hard swimming, fast cycling)

* **Duke Activity Status Index (DASI):** Structured questionnaire assessing functional capacity `[PDF:6]`

**Objective Assessment Methods:**
* **6-Minute Walk Test (6MWT):** Standardized distance-based assessment
* **Incremental Shuttle Walk Test (ISWT):** Externally paced walking test
* **Cardiopulmonary Exercise Testing (CPET):** Gold standard for objective functional capacity `[PDF:6]`

**METS Study Evidence (Lancet 2018):**

💡 **Critical Findings that Changed Practice:**
* **Subjective assessment NOT predictive:** Subjective functional capacity assessment was not predictive of postoperative MI and not predictive of ability to attain 4 METs on CPET `[PDF:7]`
* **Only DASI score was predictive:** Of subjective measures, only DASI score was predictive of 30-day death/MI `[PDF:7]`
* **Objective CPET findings:** Reduced VO2peak (<14ml/kg/min) on CPET predictive of postoperative complications, but interestingly not cardiac complications specifically `[PDF:7]`
* **NT-proBNP superiority:** Higher NT-proBNP levels were predictive of 30-day death/MI, suggesting biomarkers may be more reliable than subjective functional assessment `[PDF:7]`

⚠️ **Clinical Implications:**
* Cannot rely solely on patient-reported METs for risk stratification
* Consider DASI structured questionnaire for more reliable subjective assessment
* NT-proBNP may be valuable addition to preoperative cardiac risk assessment
* CPET remains gold standard but primarily predicts overall complications rather than cardiac-specific events

In virtually all studies, the preoperative presence of active congestive heart failure is a major risk factor for increased perioperative cardiac morbidity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Clinical evidence of heart failure includes symptoms of dyspnea, limited exercise tolerance, and orthopnea, as well as physical signs of jugular venous distention, crackles, third heart sound, and peripheral edema [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A chest x-ray can reveal pulmonary edema or vascular redistribution [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Significantly reduced left ventricular ejection fraction (e.g., <30%) appears to be an independent risk factor for adverse perioperative outcome and long-term mortality [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Another study suggests that asymptomatic systolic or diastolic dysfunction is associated with increased 30-day cardiovascular perioperative risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Optimization of ventricular function and treatment of pulmonary edema are both important prior to elective surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Identifying the cause of heart failure is important (e.g., nonischemic cardiomyopathy or cardiac valvular insufficiency and/or stenosis) as it may alter the plan for perioperative monitoring and treatment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Patients with known valvular heart disease can be effectively managed during the perioperative period to limit morbidity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The 2020 AHA/ACC guideline for the management of patients with valvular disease supports the use of preoperative echocardiography in patients with moderate or severe valvular stenosis or regurgitation with no echocardiography studies within 1 year, or worsening clinical status [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Valvular interventions (e.g., repair or replacement) may be indicated before elective noncardiac surgery depending on the symptoms or severity of disease and the urgency of the noncardiac surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Understanding the severity of stenotic or regurgitant valvular disease, coupled with an intraoperative monitoring and management plan, may reduce the risk of perioperative congestive heart failure and respiratory failure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Adults with a prior MI almost always have CAD [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Traditionally, risk assessment for noncardiac surgery was based on the time interval between the MI and surgery, which was based on older data that demonstrated an increased incidence of reinfarction if the MI was within 6 months of surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The importance of the intervening time interval may no longer be valid in the current era of interventional therapy and improvements in perioperative care [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Although many patients with an MI may continue to have myocardium at risk for subsequent ischemia and infarction, other patients may have their critical coronary stenoses either totally occluded or widely patent, with no such risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, the use of percutaneous transluminal coronary angioplasty, thrombolysis, and early coronary artery bypass grafting (CABG) has changed the natural history of the disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Therefore, patients should be individually evaluated from the perspective of their risk for ongoing ischemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 3.1.3. IDENTIFYING PATIENTS AT RISK FOR ATHEROSCLEROTIC CARDIAC DISEASE

For those patients without overt cardiac symptoms, the probability of CAD varies with the type and number of atherosclerotic risk factors present [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Peripheral arterial disease has been shown to be associated with CAD in multiple studies [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In patients with symptomatic coronary disease, the preoperative evaluation may reveal a change in the frequency or pattern of anginal symptoms [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Certain populations of patients—for example, the elderly, women, or diabetics—may present with more atypical features and must be carefully assessed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The presence of unstable angina has been associated with a high perioperative risk of MI [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Diabetes Mellitus:** Diabetes mellitus is a common disease with a pathophysiology that affects multiple organ systems [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Complications of diabetes mellitus are frequently the cause of urgent or emergent surgery, especially in the elderly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Diabetes accelerates the progression of atherosclerosis, so it is not surprising that diabetics have a higher incidence of CAD than nondiabetics as well as a high incidence of both silent MI and myocardial ischemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Eagle et al. demonstrated that diabetes is an independent risk factor for perioperative cardiac morbidity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The duration of the disease and presence of associated end-organ dysfunction may also alter the overall cardiac risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Autonomic neuropathy has been reported as the best predictor of silent ischemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Because these patients are at very high risk for a silent MI, a preoperative electrocardiogram (ECG) should be obtained to examine for the presence of Q waves [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Hypertension:** Hypertension has also been associated with an increased incidence of silent myocardial ischemia and infarction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hypertensive patients who have left ventricular hypertrophy and are undergoing noncardiac surgery are at a higher perioperative risk than nonhypertensive patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Investigators have suggested that the presence of a strain pattern on ECG suggests a chronic ischemic state [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Aggressive treatment of blood pressure is associated with reduction in long-term MI risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The 2017 ACC/AHA guideline for the detection and management of high blood pressure, which defines normal blood pressure as less than 120 systolic and less than 80 diastolic, recommends treatment of confirmed hypertension through lifestyle or pharmacologic methods with a target BP less than 130/80 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, there are no such guidelines to suggest a safe upper blood pressure limit when a patient presents for surgery with significant hypertension [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. 

![[Pasted image 20250720104856.png]]

S1 HTN has ~2x increase in CVD risk whereas recent RCTs have demonstrated benefit with an SBP below 130mmHg (Thomopolous et al (2016), Xie et al (2016), Verdecchia et al (2016), Bangalore et al (2017), Bundy et al (2017).

Indications for pharmacological intervention:
- Stage 1 HTN with
    - Secondary prevention: Adults with existing CVD
    - Primary prevention: 10-year ASCVD risk ≥10%, DM, CKD, age ≥ 65YO (SPRINT Research Group, NEJM, 2015)
- All patients with stage 2 HTN

First line therapy, to treat to target of <130/80mmHg (Bundy et al, 2017):
- First-line: ACEIs or ARBs, CCBs and thiazide diuretics (COR: I, LOE: A)
    - General similarity in efficacy and safety of first-line agent, and achieving BP lowering targets more important than choice of drugs (Reboussin, 2017
    - BB are not recommended as first line for primary prevention, HTN management or stable angina without prior MI
- Stage 2 hypertension: Initiation of 2 agents from different classes recommended if SBP >20mmHg and DBP >10mmHg above target of 130/80mmHg (Class 1, LOE: C-EO)

There is little association reported between elevated blood pressures (up to 180 mmHg systolic or up to 110 mmHg diastolic) and postoperative outcomes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. 
- In the absence of concomitant CVD or hypertensive end-organ damage (e.g., LVH or renal dysfunction), isolated hypertension does not seem to increase perioperative risk in noncardiac surgery
- However, such patients are prone to perioperative myocardial ischemia, ventricular dysrhythmias, and lability in blood pressure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. 

It is less clear in patients with blood pressures above 180/110 mmHg, as no absolute evidence exists that postponing surgery will reduce risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Although the literature suggests that elective surgery should be delayed if the diastolic pressure is above 110 mmHg, this study demonstrated no major morbidity in that small group of patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Thus, in the absence of end-organ changes, such as renal insufficiency or left ventricular hypertrophy with strain, the benefits of optimizing blood pressure must be weighed against the risks of delaying surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Perioperative HTN may be significant in the following surgeries
1. Adult cardiac surgery - systolic HTN > 140mmHg, systolic hypervariability (SBP >140 and/or <80mmHg) and pulse pressure HTN (PP > 80mmHg) are significant risk factors for perioperative death, stroke, LV dysfunction and renal failure.
2. Pheochromocytoma surgery
3. Vascular surgery (carotid endarterectomy and carotid stenting) - increased risk of neurological deficit
4. Presence of LV Hypertrophy undergoing major vascular surgery increases risk of MI and/or cardiac death (Landesberg etal, J Vasc Surg, 1997)
5. Severe baseline HTN (>180/110mmHg to delay as above)

**Metabolic Syndrome/Tobacco:** Metabolic syndrome and tobacco use are also associated with atherosclerotic cardiac disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Metabolic syndrome is a disorder comprising a group of risk factors that includes high blood pressure, atherogenic dyslipidemia, high fasting glucose concentrations, and central obesity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Metabolic syndrome has been associated with higher rates of cardiovascular, pulmonary, and renal perioperative events, as well as wound infections [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 3.1.4. IMPORTANCE OF SURGICAL PROCEDURE

The surgical procedure influences the scope of the preoperative evaluation by suggesting the range of physiologic changes that may occur during the perioperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Peripheral procedures are associated with an extremely low incidence of morbidity and mortality, whereas major open vascular procedures are associated with the highest incidence of complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Eagle et al. published data from the Coronary Artery Surgery Study (CASS) on the incidence of perioperative MI and mortality by procedure for patients with known CAD and found that high-risk procedures included major vascular, abdominal, thoracic, and orthopedic surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Contemporary surgical techniques such as endovascular or minimally invasive surgery may decrease MACE in otherwise high-risk procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

#### 3.1.4.1. SURGICAL RISK STRATIFICATION - 2022 ESC GUIDELINES [PART B - CLINICAL CLASSIFICATION]

**Comprehensive Surgical Risk Classification:**

The 2022 ESC Guidelines provide a detailed stratification of surgical procedures by cardiac risk `[PDF:8]`:

**Low Surgical Risk (<1% 30-day risk of death/MI):**
* Breast surgery
* Dental procedures
* Endocrine: thyroid surgery
* Eye surgery
* Gynaecological: minor procedures
* Orthopaedic: minor (e.g., meniscectomy)
* Reconstructive surgery
* Superficial surgery
* Urological: minor (e.g., transurethral resection of the prostate)
* VATS: minor lung resection

**Intermediate Surgical Risk (1-5% 30-day risk of death/MI):**
* **Carotid symptomatic:** CEA or CAS
* **Carotid symptomatic:** CEA (carotid endarterectomy)
* **Endovascular aortic aneurysm repair**
* Head or neck surgery
* **Intraperitoneal:** splenectomy, hiatal hernia repair, cholecystectomy
* Intrathoracic: non-major
* Neurological or orthopaedic: major (e.g., hip and spine surgery)
* Peripheral arterial angioplasty
* Renal transplants
* Urological or gynaecological: major

**High Surgical Risk (>5% 30-day risk of death/MI):**
* **Adrenal resection**
* **Aortic and major vascular surgery**
* **Carotid symptomatic:** CAS (carotid artery stenting)
* **Duodenal-pancreatic surgery**
* **Liver resection, bile duct surgery**
* **Oesophagectomy**
* **Open lower limb revascularization for acute limb ischaemia or amputation**
* **Pneumonectomy** (VATS or open surgery)
* **Pulmonary or liver transplant**
* **Repair of perforated bowel**
* **Total cystectomy**

**Factors Influencing Surgical Risk Assessment:** `[PDF:8]`
* **Type and duration of surgery:** Longer procedures carry higher risk
* **Invasiveness of surgery:**
  * Neuroendocrine stress response magnitude
  * Anticipated fluid shifts and blood loss
  * Thrombogenicity of the procedure
* **Surgical approach:** Open vs laparoscopic/endovascular
  * Laparoscopic procedures: Consider ability to tolerate pneumoperitoneum (hemodynamic effects, increased PVR, reduced venous return)
  * Positioning requirements (e.g., Trendelenburg, prone, beach chair)

💡 **Clinical Pearl:** The 2022 ESC guidelines emphasize that for patients with established CAD undergoing **low-risk surgery**, no additional cardiac investigations are required even in the presence of cardiovascular risk factors. This represents a shift toward more selective testing strategies.

**Bottom Line:** Surgical risk stratification is essential for determining the need for preoperative cardiac testing and perioperative monitoring intensity. High-risk procedures (vascular, major intraperitoneal, intrathoracic) warrant more comprehensive evaluation, while low-risk procedures rarely require additional testing regardless of patient risk factors.

### 3.1.5. CALCULATION OF RISK TO PREDICT PERIOPERATIVE MORBIDITY

Validated risk-prediction tools can help predict perioperative MACE in patients undergoing noncardiac surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Historically, these have included the ASA physical status index and the Goldman Cardiac Risk Index [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The ASA classification system is independently associated with postoperative outcomes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In a prospective study of 6,301 surgical patients, healthy ASA I patients had a significantly lower risk of cardiac complications and mortality (0.1% and 0.1%), compared to ASA IV patients (18% and 18.3%) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The Revised Cardiac Risk Index (RCRI) is an update of the Goldman Cardiac Risk Index, and is a validated method to assign perioperative risk using readily available clinical variables [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In a population of 4,315 patients aged 50 years and older undergoing elective, major noncardiac procedures, 6 independent predictors of complications were identified and included in the RCRI: high-risk type of surgery, history of ischemic heart disease, history of congestive heart failure, history of cerebrovascular disease, insulin-dependent diabetes, and chronic kidney disease (serum creatinine >2 mg/dL) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Cardiac complications rose with an increase in the number of risk factors present [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In patients with an RCRI score of 0, the risk of perioperative cardiovascular complications is 0.4% compared to approximately 11% in patients with a score of 3 or more [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Revised Cardiac Risk Index (RCRI):**
* **Risk Factors** (1 point each): Ischemic heart disease, Congestive heart failure, Cerebrovascular disease, Insulin-dependent diabetes mellitus, Serum creatinine >2 mg/dL, High-risk surgery (intraperitoneal, intrathoracic, or suprainguinal vascular) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Points and Risk (Updated 2019 Values):**
  * 0 points: 3.9% risk (95% CI 2.8-5.4%) `[PDF:9]`
  * 1 point: 6.0% risk (95% CI 4.9-7.4%) `[PDF:9]`
  * 2 points: 10.1% risk (95% CI 8.1-12.6%) `[PDF:9]`
  * ≥3 points: 15% risk (95% CI 11.1-20.0%) `[PDF:9]`

⚠️ **Historical Note:** Numbers updated in 2019 from newer studies (Duceppe 2017). Not to be confused with original 1977 study which quoted much lower numbers: 0.4%, 0.9%, 6.6%, 11%. The updated values reflect contemporary surgical and anesthetic practice and should be used for current risk stratification `[PDF:9]`.

**RCRI Clinical Application:**
* Provides standardized risk communication framework
* Helps guide need for further cardiac testing
* Informs intensity of perioperative monitoring
* Facilitates multidisciplinary care planning for high-risk patients

Other validated risk-prediction tools developed by the American College of Surgeons (ACS) include the National Surgical Quality Improvement Program (NSQIP) and the Myocardial Infarction and Cardiac Arrest (MICA) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The ACS NSQIP Surgical Risk Calculator uses current procedural terminology codes and 21 patient-specific variables for prediction of several groups of outcomes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. This may provide better predictive discrimination than the RCRI, although may not be practical in a busy preoperative clinic as the calculation takes more time and is more complex [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 3.1.6. PREOPERATIVE CARDIAC TESTING

Multiple algorithms have been proposed to determine which patients require further cardiovascular testing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. As described previously, the risk associated with the proposed surgical procedure influences the decision to perform further diagnostic testing and interventions [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. With the reduction in perioperative morbidity, it has been suggested that extensive cardiovascular testing is not always necessary [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The algorithm to determine the need for testing in a patient at risk for CAD proposed by the 2014 ACC/AHA Task Force is based on available evidence and expert opinion that integrates clinical history, surgery-specific risk, and exercise tolerance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In the first step, the clinician evaluates the urgency of the surgery and the appropriateness of a formal preoperative assessment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Next, one determines if the patient has undergone a recent revascularization procedure or coronary evaluation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Those patients with unstable coronary syndromes should be identified, have appropriate consultations made, and treatment instituted [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Finally, the decision to perform further testing depends on the interaction of the clinical risk factors, surgery-specific risk, and functional capacity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For patients at high risk, both exercise tolerance and the extent of the surgery are taken into account to determine the need for further testing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Most importantly, no preoperative cardiovascular testing should be performed if the results will not change perioperative management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Electrocardiogram:** A preoperative 12-lead ECG can establish the patient's baseline cardiac rhythm, identify clinically silent cardiac disease (e.g., prior MI and left ventricular hypertrophy), and serve as a baseline for changes in the postoperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Abnormal Q waves in high-risk patients are highly suggestive of a past MI [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It has been estimated that approximately 30% of MIs occur without symptoms ("silent infarctions") and can only be detected on screening ECGs, with the highest incidence occurring in patients with either diabetes or hypertension [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The Framingham Study showed that long-term prognosis after MI is not improved by lack of symptoms at the time of MI [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The absence of Q waves on the ECG, however, does not exclude the occurrence of a Q-wave MI in the past [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Those patients in whom the ECG reverts to normal have improved survival compared to those with consistent abnormalities, with or without Q waves [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The presence of Q waves on a preoperative ECG in a high-risk patient, regardless of symptoms, should alert the anesthesiologist to the increased perioperative risk and the potential for active ischemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The 2014 ACC/AHA Clinical Practice Guideline recommends a preoperative resting 12-lead ECG only for patients with known CAD or other structural heart disease (except for low-risk surgical procedures) and consideration in asymptomatic patients with clinical risk factors [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Transthoracic Echocardiography:** Transthoracic echocardiography is a noninvasive method of evaluating cardiac ventricular function and valvular heart disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There is an association between decreased LV ejection fraction and incidence of MACE, with the highest-risk patients having resting LVEF <35% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The ACC/AHA endorses those with dyspnea of unknown origin and for those with current or prior heart failure with worsening dyspnea or other change in clinical status to have preoperative evaluations of left ventricular function [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Otherwise, routine preoperative evaluation of LVEF is not recommended [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Echocardiography has the added advantage of assessing valvular function, which may have important implications for noncardiac surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Aortic stenosis has been associated with a poor prognosis in noncardiac surgical patients, and knowledge of valvular lesions may modify perioperative hemodynamic goals and therapy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The 2020 ACC/AHA guideline for the management of patients with valvular disease endorses preoperative echocardiography testing for moderate or severe degrees of valvular stenosis or regurgitation with no echocardiography studies within 1 year, or worsening clinical status [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Assessment of Functional Capacity and Stress Testing for Myocardial Ischemia:** The exercise ECG stress test has been the traditional method for evaluating patients with suspected CAD [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It represents the most cost-effective and least invasive method for detecting ischemia, with a sensitivity of 70% to 80% and a specificity of 60% to 75% for identifying CAD [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A positive exercise stress test alerts the anesthesiologist that the patient is at risk for ischemia associated with increased heart rate, with the greatest risk in those who develop ischemia after only mild exercise [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. As noted earlier, exercise tolerance remains one of the most important predictors of perioperative risk for noncardiac surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Therefore, the ability to exercise without symptoms of ischemia suggests that no further testing is necessary, and exercise ECG stress testing is infrequently indicated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Noninvasive pharmacologic stress testing before surgery can be used in high-risk patients who either are unable to exercise or have contraindications to exercise (e.g., claudication) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These tests offer value in assessing risk in patients who have poor or indeterminate functional capacity (<4 METs), but should be performed only if their results will change management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Testing options include the dobutamine stress echocardiogram (DSE), in which dobutamine is used to increase myocardial oxygen demand by increasing heart rate and blood pressure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The appearance of either new or more severe regional wall motion abnormalities on the echocardiogram represents areas at risk for myocardial ischemia and is considered a positive test [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The advantage of a stress echo is that it is a dynamic assessment of ventricular function [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is generally accepted that those at greatest risk demonstrate regional wall motion abnormalities at low heart rates [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Another noninvasive stress test is dipyridamole/adenosine/regadenoson myocardial perfusion imaging (MPI) with thallium-201 and/or technetium-99m and rubidium-82 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Dipyridamole, adenosine, or regadenoson is administered as a coronary vasodilator to assess flow heterogeneity and the presence of a redistribution defect [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The 2014 ACC/AHA Clinical Practice Guideline suggests that (1) a normal DSE or MPI supports a high negative predictive value for perioperative MI and/or cardiac death and (2) moderate to large areas of ischemia are associated with increased risk of perioperative MI and/or cardiac death [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Findings of a fixed perfusion defect by MPI support a prior MI but offer limited predictive value, although this patient subset is at increased risk for long-term cardiac risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Current recommendations are that patients with active cardiac conditions such as unstable angina, congestive heart failure, significant dysrhythmias, and severe valvular disease should undergo noninvasive stress testing before noncardiac surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For patients who require vascular or other high-risk surgery, and have multiple clinical risk factors and poor functional capacity, it is reasonable to undergo noninvasive stress testing if it will change management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Routine screening with noninvasive stress testing is not recommended for patients undergoing noncardiac surgery who are at low risk for perioperative MACE [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Coronary Angiography and Revascularization:** Coronary angiography remains the best method for defining coronary anatomy and also assesses ventricular and valvular function [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hemodynamic indices can be determined, such as atrial and ventricular pressures, as well as pressure gradients across valves [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Although a critical coronary stenosis delineates an area of risk for developing myocardial ischemia, the functional response of that ischemia cannot be assessed by angiography alone [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A critical stenosis may or may not be the underlying cause for a perioperative MI that occurs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In the ambulatory population, many infarctions are the result of acute thrombosis of a noncritical stenosis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The 2014 ACC/AHA Clinical Practice Guidelines do not recommend routine preoperative coronary angiography prior to noncardiac surgery without specific clinical indications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Angiography is typically utilized in patients with signs or symptoms of unstable angina or a positive stress test, but only when the results of angiography would alter perioperative management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In patients with CAD, there are little data to support the use of coronary revascularization solely for the purpose of improving perioperative outcome, although it is true that for some patients scheduled for high-risk surgery, long-term survival may be enhanced by revascularization [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The Coronary Artery Revascularization Prophylaxis trial randomized 510 patients with CAD to undergo coronary artery revascularization prior to vascular surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. This trial failed to show a difference in the rates of perioperative MI or long-term mortality between the two groups, although it was noted that high-risk patients with left main disease, LVEF <20%, or severe aortic stenosis were excluded from the trial [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Based on this data, the ACC/AHA Clinical Practice Guidelines do not endorse routine coronary revascularization prior to noncardiac surgery for the sole purpose of reducing perioperative MACE [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. After reviewing all available data, most clinicians believe the indication for CABG prior to noncardiac surgery remains the same as in other settings and is independent of the proposed noncardiac surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Biomarker Measurement:** There is a growing body of evidence for the use of biomarkers to aid in perioperative risk assessment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Laboratory measurement of biomarkers such as brain natriuretic peptide (BNP), N-terminal brain natriuretic peptide (NT-proBNP) may increase prediction accuracy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, due to significant variations in biomarker collection, and lack of control arms, routine biomarker measurements are not currently endorsed by the AHA/ACC as part of the routine perioperative risk evaluation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Cardiac troponin is a sensitive marker of myocardial injury, and should be measured along with an ECG if the patient is displaying signs and symptoms of myocardial ischemia or infarction in the perioperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

#### 3.1.6.1. ENHANCED BIOMARKER GUIDELINES - 2022 ESC RECOMMENDATIONS [PART B/C - EVIDENCE-BASED]

**Updated Evidence for Perioperative Biomarker Screening:**

While the 2014 ACC/AHA guidelines do not endorse routine biomarker screening, the **2022 ESC Guidelines** provide more specific recommendations for selective biomarker use in high-risk patients `[PDF:12]`:

**Preoperative Troponin Screening:**
* **Class I Recommendation:** Troponin screen recommended for high-risk patients going for intermediate- and high-risk surgery `[PDF:12]`
* **High-risk patient criteria:**
  * Known CAD or history of IHD
  * Cardiovascular risk factors (HTN, DM, hyperlipidemia, smoking)
  * Age >65 years
* **Rationale:** Early detection of subclinical myocardial injury or ongoing ischemia
* **Timing:** Within days before planned surgery

**Preoperative NT-proBNP/BNP Screening:**
* **Class IIa Recommendation:** Consider BNP/NT-proBNP screen for high-risk patients (same criteria as above) going for intermediate- and high-risk surgery `[PDF:12]`
* **Evidence base:** METS study demonstrated NT-proBNP predictive value for 30-day death/MI `[PDF:7]`

**METS Study (Lancet 2018) - Key Biomarker Findings:** `[PDF:7]`

💡 **Critical Discovery:** NT-proBNP levels were **more predictive** of 30-day death/MI than subjective functional capacity assessment:
* **NT-proBNP superiority:** Higher NT-proBNP levels strongly associated with 30-day death/MI
* **Functional capacity limitation:** Patient-reported METs were NOT predictive of postoperative MI
* **DASI advantage:** Only the Duke Activity Status Index (DASI) score was predictive among subjective measures
* **CPET findings:** Reduced VO2peak (<14ml/kg/min) on CPET predictive of overall postoperative complications, but interestingly not cardiac-specific complications

**Clinical Application of Biomarkers:**

**NT-proBNP/BNP Use:**
* **Risk stratification:** Provides objective measure of cardiac stress and ventricular dysfunction
* **Cutoff values:** Elevated levels (institution-specific thresholds) warrant further evaluation
* **Clinical correlation:** Must interpret in context of renal function, age, BMI
* **Action items:** Elevated levels may prompt:
  * Cardiology consultation
  * Optimization of heart failure therapy
  * Enhanced perioperative monitoring
  * Consideration for postoperative ICU/HDU care

**Troponin Screening:**
* **Detect occult MI:** Identifies patients with recent or ongoing myocardial injury
* **Baseline measurement:** Provides reference for postoperative troponin surveillance
* **Positive screen action:**
  * Cardiology referral for further evaluation (ECG, echocardiography, stress testing)
  * Consider delaying elective surgery pending workup
  * Rule out acute coronary syndrome

**Postoperative Troponin Surveillance:**

⚠️ **2022 ESC Recommendations for High-Risk Patients:**
* **Daily troponin measurements** for 48-72 hours postoperatively
* **Target population:**
  * Patients with elevated preoperative biomarkers
  * Patients with known CAD undergoing high-risk surgery
  * Patients with RCRI ≥1 undergoing high-risk surgery
* **Detection of PMI:** Most perioperative myocardial infarctions are clinically silent
* **Early intervention:** Allows early detection and management of perioperative MI

**Comparison: 2014 ACC/AHA vs 2022 ESC Biomarker Recommendations:**

| Aspect | 2014 ACC/AHA | 2022 ESC |
|--------|--------------|----------|
| **Routine screening** | Not recommended | Not recommended |
| **Selective screening** | Limited guidance | Clear criteria (age >65, CAD, CVRFs) |
| **Troponin screening** | Only for symptoms | Class I for high-risk patients |
| **BNP screening** | Not endorsed | Class IIa for high-risk patients |
| **Postop surveillance** | Limited guidance | Daily troponin x 48-72h recommended |

💡 **Clinical Pearl:** The 2022 ESC guidelines represent a shift toward more proactive biomarker screening in selected high-risk patients, particularly for intermediate- and high-risk surgery. This aligns with METS study findings showing biomarkers outperform subjective functional assessment.

**Bottom Line:** While routine biomarker screening is not recommended, the 2022 ESC guidelines support selective use of troponin (Class I) and NT-proBNP (Class IIa) in high-risk patients (age >65, known CAD, cardiovascular risk factors) undergoing intermediate- or high-risk surgery. NT-proBNP has been shown to be more predictive of cardiac events than subjective functional capacity assessment. Postoperative troponin surveillance for 48-72 hours is recommended in high-risk patients to detect clinically silent perioperative MI.

### 3.1.7. SPECIAL PATIENT POPULATIONS

**The Elderly:** An aging population combined with advances in cardiovascular care has led to an increase in the incidence and prevalence of elderly patients presenting for noncardiac surgery with cardiovascular disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Consistently, advanced age is independently associated with an increased risk of MACE [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The Perioperative Ischemic Evaluation-2 (POISE-2) Trial showed that age over 75 years old was an independent risk factor for postoperative MI (adjusted HR [95% CI] 1.89 [1.6–2.23]; P < .001) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Elderly patients have age-related changes in cardiovascular physiology such as decreased sympathetic response to stress, reduced vascular compliance, impaired baroreceptor responses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. This may place them at risk for blood pressure lability and susceptibility to hypotension in the perioperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Elderly patients are an underrepresented population in clinical trials, and there are few guidelines which specifically address perioperative risk stratification and management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In addition to conventional outcomes, future studies should incorporate important outcomes such as functional status, maintenance of independence, and quality of life [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Patients With Coronary Artery Stents:** A history of prior MI is associated with both perioperative MI and 30-day mortality [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Recent clinical practice guidelines support delaying noncardiac surgery at least 60 days after an MI in the absence of coronary intervention [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Importantly, recent MI within 6 months of noncardiac surgery appears to be a risk factor for perioperative stroke [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Early surgery after coronary stent placement is also associated with adverse cardiac events [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Coronary stent placement within 1-year of noncardiac surgery is an independent risk factor for perioperative MACE and bleeding in patients with bare metal (BMS) or drug-eluting stents (DES) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

⚠️ The current 2014 ACC/AHA Clinical Practice Guideline supports the delay of elective noncardiac surgery for 14 days after coronary balloon angioplasty and 30 days after BMS placement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The optimal waiting period for elective noncardiac surgery after DES placement is 12 months; however, elective noncardiac surgery may be considered after 6 months based on comparative benefits of surgery compared with risk of stent thrombosis and myocardial ischemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. This difference is because the incidence of stent thrombosis for DESs has been found to be similar to that of BMSs in the early phase after placement but is less well defined over a longer period of time [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Dual antiplatelet therapy, for example, aspirin and clopidogrel, is often used after stent placement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A thienopyridine (ticlopidine or clopidogrel) is generally continued with aspirin for 1 month after BMS placement and for 12 months after DES placement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Perioperative management must weigh the risk of bleeding versus stent thrombosis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The decision must involve the anesthesiologist, surgeon, cardiologist, and intensivist [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For those patients who have a high risk for stent thrombosis, many advocate that at least aspirin be continued in the perioperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Also, the anesthesiologist must weigh the risk of regional versus general anesthesia when these patients are taking antiplatelet therapy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Surgery in patients with recent stent placement should probably only be considered in centers where interventional cardiologists are available [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

#### 3.1.7.1. COMPREHENSIVE ANTIPLATELET MANAGEMENT PROTOCOLS [PART B/C - CLINICAL GUIDELINES]

**Recommended Duration of DAPT Before Elective Non-Cardiac Surgery:**

**Evidence-Based Timing Guidelines:**
* **Balloon Angioplasty (POBA):** 2 weeks minimum `[PDF:13]`
* **Bare Metal Stent (BMS):** 1 month minimum `[PDF:13]`
* **Drug Eluting Stent (DES):** 6 months standard recommendation `[PDF:13]`
  * **Risk-benefit consideration:** 3 months if risk of delaying surgery exceeds risk of stent thrombosis
  * **2022 ESC Guidelines update:** Minimum 1 month based on data from modern DES stents `[PDF:13]`
  * Requires collaborative risk-benefit discussion between cardiologist and surgeon

⚠️ **Critical Safety Considerations:**
* Indication for stent placement may predict perioperative cardiac complications more than exact type of stent
* Recent PCI within recommended DAPT period represents one of highest risk scenarios

**Aspirin Management:**
* **General principle:** Most surgeries can be performed without stopping aspirin `[PDF:13]`
* **Exceptions requiring aspirin cessation:**
  * Intracranial neurosurgery
  * Spinal neurosurgery
  * Posterior eye surgery (vitreoretinal procedures) `[PDF:13]`
* **All other procedures:** Continue aspirin perioperatively when possible
* If withholding: 7 days preoperatively

**P2Y12 Inhibitor Cessation Timing:**
* **Ticagrelor:** 3-5 days preoperatively `[PDF:13]`
* **Clopidogrel:** 5 days preoperatively `[PDF:13]`
* **Prasugrel:** 7 days preoperatively `[PDF:13]`

**Time-Sensitive Surgery with Recent PCI:**

When surgery cannot be safely delayed and patient is within recommended DAPT period:
* **Consider platelet bridging protocols:** `[PDF:13,14]`
  * GP IIb/IIIa inhibitors (short-acting, reversible)
  * Cangrelor (ultra-short-acting P2Y12 inhibitor)
* **Multidisciplinary discussion required:** Anesthesiologist, surgeon, cardiologist, intensivist
* **Risk stratification framework:**
  * **High bleeding risk related to NCS:** Balance against
  * **High thrombotic risk:** PCI <1 month, ACS <3 months, high risk of stent thrombosis

**2022 ESC DAPT Management Algorithm:**

**For patients on dual antiplatelet therapy:** `[PDF:14]`

**Step 1 - Assess NCS-Related Bleeding Risk:**
* High bleeding risk → Proceed to thrombotic risk assessment
* Low bleeding risk → Continue DAPT through surgery

**Step 2 - Assess Thrombotic Risk:**
* **High thrombotic risk indicators:**
  * PCI <1 month
  * ACS <3 months
  * High risk of stent thrombosis
* **If high thrombotic risk AND high bleeding risk:**
  * **Option A:** Defer NCS (Class I recommendation)
  * **Option B:** Bridge with GPI or cangrelor (Class IIb)
  * **Option C:** Continue DAPT (if bleeding manageable)

**Step 3 - Antiplatelet Interruption Strategy (if proceeding):**
* **Continue aspirin (Class I)**
* **Interrupt P2Y12 inhibitor:**
  * Ticagrelor: 3-5 days
  * Clopidogrel: 5 days  
  * Prasugrel: 7 days (Class IIa)

**Regional Anesthesia Considerations:**
* **Neuraxial anesthesia:** Generally acceptable with aspirin alone
* **DAPT (aspirin + P2Y12):** Typically contraindicates neuraxial techniques
* **Individual risk-benefit analysis required:** Consider procedure-specific bleeding risks

💡 **Clinical Pearl:** For high-risk procedures in patients with recent coronary stents, strong consideration should be given to performing surgery at centers with immediate interventional cardiology backup capability.

**Patients With Cardiovascular Implantable Electronic Devices:** With the increasing prevalence of patients treated with pacemakers and implantable defibrillators, preoperative evaluation must address the management of cardiovascular implantable electronic devices (CIEDs) during the perioperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The function of these devices can be impaired by electromagnetic interference during surgery and it is imperative that the surgical team is aware of the best placement for the electrocautery pad, and understands when bipolar rather than unipolar cautery is the safest for the patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is important to understand the type of device, its programming, and its underlying clinical need [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The cardiologist often needs to be involved in programming the CIED before and after surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In 2020 the ASA published updated clinical practice guidelines for perioperative management of patients with implantable defibrillators, pacemakers, and arrhythmia monitors [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

#### 3.1.7.2. COMPREHENSIVE CIED PERIOPERATIVE MANAGEMENT [PART B/C - CLINICAL GUIDELINES]

**Main Intraoperative Risks:** `[PDF:15]`

**Electromagnetic Interference Effects:**
* **Inappropriate shocks:** EMI misinterpreted as ventricular arrhythmia
* **Inhibition of pacing:** Life-threatening in pacing-dependent patients
* **Triggering factors:**
  * Monopolar electrocautery (most common)
  * Shivering (can mimic VT/VF)
  * Succinylcholine fasciculations
  * MRI (if device not MRI-conditional)

**Device Damage Risks:**
* Lead damage from diathermy current
* Lead dislodgement during defibrillation attempts
* Lead damage during central venous catheter insertion

**Defibrillator Function Deactivation Criteria:**

⚠️ **Deactivation Required When ALL Three Present:**
1. CIED has defibrillator function (ICD or CRT-D)
2. Surgery above level of umbilicus
3. Use of monopolar diathermy `[PDF:15]`

**Deactivation Methods:**
* **ICD (no pacing function):** External magnet application `[PDF:15]`
  * Magnet suspends tachyarrhythmia detection/therapy
  * Remove magnet to reactivate defibrillator function
* **CRT-D (pacing + defibrillation):** Requires formal reprogramming `[PDF:15]`
  * Magnet causes asynchronous pacing only
  * Does NOT deactivate defibrillator function
  * **Must reprogram before surgery**

**2022 ESC CIED Recommendations:**

**Class IIa, Level C:**
* Patients with temporarily deactivated ICDs should have **continuous ECG monitoring**
* During perioperative period, accompanied by personnel skilled in early detection and treatment of arrhythmias
* High-risk patients require:
  * **Transcutaneous pacing/defibrillation pads** placed prior to NCS `[PDF:15]`
  * Immediate access to external defibrillator
  * Skilled personnel present

**Class I, Level C:**
* All patients with CIEDs that are reprogrammed before surgery should have **re-check and necessary reprogramming** as soon as possible postoperatively `[PDF:15]`

**Class IIa, Level C:**
* In high-risk CIED patients (with ICD or pacing-dependent) undergoing NCS with high probability of electromagnetic interference (e.g., unipolar electrosurgery above umbilical area):
  * **CIED check-up and necessary reprogramming** immediately before procedure should be considered `[PDF:15]`

**Preoperative CIED Assessment:**

**Essential Information to Obtain:**
* **Device type:** PPM, ICD, CRT-P, CRT-D
* **Indication:** Bradyarrhythmia, tachyarrhythmia, heart failure
* **Pacing dependency:** Assess for underlying rhythm
* **Most recent interrogation:**
  * Battery status
  * Lead function and thresholds
  * Programming parameters
  * Arrhythmia episodes

**Pacing Dependency Assessment:**
* **Clinical assessment:** Symptomatic bradyarrhythmia history
* **ECG review:** Look for pacing spikes with capture
* **Device interrogation:** Check underlying rhythm
* **If uncertain:** Assume pacing-dependent for safety

**Intraoperative Management Strategies:**

**For Pacing-Dependent Patients:**
* Consider asynchronous pacing mode (DOO/VOO)
* Have external pacing capability immediately available
* Minimize EMI exposure duration
* Use bipolar cautery when possible

**For ICD Patients with Deactivated Therapy:**
* Continuous ECG monitoring mandatory
* External defibrillator at bedside
* Transcutaneous pacing/defibrillation pads in place
* Skilled personnel present throughout

**Postoperative Management:**
* **Immediate CIED recheck:** Ensure proper function restoration
* **Reprogram as needed:** Return to optimal settings
* **Document:** Any intraoperative EMI events or device behaviors

💡 **Clinical Pearl:** Coordination between anesthesiology, surgery, and electrophysiology/cardiology is essential for safe perioperative CIED management. Early consultation and clear communication prevent complications.

**Bottom Line:** Cardiovascular evaluation should focus on identifying risk factors, assessing functional capacity, and determining appropriate testing. Use validated tools like RCRI to quantify risk. Exercise tolerance remains a critical predictor of outcomes. For patients with coronary stents, timing of surgery is crucial (at least 30 days after BMS and ideally 6-12 months after DES). Special attention should be given to elderly patients and those with cardiac implantable devices.

### 3.1.8. PREOPERATIVE OPTIMIZATION FOR IHD PATIENTS [PART B/C - EVIDENCE-BASED STRATEGIES]

**Evidence-Based Optimization Strategies:**

The preoperative period represents an opportunity to optimize patients with IHD before non-cardiac surgery. Key evidence-based strategies include `[PDF:16]`:

#### 3.1.8.1. MEDICATION OPTIMIZATION

**Beta-Blockers:**
* **Continue existing therapy:** Beta-blockers should be continued perioperatively in patients already receiving them for appropriate indications (Class I evidence) `[PDF:16]`
  * Established indications: Angina pectoris, symptomatic arrhythmias, heart failure, hypertension, post-MI
  * Continue through morning of surgery with sips of water
* **Routine initiation NOT recommended:** (Class III evidence) `[PDF:16]`
  * **POISE Trial evidence:** Initiation of perioperative beta-blockers (high-dose metoprolol begun on day of surgery) associated with:
    * ↓ Myocardial infarction and cardiovascular death
    * BUT ↑ Stroke and total mortality
    * ↑ Hypotension and bradycardia
  * **Conclusion:** Fixed, high-dose beta-blocker therapy begun on day of surgery carries more risks than benefits
* **Selective initiation:** May consider in specific patients based on individual risk-benefit analysis
  * If initiated, should ideally begin ≥2 days before surgery for optimal effect
  * Use careful dose titration to avoid excessive bradycardia or hypotension

**Statins:**
* **Continue perioperatively:** Strong evidence supports continuation of statin therapy in patients already taking them (Class I evidence) `[PDF:16]`
  * **VISION Trial:** Perioperative continuation associated with improved outcomes
  * **Benefits beyond lipid lowering:** Vasodilatory, anti-inflammatory, and antithrombotic effects
  * ⚠️ **Withdrawal risks:** Perioperative statin withdrawal increases morbidity
* **Restart early:** Should be restarted postoperatively as soon as patient can take oral medications
* **New initiation:** Consider starting in high-risk patients 30 days to 1 week before surgery when feasible (limited evidence)

**ACE Inhibitors/ARBs:**
* See comprehensive management guidelines in section 1.7.4
* Decision to continue or withhold should be individualized based on patient and surgical factors

#### 3.1.8.2. CARDIOVASCULAR RISK FACTOR OPTIMIZATION

**Optimize Control of Comorbidities:** `[PDF:16]`
* **Hypertension:**
  * Target BP <130/80 mmHg when possible
  * Balance optimization against surgical timeline
  * See section 3.1.3 for BP thresholds and perioperative implications
* **Hyperlipidemia:**
  * Ensure compliance with statin therapy
  * LDL targets per cardiology guidelines
* **Diabetes Mellitus:**
  * Target HbA1c optimization (generally <8% for most surgical patients)
  * Balance glycemic control against risk of hypoglycemia
  * Ensure appropriate perioperative glucose management plan
  * See section 3.3.2 for detailed DM management

#### 3.1.8.3. SMOKING CESSATION [PART B/C - EVIDENCE-BASED TIMING]

**Optimal Timing and Benefits:** `[PDF:16]`
* **Ideal preoperative cessation:** >4 weeks before elective non-cardiac surgery (Class I evidence)
  * ↓ Postoperative complications
  * ↓ Mortality
  * Mechanism: Time required for physiological recovery
* **Benefits of smoking cessation:**
  * **Oxygen carrying capacity:** Improved within 12-24 hours (COHb clearance)
  * **Wound healing:** Improved tissue oxygenation and collagen synthesis
  * **Reduced infections:** Enhanced immune function
  * **Cardiovascular benefits:** ↓ Sympathomimetic effects of nicotine
  * **Pulmonary benefits:** Improved mucociliary clearance, ↓ secretions

**Time-Sensitive Surgery:**
* **Any duration better than none:** For time-sensitive surgery when 4-week delay not feasible, any duration of preoperative cessation provides some benefit `[PDF:16]`
* **Even 24 hours:** Improves oxygen-carrying capacity
* **Smoking cessation support:**
  * Provide counseling and resources
  * Consider nicotine replacement therapy
  * Involve smoking cessation programs when available

**See also:** Section 5.1 for comprehensive smoking cessation strategies

#### 3.1.8.4. ANEMIA MANAGEMENT [PART B/C - IHD-SPECIFIC TARGETS]

**Hemoglobin Targets for IHD Patients:** `[PDF:16]`
* **Keep Hb >8 g/dL** for patients with ischemic heart disease
  * Higher threshold than general population (Hb >7 g/dL)
  * Rationale: Maintain adequate myocardial oxygen delivery
* **Preoperative anemia workup:**
  * Check hematinics: Iron studies (ferritin, TSAT, TIBC), B12, folate
  * Identify and treat underlying cause when possible
  * Review for occult blood loss, chronic disease, nutritional deficiency

**Iron Supplementation:**
* **Intravenous iron:** Consider if time allows `[PDF:16]`
  * **Minimum duration:** Needs minimum 2 weeks for >1 g/dL ↑Hb
  * **Advantages over oral:**
    * Faster response
    * Better absorption
    * Higher dose tolerance
  * **Indications:**
    * Iron deficiency anemia
    * Functional iron deficiency (chronic inflammation)
    * Intolerance to oral iron
* **Oral iron:** May use if >4 weeks before surgery
  * Slower response, limited by absorption and GI tolerance

**Blood Management Strategies:**
* **Preoperative optimization:** Address anemia before surgery when time permits
* **Intraoperative management:** Liberal transfusion threshold (Hb >8 g/dL) for IHD patients
* **Postoperative monitoring:** Continue monitoring Hb and symptoms

#### 3.1.8.5. CORONARY REVASCULARIZATION - EVIDENCE AGAINST ROUTINE USE

**No Evidence for Routine Preoperative Coronary Revascularization:** `[PDF:16]`

* **CARP Trial (Coronary Artery Revascularization Prophylaxis):** `[PDF:16]`
  * **Study design:** Randomized 510 patients with CAD to coronary artery revascularization vs. no revascularization before vascular surgery
  * **Results:** NO difference in:
    * Rates of perioperative MI
    * Long-term mortality
  * **Excluded:** High-risk patients with left main disease, LVEF <20%, severe aortic stenosis
  * **Conclusion:** Routine preoperative revascularization not beneficial for most patients

* **ACC/AHA Guideline Recommendation:** (Class III evidence)
  * Do NOT perform routine coronary revascularization prior to non-cardiac surgery solely for purpose of reducing perioperative MACE `[PDF:16]`
  * **Indications for revascularization remain the same** as in non-surgical settings:
    * Left main disease
    * Severe multi-vessel disease with reduced LVEF
    * Unstable/refractory angina
    * High-risk stress test findings
  * Indication for CABG/PCI is independent of the proposed non-cardiac surgery

**Clinical Implications:**
* Do not delay necessary non-cardiac surgery to pursue prophylactic revascularization
* Optimize medical management instead
* If revascularization indicated for standard cardiac reasons, must then delay non-cardiac surgery appropriately (see section 3.1.7.1 for stent timing)

#### 3.1.8.6. INTEGRATED OPTIMIZATION CHECKLIST FOR IHD PATIENTS

**Preoperative Optimization Checklist:**

□ **Medications:**
  * Continue beta-blockers (do not initiate routinely)
  * Continue statins
  * Individualize ACE-I/ARB management
  * Continue antiplatelet therapy per guidelines (section 3.1.7.1)

□ **Risk Factors:**
  * Optimize BP control (target <130/80)
  * Optimize glycemic control in diabetics
  * Address hyperlipidemia

□ **Smoking Cessation:**
  * Ideally >4 weeks before surgery
  * Any duration if time-limited

□ **Anemia:**
  * Target Hb >8 g/dL
  * Check hematinics
  * Consider IV iron if time permits (≥2 weeks)

□ **Cardiac Evaluation:**
  * Rule out active/unstable cardiac conditions
  * Assess functional capacity
  * Appropriate cardiac testing based on risk (see section 3.1.6)

□ **Revascularization:**
  * NOT indicated solely for perioperative risk reduction
  * Only if standard cardiac indications present

**Bottom Line:** Preoperative optimization for IHD patients should focus on continuing existing beta-blockers and statins (NOT initiating new beta-blockers), optimizing cardiovascular risk factors, achieving smoking cessation >4 weeks when possible (any duration better than none), correcting anemia to Hb >8 g/dL with IV iron if time permits, and recognizing that routine prophylactic coronary revascularization is not indicated (CARP trial). Medical optimization is preferred over procedural intervention for perioperative risk reduction.

### 3.1.9. INTRAOPERATIVE MANAGEMENT OF IHD PATIENTS [PART B/C - PRACTICAL STRATEGIES]

**Comprehensive Intraoperative Care Framework:**
