The intraoperative period for IHD patients requires vigilant monitoring, proactive prevention of myocardial ischemia, and prompt treatment of hemodynamic derangements `[PDF:19-21]`.

#### 3.1.9.1. ISCHEMIA DETECTION AND MONITORING [PART B - CLINICAL APPROACH]

**1. Continuous ECG Monitoring with Computerised ST Segment Analysis:** `[PDF:19]`

**Standard 3-Lead ECG Configuration:**
* **Leads monitored:** Lead I, II, or III
* **Sensitivity for ischemia detection:** Only **33%**
* **Primary utility:** Sensitive for atrial arrhythmias
* **Limitation:** Inadequate for comprehensive ischemia monitoring

**CM5 Configuration (Modified Lead V5):**
* **Electrode placement:**
  * Right arm electrode: Suprasternal notch
  * Left arm electrode: Cardiac apex (V5 position)
  * Left leg electrode: Standard position
* **Ischemia detection:** **>80%** compared to standard 3-lead ECG
* **Advantage:** Simple modification with significantly improved sensitivity

**5-Lead ECG (RECOMMENDED FOR IHD PATIENTS):** `[PDF:19]`
* **Configuration:** Allows monitoring of all limb leads PLUS any one precordial lead
* **Ischemia Detection Rates:**
  * **Lead II + V4:** 82% detection
  * **Lead II + V5:** 80% detection
  * **Lead II + V4 + V5:** **95% detection** (OPTIMAL)
* **Clinical Recommendation:** Monitor leads II, V4, and V5 simultaneously for maximum sensitivity

**ST Segment Criteria for Myocardial Ischemia:** `[PDF:19]`

⚠️ **Diagnostic Cutoff Values:**
* **ST Depression (Subendocardial Ischemia):**
  * **>1mm (0.1mV)** horizontal or downsloping depression, OR
  * **>0.5mm** depression in 2 contiguous leads
* **ST Elevation (Transmural Injury):**
  * **>1mm elevation** in 2 contiguous leads
  * **Exception:** Leads V2-V3 require **>2mm elevation** for significance

**Computerised ST Segment Monitoring:**
* Provides continuous, automated ST trend analysis
* Alerts to significant ST changes
* Documents ischemic burden throughout case
* Most modern monitors have this capability - ensure it is activated

#### 3.1.9.2. ISCHEMIA PREVENTION STRATEGIES [PART B/C - MYOCARDIAL OXYGEN BALANCE]

**Fundamental Principle: Optimize Myocardial O2 Supply vs Demand** `[PDF:19]`

**Optimize Myocardial Oxygen SUPPLY:** `[PDF:19]`
* **Oxygenation/Ventilation:**
  * Maintain adequate FiO2 and avoid hypoxemia
  * Ensure adequate minute ventilation (avoid hypercapnia which increases PVR)
  * Use appropriate PEEP to prevent atelectasis
* **Blood Management:**
  * Maintain Hb >8 g/dL (liberal transfusion threshold for IHD)
  * Prompt treatment of surgical bleeding
  * Ensure adequate preload/intravascular volume
* **Coronary Perfusion:**
  * **Avoid tachycardia:** Tachycardia reduces diastolic filling time (when coronary perfusion occurs)
  * **Maintain diastolic BP:** Coronary perfusion pressure = DBP - LVEDP
  * Target heart rate <100 bpm when possible

**Minimize Myocardial Oxygen DEMAND:** `[PDF:19]`
* **Prevent Hemodynamic Surges:**
  * **Intubation:** Cardiostable induction, blunt hypertensive response
    * Consider: IV lignocaine, remifentanil, esmolol, adequate depth
  * **Extubation:** Ensure adequate analgesia, consider beta-blockade
  * **Surgical stimulation:** Maintain adequate depth and analgesia throughout
* **Beat-to-Beat BP Monitoring:**
  * Pre-induction arterial line for high-risk patients
  * Allows immediate detection and treatment of hemodynamic changes
* **Anticipate Physiologic Perturbations:**
  * **Pneumoperitoneum:** ↑SVR, ↑preload, ↓venous return
  * **Trendelenburg:** ↑preload, ↑ICP, respiratory mechanics changes
  * **Positioning:** Beach chair, prone, anti-Trendelenburg all affect hemodynamics
* **Perioperative Analgesia:**
  * Good pain control reduces stress response and catecholamine surge
  * Consider multimodal analgesia
  * Neuraxial/regional techniques when appropriate
* **Maintain Electrolytes:**
  * Avoid tachyarrhythmias secondary to electrolyte derangements
  * Monitor and correct K, Mg, Ca as needed

**Avoid Prolonged Hypotension:** `[PDF:19]`
* **Definition:** >20% decrease from baseline OR MAP <60-70mmHg for >10 minutes
* **Mechanism:** Compromises coronary perfusion, worsens supply-demand mismatch
* **Prevention:** Depth of anaesthesia monitoring to avoid excessive depth-related hypotension

#### 3.1.9.3. ISCHEMIA TREATMENT PROTOCOLS [PART B/C - SYSTEMATIC APPROACH]

**Immediate Assessment and Treatment:** `[PDF:19]`

**Step 1: ABC Approach**
* **Airway/Breathing:** Increase FiO2 to 100%
* **Circulation:** Assess hemodynamics and treat underlying cause

**Step 2: Treat Hypotension** `[PDF:19]`
* **Assess cause:** Hypovolemia, excessive anesthetic depth, vasodilation, cardiac dysfunction
* **Treatment options:**
  * **Fluid bolus:** 250-500 mL crystalloid if volume-responsive
  * **Vasopressors:** Phenylephrine, norepinephrine (maintain SVR)
  * **Lighten anesthetic depth:** Reduce volatile agent concentration
  * **Blood transfusion:** If anemic and ongoing blood loss

**Step 3: Treat Hypertension/Tachycardia** `[PDF:19]`
* **Ensure adequate depth:** May need to increase volatile/propofol
* **Ensure adequate analgesia:** Consider opioid bolus or infusion
* **Stop surgical stimulation:** Communicate with surgeon
* **Short-acting beta-blockers:** Esmolol 10-20mg IV boluses
  * Onset: 1 minute
  * Half-life: 10 minutes
  * Ideal for acute intraoperative control
* **Consider GTN:** Nitroglycerin infusion for coronary vasodilation (reduce preload and afterload)

**Step 4: Monitor Surgical Field**
* Communicate with surgical team
* Assess for ongoing blood loss or surgical complications

**Step 5: Treat Tachyarrhythmias**
* Follow ACLS protocols as appropriate
* Maintain electrolyte balance

#### 3.1.9.4. BLOOD PRESSURE AND FLUID MANAGEMENT [PART B/C - HEMODYNAMIC TARGETS]

**Blood Pressure Management:** `[PDF:20]`

**Target Parameters:**
* **Keep BP within 20% of baseline**
* **MAP >65-70 mmHg** (higher targets for chronic hypertensives - consider MAP >70-80)
* **Avoid extremes:** Both hypotension and severe hypertension compromise myocardial O2 balance

**Beat-to-Beat Monitoring:**
* **Pre-induction arterial line** for high-risk patients `[PDF:20]`
  * Allows immediate detection of BP changes
  * Facilitates tight BP control
  * Permits frequent blood gas sampling if needed

**Anticipate and Treat Hemodynamic Surges:**
* **During intubation:** Have vasopressor ready, ensure adequate depth
* **During extubation:** Adequate analgesia, consider prophylactic beta-blockade
* **During pneumoperitoneum insufflation:** Anticipate ↑SVR and ↑BP
* **During Trendelenburg positioning:** Anticipate ↑preload

**Standby Medications:** `[PDF:20]`
* **Short-acting vasopressors:**
  * Phenylephrine 50-100 mcg boluses
  * Norepinephrine 2-10 mcg/min infusion for sustained support
* **Short-acting beta-blockers:**
  * Esmolol 10-20mg boluses (onset 1 min, half-life 10 min) `[PDF:21]`
  * Ideal for acute hypertension/tachycardia

**Fluid Management Principles:** `[PDF:20]`

**Preoperative Fluid Status:**
* Ensure adequately fluid resuscitated before induction
* Avoid hypovolemia which exacerbates induction hypotension
* Balance against avoiding fluid overload (especially if heart failure)

**Intraoperative Fluid Therapy:**
* **Maintenance fluids:** Holliday-Segar formula (4-2-1 rule)
* **Insensible losses:**
  * Normal: 0.5 mL/kg/hr
  * Open abdomen: 1-5 mL/kg/hr
* **Replace NGT losses and blood loss**

**Goal-Directed Fluid Therapy:** `[PDF:20]`
* **PPV (Pulse Pressure Variation):** Target <13%
  * ⚠️ **Caution:** Less sensitive during pneumoperitoneum (increased intrathoracic pressure)
  * Requires controlled ventilation, sinus rhythm, tidal volume ≥8 mL/kg
* **Urine Output:** Target >0.5-1 mL/kg/hr
  * May be lower during pneumoperitoneum (increased IAP reduces renal perfusion)
* **Stroke Volume Variation (SVV):** If advanced monitoring available

**Avoid Excessive Crystalloid:** `[PDF:20]`
* **Rationale:**
  * Trendelenburg positioning increases risk of cerebral and pulmonary edema
  * Avoid gut edema (especially for GI surgery)
  * Minimize third-spacing
* **Balanced approach:** Use goal-directed therapy rather than fixed-volume protocols

#### 3.1.9.5. ANAESTHESIA TECHNIQUE CONSIDERATIONS [PART B/C - PRACTICAL APPROACH]

**Premedication:** `[PDF:21]`
* **Consider anxiolytic premedication cautiously**
  * May help reduce preoperative stress and catecholamine surge
  * Balance against respiratory depression risk
  * Lower doses in elderly or frail patients

**Induction:** `[PDF:21]`
* **Preoperative fluid resuscitation:** Ensure adequate intravascular volume
* **Pre-induction arterial line:** For beat-to-beat monitoring
* **Standby vasopressors:** Drawn up and ready
* **Cardiostable induction technique:**
  * **Options:** Midazolam co-induction, high-dose opioid technique, etomidate
  * ⚠️ **Avoid ketamine:** Sympathomimetic effects increase myocardial O2 demand
  * Slow titration to effect (avoid large boluses of propofol)

**Intubation:** `[PDF:21]`
* **Attenuate stress response to laryngoscopy:**
  * **IV lignocaine:** 1.5 mg/kg 2-3 minutes before intubation
  * **Topical lignocaine:** Spray to oropharynx and vocal cords
  * **Remifentanil:** Effect-site TCI or bolus dosing
  * **Esmolol:** 10-20mg if tachycardic

**Maintenance:** `[PDF:21]`
* **Adequate Depth of Anaesthesia:**
  * Prevent awareness
  * Prevent hypertensive surges from inadequate depth
  * Avoid excessive depth causing hypotension
* **Depth of Anaesthesia Monitoring:**
  * BIS/entropy monitoring to guide anesthetic depth `[PDF:21]`
  * Target: BIS 40-60 for general anesthesia
  * Helps avoid hypotension from excessive depth
* **Good Perioperative Analgesia:**
  * Multimodal approach
  * **Avoid NSAIDs** in patients with renal impairment or on antiplatelet therapy
  * Consider neuraxial/regional techniques when anticoagulation status permits
* **Volatile vs TIVA:**
  * **Theoretical advantage of volatile agents:** Ischemic preconditioning effect `[PDF:21]`
  * Both techniques acceptable - choose based on patient factors and clinical situation

**Ventilation:** `[PDF:21]`
* **Maintain adequate ventilation and oxygenation**
* **PEEP:** Use to prevent atelectasis, optimize oxygenation
* **Increase minute ventilation:** To avoid hypercapnia
  * Hypercapnia causes sympathetic stimulation and ↑PVR
  * Important during pneumoperitoneum (increased CO2 absorption)

**Extubation:** `[PDF:21]`
* **Administer reversal agent slowly:** Avoid tachycardia from anticholinergic component
* **Smooth extubation:**
  * **Adequate analgesia:** Ensure pain well-controlled before emergence
  * **Standby short-acting beta-blockers:** Esmolol 10-20mg for hypertension/tachycardia
  * Deep extubation technique may be considered in selected patients
* **Standby esmolol dosing:** 10-20mg boluses (onset 1 min, half-life 10 mins) `[PDF:21]`

**Postoperative Disposition:** `[PDF:21]`
* **SHD/ICU for hemodynamic monitoring:** For high-risk patients
  * Recent MI, severe LV dysfunction, extensive surgery, intraoperative instability
* **Avoid postoperative complications:**
  * **Pain:** Adequate analgesia reduces stress response
  * **Hypothermia:** Maintain normothermia (shivering increases O2 demand)
  * **Hypotension:** Continue hemodynamic monitoring and support
  * **Anemia:** Monitor Hb, transfuse if <8 g/dL or symptomatic

**Bottom Line:** Intraoperative management of IHD patients requires meticulous attention to myocardial oxygen supply-demand balance. Use 5-lead ECG (II, V4, V5) for 95% ischemia detection sensitivity. Prevent ischemia by avoiding tachycardia, maintaining Hb >8 g/dL, and keeping BP within 20% baseline (MAP >70). Employ cardiostable induction techniques, depth monitoring to avoid excessive hypotension, goal-directed fluid therapy, and prepare vasopressors and esmolol for rapid treatment of hemodynamic derangements. Smooth extubation with adequate analgesia and standby beta-blockade is essential.

## 3.2. PULMONARY DISEASE

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|ⓘ|Part A: Respiratory physiology]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to respiratory disease]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Perioperative management strategies]]

**Exam Weighting:** High (Common comorbidity)

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CarePostoperative respiratory complications defined by the European Perioperative Clinical Outcome consensus statement included (1) respiratory infection, (2) respiratory failure, (3) pleural effusion, (4) atelectasis, (5) pneumothorax, (6) bronchospasm, and (7) aspiration pneumonitis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. During the perioperative period postoperative respiratory complications occur more frequently than cardiac complications in patients having major noncardiac surgeries [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These are major causes of morbidity and mortality with increased length of hospital stay and substantial economic cost [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The incidence of postoperative respiratory failure, defined as greater or equal to 48 hours of mechanical ventilation or reintubation after surgery, has been estimated at 3% in vascular and general surgery patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Notably, the risk of mortality with respiratory failure appears substantially higher than the risk after a perioperative MI [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Analyses of large clinical databases have substantially increased the understanding of clinical risk factors [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Clinical guidelines from the American College of Physicians have estimated preoperative risk and recommend prevention strategies [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Preoperative pulmonary function testing and chest radiographs, although important diagnostic tools, appear to offer limited benefit in predicting perioperative respiratory failure and complication rates [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Although chest x-rays identify structural lung abnormalities, these are not frequently associated with significant changes in management for the general population [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In contrast, laboratory studies identifying a reduction in serum albumin levels and increased levels of blood urea nitrogen (BUN) appear associated with an increased risk of perioperative pulmonary morbidity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Epidemiologic studies support the relationship of the anatomic location of surgery and pulmonary risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Open aortic, thoracic, and upper abdominal surgeries are associated with the highest risk for postoperative pulmonary morbidity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Furthermore, cranial procedures, vascular, and neck surgeries are associated with increased risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The pathophysiology of decreased postoperative vital capacity and functional residual capacity with diaphragmatic dysfunction contribute to hypoxemia and atelectasis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Changes in functional residual capacity may take weeks to return to baseline [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Diaphragmatic dysfunction can develop despite adequate analgesia related to phrenic nerve dysfunction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Neurosurgery and neck surgery are associated with perioperative aspiration pneumonia, related to an altered sensorium or cranial nerve dysfunction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Emergency surgery and general anesthesia are consistent factors associated with increased risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Not only can the surgery affect pulmonary function, but general anesthesia also results in mechanical changes, such as a decrease in the functional residual capacity and reduced diaphragmatic function, leading to ventilation/perfusion abnormalities and atelectasis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Volatile anesthetics inhibit mucociliary clearance, can increase alveolar–capillary permeability, inhibit surfactant production, increase nitric oxide synthetase, and increase sensitivity of the pulmonary vasculature to neurohumoral mediators [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Subanesthetic levels of intravenous or volatile agents can blunt ventilatory response to hypoxemia and hypercarbia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Prolonged anesthesia is a well-established risk factor for postoperative pulmonary complications with morbidity rates increasing after 2 to 3 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, laparoscopic surgery, often longer in duration, is associated with decreased pulmonary complications compared with an open procedure despite increased anesthesia duration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

ASA class 3 or greater, emergency surgery, high-risk surgical procedure (vascular, transplant, neurosurgery, thoracic, general, and burn surgery), history of congestive heart failure, and chronic pulmonary disease are the most common independent predictors for reintubation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The preoperative evaluation can identify pre-existing pulmonary disease, with a goal to maximize the patient's health status by working with the patient and consultants [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Collaboration with the surgeon can support specific risk reduction strategies, such as operative approach, epidural or regional analgesia when appropriate, lung expansion methods, and deep venous thrombosis prophylaxis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Intraoperative measures to limit the risk of hospital-acquired pneumonia have been proposed, largely focused on reducing the risk of bacterial contamination of the lung during the perioperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For high-risk patient groups, studies support preoperative oral antiseptic decontamination before tracheal intubation to decrease the risk of nosocomial pneumonia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 3.2.1. SMOKING

Smoking is an important risk factor for perioperative respiratory complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Smokers without chronic lung disease show increased carboxyhemoglobin levels, decreased tracheal ciliary function, and increased sputum production, with nicotine-induced stimulation of the cardiovascular system [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Although cessation of smoking for 2 days can decrease carboxyhemoglobin levels, abolish nicotine effects, and improve mucous clearance, smoking cessation for 4 to 8 weeks appears necessary to reduce the rate of postoperative pulmonary complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Nicotine transdermal patches and nicotine e-cigarettes during the perioperative period remain controversial [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Patients who smoke can show increased airway reactivity under general anesthesia and may benefit from preoperative inhaled bronchodilators [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Recently, vaping has become popular among young adults [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. While no large studies are available at this time, there are reports of increased airway reactivity and complications under anesthesia due to pulmonary interstitial changes from e-cigarette use [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 3.2.2. ASTHMA

Asthma is one of the most common coexisting diseases that confront the anesthesiologist [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The patient interview can elicit information regarding inciting factors, severity, reversibility, and current status [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Frequent use of bronchodilators, hospitalizations for asthma, and requirement for systemic steroids are all indicators of more severe disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. After an acute exacerbation of asthma, airway hyperreactivity may persist for several weeks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In addition to bronchodilators, perioperative steroids may benefit the severe asthmatic [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Steroid-induced adrenal insufficiency is a concern in patients who have received more than a "burst and taper" of steroids in the previous 6 months [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. This group of patients should be considered for "stress doses" of steroids perioperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The complication rate for asthmatics treated with short-term steroids undergoing surgery is low, with no evidence for impaired wound healing or infections [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Patients using chronic inhaled steroids should continue regular use during the perioperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 3.2.3. OBSTRUCTIVE SLEEP APNEA (OSA)

OSA is a syndrome defined by periodic obstruction of the upper airway during sleep, leading to episodic oxygen desaturation and hypercarbia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. This episodic desaturation, in turn, causes episodic sleep arousal, leads to chronic sleep deprivation with daytime hypersomnolence and behavioral changes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It can result in chronic pulmonary hypertension and right heart failure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It afflicts 26% of U.S. adults between 30 and 70 years of age, exacerbated by the obesity epidemic [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Because of airway collapse and sleep deprivation, patients with OSA are susceptible to respiratory depression and airway obstruction after sedatives, opioids, and inhaled anesthetics both intraoperatively and postoperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

In 2014, the ASA published practice guidelines for the perioperative management of patients with OSA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Preoperative identification of those patients at risk is critical to formulate a safe perioperative plan [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Preoperative polysomnography is the standard diagnostic method for OSA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For the preoperative assessment, screening questionnaires can support the diagnosis of OSA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, the STOP-Bang questionnaire (snoring, tiredness, observed apnea, blood pressure, body mass index, age, neck size, gender) accurately identifies moderate to severe OSA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Use of the STOP-Bang Questionnaire to Identify Surgical Patients at Risk for OSA:**
* **S** – Snore loudly
* **T** – Tired in the daytime
* **O** – Observed apnea, choking/gasping during sleep
* **P** – high blood Pressure
* **B** – BMI > 35 kg/m²
* **A** – Age > 50 yr
* **N** – Neck size large (Male: >17 in; Female >16 in)
* **G** – Gender male
* 3–4 positive answers = intermediate risk for moderate to severe OSA
* > 5 positive answers = high risk for moderate to severe OSA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

The risk of perioperative complications in patients with OSA increases with the severity of sleep apnea, the invasiveness of surgery, and required postoperative opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There is general consensus that preoperative initiation of nasal mask continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) reduces perioperative risk, by decreasing sleep deprivation and secondary hypersomnolence [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Importantly, OSA is associated with difficult airway management, making it even more important to examine previous anesthesia records and to perform a thorough airway examination [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Emergency airway equipment should be readily available at the surgical center [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

There are multiple management decisions to make in coordination with the surgeon with respect to the OSA patient: (1) consider a less invasive procedure to limit opioid need, (2) evaluate the feasibility of neuraxial, regional, or local anesthesia, decreasing the total amount of anesthesia or opioids, (3) review the benefit to risks of nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory agents, (4) evaluate safety of outpatient surgery, (5) plan for perioperative CPAP support, and (6) plan for postoperative ICU or monitored unit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The ASA practice guidelines for OSA recommend hospitalization after uvulopalatoplasty surgery and after tonsillectomy for OSA in children younger than 3 years [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Postoperative hospitalization is also recommended for those OSA patients with other coexisting diseases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Pulmonary risk assessment should focus on identifying patients with chronic lung disease, asthma, or OSA. For smokers, encourage cessation ideally 4-8 weeks preoperatively. For asthma, evaluate severity, control status, and medication requirements; consider perioperative steroids for severe asthmatics. For OSA, use screening tools like STOP-Bang, plan for continued CPAP use, consider regional anesthesia techniques, minimize opioids, and evaluate need for postoperative monitoring.

## 3.3. ENDOCRINE DISEASE

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|ⓘ|Part A: Endocrine physiology]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to endocrine disorders]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Perioperative management strategies]]

**Exam Weighting:** High (Common comorbidity)

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient Care### 3.3.2. DIABETES MELLITUS

Diabetes mellitus is the most common endocrinopathy, and according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). Americans have an incidence of approximately 11% (>20% in those 65 years and older), with the highest rates in Native Americans, followed by Hispanics, Blacks, Asian Americans, then Whites [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The incidence is expected to rise significantly for Americans born after 2000, largely because of the rise in obesity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Critical illness–induced hyperglycemia, defined as a blood glucose above 200 mg/dL in the absence of known diabetes, occurs most frequently in the elderly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Diabetes mellitus has acute and chronic disease manifestations, making it more likely for diabetics to require surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The majority of diabetics develop secondary disease in one or more organ systems, which must be identified preoperatively so that an appropriate plan can be developed for perioperative management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Although long-term close control of glucose may limit some of the microvascular effects of diabetes (retinopathy, neuropathy, and nephropathy), macrovascular events such as myocardial ischemia, MI, or stroke may not be decreased [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Diabetics have an increased risk of CAD, hypertension, congestive heart failure, and perioperative MI, with the incidence of silent ischemia increased due to associated autonomic neuropathy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The 2014 ACC/AHA Clinical Practice Guideline places diabetics, especially those receiving insulin, at an elevated risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Diabetics are also more likely than the general population to have cerebral vascular, peripheral vascular, and renal vascular disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Diabetes mellitus is the leading cause of renal failure requiring dialysis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Peripheral neuropathies and vascular disease make these patients more susceptible to positioning injuries both during and after surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Autonomic neuropathy may predispose the patient to hemodynamic instability during anesthesia and theoretically increase the risk of pulmonary aspiration because of the associated gastroparesis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These deficits should be documented prior to anesthesia and the anesthetic plan adjusted accordingly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Stiff joint syndrome due to glycosylation of proteins and abnormal collagen cross-linking may significantly affect the temporomandibular, atlanto-occipital, and cervical spine joints in patients with longstanding type 1 diabetes, resulting in difficulty with tracheal intubation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A thorough airway examination should be performed prior to anesthesia and a high index of suspicion maintained for a potentially difficult airway [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Some suggest using the "prayer sign" as an additional evaluation tool; patients who are unable to completely oppose their hands (with no space between) should be suspected of also having changes in other joints potentially impacting airway manipulation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Regimens for perioperative glycemic control vary enormously, not only between type 1 and type 2 diabetics but also within each group [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Patients with type 1 diabetes have an absolute insulin deficiency usually due to destruction of pancreatic β-cells [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These patients must receive insulin to prevent diabetic ketoacidosis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Home glucose management most often relies on some combination of short, intermediate, and long-acting insulin regimens [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Insulin pumps are increasingly common and are used to administer a continuous subcutaneous infusion of short-acting insulin, supplemented by boluses dictated by glucose levels, diet, and exercise [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Type 2 diabetes accounts for the great majority of diabetics and is defined by variable degrees of insulin deficiency and resistance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Although most commonly associated with obesity, it may also be induced by corticosteroids or pregnancy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Ketoacidosis is uncommon in type 2 diabetics and the stress of severe infection or illness is more likely to provoke a nonketotic hyperosmolar state, which is characterized by severe dehydration, hyperglycemia, and hyperosmolarity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In type 2 diabetics, glucose control is most commonly achieved with diet, exercise, and oral hypoglycemic drugs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These agents primarily work by increasing endogenous insulin release, increasing insulin sensitivity, and/or decreasing hepatic gluconeogenesis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These drugs fall under the main categories of sulfonylureas, biguanides, thiazolidinediones, and meglitinides [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. If glycemic control is unsuccessful, then insulin is generally added to the regimen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Ideally, both types 1 and 2 diabetic patients should be evaluated in the preoperative clinic as well as by the patient's endocrinologist 1 to 2 weeks before elective surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Questions should address the type, dose, and time of antidiabetic therapy as well as the frequency and manifestations of hypoglycemia and level at which symptoms occur [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

In addition to a thorough history and physical examination, a judicious laboratory investigation should include determination of blood glucose, hemoglobin A1c, serum electrolytes, creatinine, and an ECG [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. If the results reveal inadequate glycemic control based on a hemoglobin A1c above the target range (<8% for type 1 diabetics and <7% for type 2 diabetics, as recommended by the American Diabetic Association), abnormal electrolytes, or ketonuria, then elective surgery should be delayed to allow optimization of preoperative glycemic control [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Of note, there is no consensus in the literature determining an HbA1c indicative of adequate glycemic control for pediatric patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Initiation of β-blockers prior to the day of surgery should be considered in diabetic patients with at least two other risk factors for an adverse cardiac event, as there is no evidence that β-blockers will worsen glucose intolerance or mask hypoglycemic symptoms [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Perioperative Glucose Management:**
Anesthesia and surgery interrupt the regular meal and insulin administration schedules of patients with diabetes mellitus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Perioperative stress may increase serum glucose concentrations secondary to the release of cortisol and catecholamines [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The majority of available literature suggests that better glycemic control may limit morbidity (length of hospital/intensive care unit stay, infection rate, wound healing, outcomes after strokes/MIs) and mortality, particularly in cardiac surgery patients, carotid endarterectomy patients, and the critically ill [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is hypothesized that elevated blood glucose (BG) levels impair neutrophil function and cause an overproduction of reactive oxygen species, free fatty acids (FFAs), and inflammatory mediators [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These pathophysiologic changes contribute to direct cellular damage and vascular and immune dysfunction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

More studies are needed to more closely define the target level for glucose control, and societies do vary on their recommendations [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There is general consensus that an attempt should be made to control the upper limit of glucose to less than 200 mg/dL, although some will argue that even tighter control is warranted [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, the Society for Ambulatory Anesthesia recommends glucose levels <180 mg/dL, the American Diabetic Association recommends 140 to 180 mg/dL, the Society of Critical Care Medicine recommends <150 mg/dL, and the American College of Physicians recommends 140 to 200 mg/dL [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Guide for Perioperative Glucose Control:**
The following recommendations can serve as a general guide [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]:

* Plan with the surgeon to schedule the surgery as the first case of the day to prevent prolonged fasting [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* As a general rule, oral hypoglycemic agents are held on the day of surgery to avoid reactive hypoglycemia until oral intake is restarted [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Insulin therapy should balance adequate glucose control with the avoidance of hypoglycemia. Insulin is usually continued through the evening before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Schedule the patient to arrive in the early morning and check blood glucose on arrival and every hour if delayed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* If patients develop symptoms or measurable hypoglycemia, they should be counseled to take a glucose tablet or clear juice if an IV is not in place to deliver a fluid with glucose [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Type 1 diabetics should be continued on basal insulin administration even during preoperative fasting to prevent ketoacidosis. Hold the usual dose of rapidor short-acting insulin, unless patient has hyperglycemia that requires correction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Use the patient's own sliding scale to administer short-acting insulin subcutaneously prior to the scheduled surgery and during short operations [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* Patients on insulin pumps may be managed by continuing the pump for short operations or changing over to an intravenous insulin infusion for longer or major operations [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

This strategy, along with blood glucose determinations every 1 to 2 hours, may be all that is necessary for well-controlled diabetics undergoing short, noninvasive outpatient operations [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In addition, it is important to prevent postoperative nausea and vomiting and to encourage the early resumption of diet, allowing return to their previous insulin regimen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For type 1 or 2 diabetics undergoing longer or major surgery, consultation with the patient's endocrinologist is recommended as insulin is generally administered in the form of an intravenous infusion of regular insulin rather than continuing a long-acting subcutaneous insulin or insulin pump [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There are several methods of administering an insulin infusion, none of which has proved superior [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Concurrent separate infusions of insulin and glucose are more easily adjusted and may provide better glycemic control than combined glucose/insulin/potassium infusions [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. To increase safety, the insulin infusion is added via a side port to the same line delivering the glucose infusion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A separate non glucose isotonic solution should be used to replace deficits and intraoperative fluid losses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. All protocols rely on the frequent determination of a plasma glucose level at least every 1 to 2 hours to allow titration of insulin [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 3.3.1. THYROID AND PARATHYROID DISEASES

Thyroid and parathyroid diseases have clinical manifestations that are pertinent to the perioperative care of the patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Although thyroid function tests are more sensitive, thyroid disease is usually adequately evaluated by clinical history, which should screen for signs and symptoms of hypothyroidism and hyperthyroidism [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hypothyroidism can lead to the development of hypothermia, hypoglycemia, hypoventilation, hyponatremia, and heart failure, as well as a susceptibility to anesthetics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Anesthesiologists should be alert to the possibility of the hypermetabolic state of thyroid storm in patients with hyperthyroidism [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A large thyroid mass may distort the upper airway, producing inspiratory stridor or wheezing, especially evident in the supine position [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In these cases, a chest x-ray should be obtained looking for evidence of tracheal deviation or narrowing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A CT scan of the upper airway and trachea will provide better detail of any airway compromise [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Patients with hyperparathyroidism often have hypercalcemia, and a preoperative determination of a serum calcium level is warranted [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Clinical Manifestations of Thyroid and Parathyroid Diseases:**

**Hyperthyroidism:**
* **General:** Weight loss; heat intolerance; warm, moist skin [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Cardiovascular:** Tachycardia, atrial fibrillation, congestive heart failure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Neurologic:** Nervousness, tremor, hyperactive reflexes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Musculoskeletal:** Muscle weakness, bone resorption [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Gastrointestinal:** Diarrhea [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Hematologic:** Anemia, thrombocytopenia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Hypothyroidism:**
* **General:** Cold intolerance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Cardiovascular:** Bradycardia, congestive heart failure, cardiomegaly, pericardial or pleural effusion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Neurologic:** Slow mental function, minimal reflexes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Musculoskeletal:** Large tongue, amyloidosis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Gastrointestinal:** Delayed gastric emptying [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Renal:** Impaired free water clearance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Hyperparathyroidism:**
* **General:** Weight loss, polydipsia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Cardiovascular:** Hypertension, heart block [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Neurologic:** Weakness, lethargy, headache, insomnia, apathy, depression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Musculoskeletal:** Bone pains, arthritis, pathologic fractures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Gastrointestinal:** Anorexia, nausea, vomiting, constipation, epigastric pain [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Renal:** Polyuria, hematuria [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

### 3.3.2. ADRENAL DISORDERS

The classic clinical presentation of a patient with pheochromocytoma includes intermittent hypertension, headache, diaphoresis, and tachycardia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Patients with endocrine tumors have a higher incidence of multiple endocrine neoplasia syndrome and pheochromocytoma should be ruled out as the cause of unexplained hypertension [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Over time, the mortality for surgical resection of a pheochromocytoma has decreased because of improvements in perioperative therapy for these patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A more important issue is that patients with a possible pheochromocytoma be identified before they are scheduled for other types of surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Adrenal–cortical suppression (or secondary adrenal insufficiency) results from exogenous steroid use producing negative feedback on the hypothalamic pituitary adrenal axis (HPAA) resulting in impaired cortisol production [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. If additional corticosteroids are not administered, vasodilation and profound hypotension may result [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Awake patients may exhibit altered mental status, weakness, nausea, vomiting, and abdominal pain [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Preoperative preparation for patients with adrenal suppression includes correction of any fluid and electrolyte abnormalities, as well as steroid supplementation, although there is not clear guidance on this practice [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Many anesthesiologists weigh the risk for HPAA suppression based upon dose and duration of previous steroid treatment, and the expected stress from surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Typically, patients taking steroids for less than 3 weeks or using prednisone 5 mg/day or less are considered low risk and may not warrant a perioperative stress dose [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. One must be wary as the addition of supraphysiologic doses of steroids can increase the risk of acute side effects, such as hyperglycemia, hypertension, fluid retention, and an increased risk of infection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. If a patient has Cushing syndrome, which is the most obvious manifestation of long-term high-dose steroid treatment (moon facies, striations of the skin, truncal obesity, hypertension, easy bruisability, and hypovolemia), then stress dose steroids should be administered [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hydrocortisone is the replacement drug of choice with doses ranging from 50 to 100 mg prior to incision followed by 25 to 50 mg every 8 hours for 24 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There is a lack of solid evidence for these recommendations and practice varies [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, experts do agree that patients should continue to receive their usual daily glucocorticoid dose [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Exogenous glucocorticoid administration should be considered in any patient who develops perioperative hypotension that is not responsive to standard resuscitative fluid administration or vasopressor therapy, and cannot be explained by other mechanisms, suggesting adrenal insufficiency [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Endocrine evaluation should focus on disease control and perioperative management strategies. For diabetes, assess type, control status, and management regimen; develop a perioperative glycemic control plan targeting 140-180 mg/dL. For thyroid disease, look for signs of hypo/hyperthyroidism and airway compromise from large goiters. For patients on steroids, assess risk of adrenal insufficiency based on dose and duration of therapy; consider stress-dose steroids for moderate to high-risk patients.

## 3.4. RENAL DISEASE

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|ⓘ|Part A: Renal physiology]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to renal disease]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Patient safety and perioperative management]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareRenal disease has important implications for fluid and electrolyte management, as well as metabolism of drugs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The cause of renal failure has considerable impact on clinical management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Patients with primary renal disease are likely to be younger and have good cardiopulmonary reserve, whereas a significant percentage of older patients with renal failure secondary to diabetes mellitus or hypertension will also have diffuse atherosclerosis and heart disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Chronic renal disease secondary to sickle cell disease, systemic lupus erythematosus, or vasculitis implies multisystem involvement and dysfunction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In patients with renal failure, the timing of their most recent dialysis will determine whether they are hyperor hypovolemic and hyperor hypokalemic [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is important to assess the patient's electrolytes prior to surgery and to ensure they are euvolemic prior to induction of anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Some patients will require dialysis before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Because renal failure is also associated with anemia and qualitatively deficient platelets, there should be a lower threshold for assessing these laboratory results preoperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Renal disease evaluation should identify cause and associated comorbidities, determine dialysis status and timing, assess volume status, and evaluate electrolyte abnormalities. Patients with renal disease often have anemia and platelet dysfunction, which may influence perioperative management.

## 3.5. LIVER DISEASE

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|ⓘ|Part A: Hepatic physiology]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to liver disease]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Patient safety and perioperative management]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareLiver disease is associated with decreased plasma protein production, thereby affecting drug binding, volume of distribution, metabolism, and clearance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Coagulopathy accompanies liver failure and the etiology can be multifactorial; it can result from malnutrition (poor absorption of nutrients as a consequence of cholestasis), impaired synthesis of coagulation factors, or thrombocytopenia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The history should identify specific risk factors for liver disease, such as previous blood transfusions, illicit drug use, or excessive alcohol intake [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The anesthesiologist should inquire about bruising, bleeding, or history of esophageal varices, the latter to potentially avoid esophageal instrumentation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Coagulation disorders may discourage the choice of regional anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The physical examination should screen for signs of underlying liver disease, such as jaundice, spider nevi, ascites, hepatosplenomegaly, or palmar erythema [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Ascites, a more obvious physical finding of liver failure, may significantly affect the patient's respiratory mechanics and make it difficult to lie flat [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In patients with chronic liver disease, perioperative risk increases with worsening severity of hepatic dysfunction as assessed by the Model for End-Stage Liver Disease (MELD) score [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The MELD assigns the patient a score of 8 to 40 that is derived from a complex formula that incorporates three biochemical variables—the serum total bilirubin concentration, serum creatinine concentration, and international normalized ratio (INR) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The MELD score has been prospectively validated as a prognostic marker of mortality in patients with cirrhosis, acute variceal bleeding, or acute alcoholic hepatitis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Liver disease evaluation should assess severity using tools like the MELD score, identify coagulation abnormalities, document signs of portal hypertension, and evaluate respiratory effects of ascites. Drug metabolism and clearance are altered, requiring dosage adjustments of anesthetic agents.

## 3.6. INFECTIOUS DISEASE

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to infectious diseases]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Infection control and staff safety]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareThe anesthesiologist should inquire about infectious diseases, including questions about foreign travel, that will dictate the need for increased protective measures for OR personnel and equipment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The COVID-19 pandemic has led to many changing guidelines and practice standards [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Due to the fluid nature of these circumstances, specific guidelines regarding tests, protective equipment and timing of surgery should be obtained from local hospital policies as well as recent information on the ASA website:<https://www.asahq.org/in-the-spotlight/coronavirus-covid-19-information> [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** For infectious diseases, follow current institutional and public health guidelines, which continue to evolve particularly for COVID-19. Consider implications for staff safety, PPE requirements, and potential modifications to anesthetic approach.

## 3.7. OTHER DISEASES

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approaches to musculoskeletal and rheumatologic conditions]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareArthritis is becoming more prevalent in our aging population, worsened by our sedentary lifestyle [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Osteoarthritis may result in difficulty positioning the head to facilitate tracheal intubation or difficulty in positioning for regional anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These are likewise problems in patients with RA; however, of particular importance is the potential for atlantoaxial instability or superior migration of the odontoid causing spinal cord compression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Involvement of the cervical spine in RA patients typically parallels the extent of peripheral disease and may warrant preoperative radiologic screening [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Although not as common, RA of the temporomandibular joint may impede mouth opening for tracheal intubation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. RA is a multisystem disease, potentially leading to derangements in other organ systems, causing restrictive lung disease, pleural effusions, pericarditis, and anemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** For arthritis, especially rheumatoid arthritis, assess for difficult airway management due to limited neck mobility or temporomandibular joint involvement. Consider C-spine imaging in patients with significant RA to evaluate for atlantoaxial instability that may influence airway management and positioning.

# 4. PREOPERATIVE LABORATORY TESTING




## 4.1. DEFINING NORMAL VALUES

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to laboratory interpretation]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Evidence-based practice]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareIn attempting to determine the optimal choice of preoperative tests, it is important to understand the interpretation of the results [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Ideally, tests would either confirm or exclude the presence of a disease; however, most tests only increase or decrease the probability of disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In determining reference ranges for diagnostic tests, values that fall outside the 95% confidence intervals for normal individuals are considered abnormal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Therefore, up to 5% of normal individuals can have "abnormal" test results [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. To determine its clinical relevance, a test must be interpreted within the context of the clinical situation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Performing tests in patients with no risk for having the pathophysiologic process of interest is likely to yield a high number of false-positive results [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Interpreting these results as abnormal, and initiating treatment, could lead to harm without any benefit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Normal laboratory values are typically defined as values within the 95% confidence interval for a healthy population. This means up to 5% of normal individuals may have "abnormal" results. Test results must be interpreted in clinical context, as testing without clinical indication yields many false positives.

## 4.2. RISKS AND COSTS VERSUS BENEFITS

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to test selection]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Cost-effective practice and healthcare resource utilization]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareThe 2012 ASA Practice Advisory for Preanesthesia Evaluation states that routine preoperative tests do not make an important contribution to preanesthetic evaluation of an asymptomatic patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Selective preoperative tests should be ordered only after consideration of specific information obtained from the medical record, history and physical, and the type or invasiveness of the planned procedure and anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Consideration should also be given to whether the results will affect the plan for the patient's perioperative course [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Medical testing is associated with significant cost, both in real dollars and in potential harm [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Routine preoperative testing has been estimated to cost billions of dollars annually in the United States [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. An "abnormal" test that is later determined to be a false result can lead to significant cost and real harm [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, a positive exercise ECG stress test in a healthy 40-year-old woman may lead to coronary angiography [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Coronary angiography is not a benign procedure and can lead to vascular injuries [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. On the basis of Bayesian analysis, a positive ECG stress test result in this patient would most likely be a false positive, and hence it would be inappropriate to order this test [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. No additional information would be obtained, thousands of dollars in medical costs would accrue, and the patient may sustain iatrogenic injury [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Routine laboratory screening tests produce only minimal benefits in healthy patients, and routine testing is not indicated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The following protocol for laboratory testing is modified from a risk–benefit analysis and the 2012 ASA Practice Advisory for Preanesthetic Evaluation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Routine preoperative testing has significant costs and potential harms with minimal benefit in healthy patients. The ASA recommends selective testing based on specific clinical indications from history, physical, and planned procedure. Tests should only be ordered if they would change perioperative management.

### 4.2.1. CLINICAL CONSIDERATIONS FOR LABORATORY TESTING

**Blood count** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Extremes of age
* Liver or kidney disease
* Anticoagulant use
* Bleeding/hematologic disorder
* Malignancy
* Type and invasiveness of procedure

**Coagulation studies** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Liver or kidney disease
* Bleeding disorder
* Anticoagulant use
* Chemotherapy

**Serum chemistries (glucose, electrolytes, renal and liver function)** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Liver or renal disease, or perioperative risk of dysfunction
* Diabetes
* Diuretic, digoxin, or steroid use
* Central nervous system disease
* Endocrine disorders
* Elderly
* Malnutrition
* Type and invasiveness of procedure

**Chest x-ray** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Pulmonary disease or clinical manifestations
* Unstable cardiovascular disease
* Type and invasiveness of procedure

**ECG** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Cardiovascular disease or clinical risk factors
* Pulmonary disease
* Type and invasiveness of procedure

**Pregnancy test** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* Possible pregnancy

**Complete Blood Count and Hemoglobin Concentration:** A preoperative hemoglobin or hematocrit value has been suggested as the only test necessary in many patients prior to elective surgery; however, even this minimal standard has been questioned [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In only 1.1% of the records of 1,863 pediatric patients scheduled for elective outpatient procedures was the hematocrit found to be abnormal, and in none of these patients was the procedure canceled or the anesthetic plan modified [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, a baseline hematocrit is still indicated in any procedure with a risk of significant blood loss [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Both the standard for the lowest acceptable perioperative hematocrit and the indication for a preoperative transfusion have changed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The current recommendations of the National Blood Resource Education Committee are that a hemoglobin level of 7 g/dL is acceptable in patients without systemic disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In patients with systemic disease, signs of inadequate systemic oxygen delivery (tachycardia, tachypnea) are an indication for transfusion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Coagulation Studies:** Coagulation disorders can have a significant impact on the surgical procedure and perioperative management [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In patients with hemophilia or von Willebrand disease, abnormal laboratory studies even in the absence of clinical abnormalities require preoperative preparation of the patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Abnormal coagulation values may delay the surgery depending on the degree of abnormality and the procedure planned [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, neurosurgery has little tolerance for values outside of the normal range due to the serious consequences if uncontrolled bleeding were to occur perioperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Surgery may be delayed for hours if fresh-frozen plasma or platelets are needed to acutely correct a coagulopathy, or for days if vitamin K is administered for correction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Pregnancy Testing:** Routine pregnancy testing in women of childbearing potential is a subject of considerable debate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Surgery itself may cause harm to the fetus or expose the fetus to potential teratogens, such as x-rays or certain medications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Thus, knowledge of pregnancy status may change the timing of surgery or anesthetic technique [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Point-of-care urine β-hCG testing is easily and rapidly performed by nursing staff with high sensitivity and specificity; however, rare circumstances may lead to false negatives or positives such as low levels of β-hCG or hormone-secreting tumors [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There are also ethical issues regarding privacy and autonomy, especially for the minor patient accompanied by parents [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The ASA Practice Advisory for Preanesthesia Evaluation recommends that females of childbearing age be offered pregnancy testing if the result would change medical management, but it is not mandatory [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Informed consent or assent should be obtained through shared decision making between the patient and provider [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Chest X-Rays:** A preoperative chest x-ray can identify abnormalities that may lead to either delay or cancellation of the planned surgical procedure or modification of perioperative care [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, identification of pneumonia, pulmonary edema, pulmonary nodules, or a mediastinal mass could all lead to modification of care [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, routine testing in the population without risk factors can lead to more harm than benefit and morbidity from additional, unwarranted follow-up procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The American College of Physicians suggests that a chest x-ray is indicated in the presence of active chest disease or an intrathoracic procedure but not on the basis of advanced age alone [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In a meta-analysis, a review of published reports from 1966 to 1992 in the English, French, and Spanish literature showed that abnormalities were reported in 10% of routine preoperative chest x-rays, of which only 1.3% were unexpected [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These findings resulted in modification in management in only 0.1% of patients, with unknown influence on outcome [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The authors estimated a cost of $23,000 for each finding that influenced management, and concluded that routine chest x-rays without a clinical indication were not justified [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Pulmonary Function Tests:** Consensus guidelines do not support the routine use of pulmonary function studies to predict perioperative respiratory complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Pulmonary function tests can be divided into two categories: spirometry and arterial blood gas analysis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Spirometry can provide information on forced vital capacity (FVC), forced expiratory volume in 1 second (FEV1), ratio of FEV1/FVC, and average forced expiratory flow from 25% to 75% (FEF 25% to 75%) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Although each of these measures has a sound physiologic basis, their practical assessment can vary greatly among healthy persons and the tests rarely provide additional information beyond that obtained from history [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For those patients considered for pulmonary resection, evaluation using spirometry, diffusion capacity measurements, radionucleotide lung perfusion scanning, and cardiopulmonary exercise testing may help to define those patients at high risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

With the ubiquitous availability of pulse oximetry, the use of preoperative arterial blood gas sampling has become less important [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It may still be indicated in those with poor pulmonary function, since determining the baseline CO2 is useful in managing perioperative ventilator settings, and resting hypercapnia is associated with increased perioperative risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. One method of assessing the probability of CO2 retention is evaluation of the serum bicarbonate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A normal serum bicarbonate value will virtually exclude the diagnosis of chronic CO2 retention [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Preoperative laboratory testing should be targeted based on specific clinical indications. Complete blood count is indicated for patients with risk of blood loss, anemia, or bleeding disorders. Coagulation studies are needed only for patients with bleeding disorders, liver disease, or on anticoagulants. Chest x-rays and pulmonary function tests have limited value without specific indications. Pregnancy testing should be offered but not mandatory.

# 5. PREPARATION

## 5.0. PREHABILITATION PROTOCOLS
<!-- EPA-1:K3:prehabilitation EPA-1:S1 -->

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|Part A: Exercise Physiology]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|Part B: Preoperative Optimization]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|Part C: Patient Education]]

**Exam Weighting:** Medium-High (Increasingly assessed in perioperative medicine)

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient Care, Systems-Based Practice

### 5.0.1. DEFINITION AND RATIONALE

**Prehabilitation** is the process of enhancing functional capacity before surgery in anticipation of physiological stress. It represents a paradigm shift from traditional perioperative care, moving focus to the preoperative period to strengthen physiological reserve before surgery.

**Core Principle:** "Building reserve before surgery draws on it" - preparing patients physically, nutritionally, and psychologically to withstand surgical stress and optimize recovery.

**Key Distinctions:**
- **Prehabilitation**: Proactive optimization BEFORE surgery
- **Rehabilitation**: Reactive recovery AFTER surgery
- **ERAS Integration**: Prehabilitation complements Enhanced Recovery After Surgery protocols; combined approach achieves optimal outcomes

**Rationale for Prehabilitation:**
* Surgery generates a predictable physiological stress response that patients must withstand
* High-risk patients have diminished physiological reserve and are at increased risk of complications
* The preoperative window offers an opportunity to modify risk factors and improve fitness
* Prehabilitated patients exhibit greater resilience and functional recovery under ERAS care [Source: BJA Education 2022]

**Evidence Base:**
* Meta-analyses demonstrate reduced postoperative complications with multimodal prehabilitation
* Home-based prehabilitation programs achieve 82% median adherence
* Hospital length of stay reduced by average 2 days in prehabilitated patients
* Functional walking capacity recovered faster in prehabilitation group at 6 weeks post-surgery

### 5.0.2. MULTIMODAL PREHABILITATION COMPONENTS

**The Four Pillars of Prehabilitation:**
1. **Exercise Prehabilitation** - Aerobic and resistance training to improve cardiorespiratory fitness
2. **Nutritional Prehabilitation** - Protein optimization, immunonutrition, carbohydrate loading
3. **Psychological Prehabilitation** - Anxiety reduction, coping strategies, patient education
4. **Behavioral Modification** - Smoking cessation, alcohol reduction (see sections 5.1 and below)

**Optimal Program Duration:**
* **Ideal**: 4-8 weeks before surgery
* **Minimum effective**: 2-4 weeks for measurable benefit
* **Maximum practical**: 3 months (adherence declines beyond this)
* Duration should balance surgical urgency against time needed for physiological adaptation

### 5.0.3. PATIENT SELECTION FOR PREHABILITATION

**High-Risk Populations (Priority for Prehabilitation):**
* Diminished physical reserves (sarcopenia, chronic illness, deconditioning)
* Malnutrition or at risk of malnutrition (screen with MUST or NRS-2002)
* Advanced age with frailty
* Cancer cachexia
* High surgical risk profiles (major abdominal, thoracic, vascular surgery)
* Poor cardiorespiratory fitness (low AT on CPET)

**Objective Risk Stratification Tools:**
| Tool | Threshold for Prehabilitation | Clinical Application |
|------|------------------------------|---------------------|
| **CPET - Anaerobic Threshold (AT)** | AT ≤11-13 mL/kg/min | High risk; strong indication for prehabilitation |
| **CPET - VO2 peak** | ≤18 mL/kg/min | Reduced fitness; benefit from exercise program |
| **6-Minute Walk Test (6MWT)** | <400m or declining | Functional capacity limitation |
| **Timed Up-and-Go (TUG)** | >12 seconds | Mobility impairment, fall risk |
| **MUST Score** | ≥1 | Nutritional risk present |
| **NRS-2002** | ≥3 | Nutritional intervention indicated |
