**CPET for Prehabilitation Planning:**
* Anaerobic threshold (AT) provides index of submaximal, sustainable exercise capacity
* AT cannot be volitionally influenced by patient (objective measure)
* AT predicts postoperative outcomes with more precision than other CPET variables in 30+ cohort studies
* CPET information guides exercise prescription intensity (typically 70-80% of AT)

### 5.0.4. EXERCISE PREHABILITATION

**Rationale:**
* Aerobic exercise enhances cardiovascular capacity and oxygen delivery critical for surgical recovery
* Resistance training targets skeletal muscle, counteracting surgical and malnutrition-induced wasting
* CPET-guided exercise prescription ensures appropriate intensity for individual patients

**Exercise Protocol Options:**

**1. High-Intensity Interval Training (HIIT):**
* Most efficient modality for improving aerobic capacity in limited preoperative window
* **Protocol**: 25-minute sessions, 3 times per week for 3-4 weeks
* **Intensity**: Based on steep ramp test performance (70-80% of threshold)
* **Outcomes**: 17% improvement in aerobic capacity in 4-week programs (high-risk liver/pancreatic surgery)
* Weekly reassessment and intensity adjustment recommended

**2. Combined Aerobic + Resistance Training:**
* **Aerobic component**: Walking, cycling, swimming - 20-30 minutes
* **Resistance component**: Major muscle groups, 2-3 sets of 8-12 repetitions
* Yields significant gains in hand grip strength and functional mobility

**3. Home-Based Programs:**
* Feasible alternative to supervised programs
* Median adherence 82% in meta-analysis of 29 RCTs (3,508 patients)
* Wearable devices improve adherence by approximately 25%
* Telemedicine support comparable to in-person supervision

**Measured Improvements After Exercise Prehabilitation:**
| Parameter | Improvement | 95% CI |
|-----------|-------------|--------|
| VO2 peak | +2.4 mL/min/kg | 0.8-3.9 |
| VO2 at AT | +1.2 mL/min/kg | 0.4-2.1 |
| Maximal aerobic power | +16.8 W | 8.2-25.3 |

**Clinical Pearl:** For high-risk patients with VO2 at AT ≤13 mL/kg/min or VO2 peak ≤18 mL/kg/min, a 4-week home-based HIIT program shows significant and clinically meaningful improvements in aerobic fitness.

### 5.0.5. NUTRITIONAL PREHABILITATION

**Nutritional Screening:**
* **MUST (Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool)**: BMI, unintentional weight loss, acute illness effect
* **NRS-2002 (Nutritional Risk Screening)**: Impaired nutritional status + disease severity
* **PG-SGA (Patient-Generated Subjective Global Assessment)**: Detailed assessment if screening positive

**Protein Requirements:**
| Patient Status | Protein Target | Rationale |
|---------------|----------------|-----------|
| General surgical patient | 1.2-1.5 g/kg/day | Maintain nitrogen balance |
| Anabolic state targeting | 1.5-1.8 g/kg/day | Expected lean mass gain 1-2 kg |
| Renal impairment | 1.0 g/kg/day | Avoid uraemia |
| Sarcopenic patient | 1.5-2.0 g/kg/day + resistance exercise | Synergistic muscle building |

**Protein Optimization Strategies:**
* Branched-chain amino acids (BCAAs), particularly leucine, stimulate muscle protein synthesis via mTOR pathway
* Whey protein optimal due to rapid digestion, high bioavailability
* **Best timing**: 30-60 minutes post-exercise for maximal anabolic effect
* Oral nutrition supplements (ONS) if dietary intake inadequate

**Immunonutrition:**

**Composition and Mechanisms:**
* **L-arginine**: Nitric oxide precursor; supports vasodilation and wound perfusion
* **Omega-3 fatty acids**: Anti-inflammatory; shift eicosanoid balance away from pro-inflammatory mediators
* **Glutamine**: Enhances lymphocyte proliferation; supports intestinal barrier function
* **Micronutrients**: Zinc, selenium, vitamins A, C, D support immune function

**Evidence for Immunonutrition:**
* Reduces postoperative infections by up to 30% in gastrointestinal surgeries
* Meta-analyses show reduced hospital length of stay
* Greatest benefit in malnourished patients undergoing major GI surgery

**Immunonutrition Protocol:**
* Initiate 5-7 days preoperatively
* Continue through immediate postoperative period if indicated
* Specialized immunonutrition formulas (SIM) available commercially

**Carbohydrate Loading:**
* **Protocol**: ≥50 g carbohydrates 2-3 hours before surgery
* **Benefits**: Improves postoperative insulin resistance; reduces hospital length of stay
* **Mechanism**: Stimulates insulin secretion, reduces gluconeogenesis, preserves muscle protein stores
* Part of standard ERAS protocols

**Timing of Nutritional Intervention by Patient Status:**
| Patient Status | Duration | Components |
|---------------|----------|------------|
| Mildly malnourished | 7-10 days | Oral nutrition supplements + dietitian review |
| High risk/malnourished | 10-14 days minimum | Nutrition therapy + resistance exercise |
| Sarcopenic/frail | 4-5 weeks optimal | Full multimodal prehabilitation program |

### 5.0.6. PSYCHOLOGICAL PREHABILITATION

**Prevalence and Impact of Preoperative Anxiety:**
* 40-85% of patients experience preoperative apprehension
* Clinically significant and debilitating anxiety in approximately 40%
* **Negative associations**: Longer hospital stay, increased postoperative pain, higher opioid use, increased rehospitalization rates

**Psychological Intervention Types:**
1. **Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT)**: Addresses maladaptive thoughts and behaviors
2. **Acceptance and Commitment Therapy (ACT)**: Promotes psychological flexibility
3. **Supportive Psychotherapy**: Emotional support, reassurance, therapeutic relationship
4. **Relaxation Techniques**: Guided imagery, mindfulness, progressive muscle relaxation
5. **Patient Education**: Information about surgery, anaesthesia, and recovery expectations

**Evidence for Psychological Prehabilitation (UCLA Meta-Analysis 2025):**
* Analysis of 20 RCTs (2004-2024), 2,376 patients
* **Hospital stay reduced**: Average 1.62 days
* **Pain reduced**: Average 3.52 points on standardized pain scales
* **Anxiety and depression reduced**: Significant across validated scales
* Type of psychotherapy did not significantly affect outcomes (except for anxiety)
* Benefits consistent regardless of surgery type

**Clinical Implications:**
* May reduce post-surgical opioid use (anxiety/depression are risk factors for persistent opioid use)
* Psychological support improves adherence to other prehabilitation components
* Patients with psychological support demonstrated higher adherence to protein/caloric intake recommendations
* Cost-effective intervention with significant return on investment

**Clinical Pearl:** An informative, comforting preoperative visit may be more effective than pharmacological sedation - "more patients were adequately prepared for surgery after a preoperative interview than after 2 mg/kg of pentobarbital given intramuscularly."

### 5.0.7. SMOKING CESSATION IN PREHABILITATION

**Risk Profile of Smokers (Evidence Summary):**
* **Overall**: 2-4 times more postoperative complications than non-smokers
* **Most frequent**: Wound complications and pulmonary complications
* **Specific risks**:
  * General surgical morbidity: 1.52x higher (meta-analysis, 107 studies)
  * 30-day mortality: 1.38x increased odds
  * Myocardial infarction: 1.80 odds ratio
  * Pneumonia: 2.09 odds ratio
  * Anastomotic leak (colorectal): 4.02x increased risk

**Optimal Cessation Timing:**
| Duration | Benefit |
|----------|---------|
| **12 hours** | Reduced nicotine and CO levels; improved tissue oxygenation |
| **2-4 weeks** | Improved mucociliary function; reduced secretions |
| **4-8 weeks (ideal)** | **Halves postoperative complication rate** |
| **>8 weeks** | Additional cardiovascular and pulmonary benefits |

**Evidence for Intensive Cessation Intervention:**
* Overall postoperative complications: RR 0.42 (95% CI 0.27-0.65)
* Wound infection: 72% reduction (RR 0.28, 95% CI 0.12-0.64)
* Joint arthroplasty: 88% relative risk reduction for wound complications
* Intensive intervention achieves 60% cessation rate by surgery (vs 7% controls)
* Preoperative patients achieve 50% cessation rates (vs 5-7.5% general population)

**Effective Intervention Components:**
1. **Weekly counseling**: Face-to-face or telephone
2. **Nicotine replacement therapy (NRT)**: Patches, gum, lozenges, inhaler
3. **Pharmacotherapy**: Varenicline, bupropion if appropriate
4. **Behavioral support**: Motivation enhancement, coping strategies
5. **Brief intervention**: Still beneficial (RR 1.3 improvement) but less effective than intensive

**Safety Note:** No increased risk from short-term cessation - earlier concerns based on flawed 1989 study. Current evidence shows even short-term cessation is beneficial.

### 5.0.8. ALCOHOL REDUCTION IN PREHABILITATION

**Risk Profile of Hazardous Drinkers:**
* 2-4 times more postoperative complications
* Most frequent: Infections, bleeding episodes, cardiopulmonary insufficiency, death
* Requires proactive management of potential withdrawal during perioperative period

**Optimal Timing:**
* 4-8 weeks abstinence before surgery significantly reduces complications
* Goal: Complete alcohol cessation before surgery

**Intervention Program Components:**
* Reassurance and communication about surgical risk
* Treatment of alcohol withdrawal if needed (monitor with CIWA-Ar)
* Relapse prophylaxis and behavioral support
* Follow-up throughout preoperative period

### 5.0.9. INTEGRATION WITH ERAS PROTOCOLS

**Complementary Relationship:**
* Prehabilitation addresses the preoperative phase (before hospital admission)
* ERAS focuses on immediate perioperative and postoperative elements
* **Key principle**: "Recovery begins preoperatively, not postoperatively"

**Evidence for Combined Approach:**
* Prehabilitated patients preserve fat-free mass at 4 and 8 weeks post-surgery under ERAS care
* Greater proportion of prehabilitation patients recovered functional walking capacity at 6 weeks
* Only 5 of 13 published ERAS guidelines currently recommend prehabilitation (opportunity for improvement)

**Recommended Prehabilitation Elements Within ERAS:**
* Patient education and expectation setting
* Smoking and alcohol cessation
* Nutritional optimization and carbohydrate loading
* Exercise prescription for high-risk patients
* Anaemia correction (see section 3.4.3)
* Psychological preparation and anxiety reduction

### 5.0.10. PRACTICAL IMPLEMENTATION

**Program Structure:**
| Component | Frequency | Duration | Key Targets |
|-----------|-----------|----------|-------------|
| Exercise | 3x/week | 25-30 min/session | HIIT or combined aerobic/resistance |
| Nutrition | Daily | Throughout program | Protein 1.2-1.5 g/kg/day |
| Immunonutrition | Daily | 5-7 days pre-op | Arginine, omega-3, glutamine |
| Psychological | 1-2 sessions | Program duration | Education, relaxation techniques |
| Smoking cessation | Continuous | Minimum 4 weeks | Complete abstinence |

**Delivery Models:**
1. **Supervised (hospital/gym-based)**: Highest intensity, direct supervision
2. **Home-based**: Feasible with good adherence (82%); use wearables/apps
3. **Hybrid**: Combination of supervised and home sessions
4. **Telemedicine-supported**: Comparable outcomes to in-person with higher patient satisfaction
5. **Group-based**: Peer support improves adherence and motivation

**Technology Integration:**
* **Wearable devices**: Activity tracking improves adherence by ~25%
* **Smartphone apps**: Monitoring, motivation, education
* **Telemedicine**: Remote supervision and follow-up
* **AI applications**: Personalized intervention tailoring (emerging)

**Barriers to Implementation:**
* Resource constraints and staffing
* Lack of standardized protocols
* Short preoperative windows (especially cancer surgery)
* Patient adherence challenges
* Variable healthcare system support

### 5.0.11. OUTCOME MEASURES

**Primary Outcomes:**
* **Functional capacity**: 6MWT distance, TUG test performance
* **Muscle parameters**: Skeletal muscle index (CT), hand grip strength, lean body mass
* **Cardiovascular fitness**: VO2 peak, ventilatory anaerobic threshold

**Secondary Outcomes:**
* **Hospital metrics**: Length of stay (average 2-day reduction), readmission rates
* **Complications**: Postoperative infections, wound complications
* **Psychological**: Anxiety scores (Amsterdam Preoperative Anxiety Scale), depression
* **Quality of life**: Patient-reported outcomes, return to function
* **Long-term**: 5-year survival, disability-free survival (emerging outcome priority)

**Bottom Line:** Prehabilitation is a multimodal intervention combining exercise, nutrition, psychological support, and behavioral modification (smoking/alcohol cessation) to optimize patients before major surgery. Programs should ideally run 4-8 weeks, targeting high-risk patients identified by CPET (AT ≤11-13 mL/kg/min), nutritional screening (MUST ≥1), and clinical assessment. Evidence demonstrates reduced complications, shorter hospital stays, and faster functional recovery. Prehabilitation complements but does not replace ERAS protocols. [Source: BJA 2022, PMC Reviews 2023-2025, UCLA Meta-Analysis 2025]

## 5.1. SMOKING CESSATION

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to smoking cessation]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Preventive health and patient education]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareSurgery provides an opportunity for intervention to promote smoking cessation, as the patient may be open to making changes to better their health [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Hence, the ASA is encouraging anesthesiologists to use the preoperative evaluation as a teachable moment to discuss smoking cessation and provide educational resources available on the ASA website [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Because patients will be unable to smoke in the hospital, such timing may help give them more incentive to quit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It should be explained to patients who smoke that they are at increased risk for pulmonary and cardiac complications as well as impaired wound healing and infection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The longer they are tobacco free before surgery the better, as their bodies will have more time for repair [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Even only 12 hours of smoking cessation will reduce levels of nicotine and carbon monoxide, improving blood flow [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The long-term benefits of quitting smoking include: addition of 6 to 8 years to their life, reduction in risk of lung cancer and heart disease, savings of at least $1,400 per year (not including health care costs), and reduced exposure of the family to secondhand smoke [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Offer patients additional help to quit by referring them to 1-800-QUIT-NOW, a free and confidential counseling service [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** The preoperative period presents an excellent opportunity for smoking cessation counseling. Educate patients about increased perioperative risks (pulmonary complications, impaired wound healing) and both short and long-term benefits of quitting. Even 12 hours of cessation improves blood flow by reducing carbon monoxide and nicotine levels.

## 5.2. CONTINUING CURRENT MEDICATIONS/TREATMENT OF COEXISTING DISEASES

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to medication management]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Medication safety]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareIt is the responsibility of the anesthesiologist to instruct patients regarding which medications to take or not take preoperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Occasionally, new medications will be prescribed or doses increased, such as steroids for adrenal insufficiency [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Prescribed and over-the-counter medications may affect the anesthetic; anesthesiologists must be knowledgeable about their actions [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. As a general rule, patients should take their prescription medications with water on the day of surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, important exceptions exist, particularly for the management of diabetes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**β-Blockers:** The role of β-blockers during the perioperative period has evolved over time, based on clinical studies evaluating both effectiveness and the potential for risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Historically, β-blockers have been felt to reduce the incidence of mortality and nonfatal MIs after surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Current guidelines recommend that β-blockers be continued in those already receiving β-blockers to treat angina pectoris, symptomatic arrhythmias, and hypertension [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Recent clinical studies, however, have questioned the decision to initiate perioperative β-blockers and have suggested that the decision be based on clinical judgment in evaluating the patient's risk factors for cardiovascular complications together with the type of surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The POISE trial (a large, multicenter, randomized controlled trial) compared preoperative β-blocker therapy with placebo [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Patients receiving β-blocker therapy had a lower risk of perioperative MI, but were at significantly increased risk for mortality and stroke [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. As potential mechanisms for enhanced risk, perioperative hypotension and bradycardia were significantly increased in patients receiving β-blockers [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A meta-analysis evaluating perioperative β-blockade in noncardiac surgery in 33 randomized controlled trials with 12,306 patients found no difference in overall mortality, but decreased risk of perioperative MI [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Similar to the POISE trial, the risk of nonfatal stroke was increased with β-blockade [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Recognizing these results, updated guidelines from the ACC/AHA support, as a class I recommendation, the perioperative continuation of β-blockers for patients already receiving β-blocker for appropriate conditions, and they support a role for β-blocker therapy titrated to heart rate and blood pressure during vascular or intermediate-risk surgeries in patients with CAD, cardiac ischemia identified by preoperative testing, or more than one RCRI risk factor [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Importantly, the ACC/AHA guidelines stress uncertainty for the role of β-blockade for vascular or intermediate-risk surgery in patients with only one risk factor without ischemic heart disease or in patients without risk factors who have not taken β-blocker therapy previously [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A review of current literature shows a decreased risk of nonfatal MI at the expense of increased perioperative risk of stroke, death, hypotension, and bradycardia associated with fixed, high-dose β-blocker therapy begun on the day of surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Initiation and titration of β-blockade 2 or more days prior to the day of surgery may offer a role in the reduction of risk for perioperative MI, but there is insufficient data to recommend this practice, and it remains controversial [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. This is acknowledged by the ACC/AHA and further multicenter RCTs are needed to address this knowledge gap [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Anticoagulation:** The perioperative management of patients receiving chronic anticoagulation can be challenging, as it requires a balance between reducing the risk of thromboembolic events versus excessive bleeding and hemorrhagic complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The ACC/AHA states that the risk of bleeding must be weighed against the benefit of remaining on anticoagulation on a case-by-case basis, considering the specific patient, medication, and procedure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Oral anticoagulation is often prescribed for patients with atrial fibrillation for stroke prevention, as well as patients with a history of venous thromboembolism and valvular heart disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Depending on the pharmacokinetics of the oral anticoagulant, therapy is often interrupted 2 to 5 days prior to noncardiac surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In a randomized controlled trial, the effect of bridging anticoagulation in 1,884 patients with atrial fibrillation who had their warfarin interrupted prior to noncardiac surgery was evaluated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. No difference was found in the rates of arterial thromboembolism, but the incidence of major bleeding was found to be significantly higher in the bridging group [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Interrupting anticoagulation therapy in patients with atrial fibrillation therefore appears to be safe, and perioperative bridging is typically unnecessary [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In contrast, bridging anticoagulation may be appropriate in patients at high risk of arterial thromboembolism, such as patients with mechanical aortic or mitral valves [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Aspirin:** Aspirin is an irreversible inhibitor of platelet cyclooxygenase-1, which decreases platelet aggregation and thrombotic risk, but may increase intraoperative blood loss and hemorrhagic complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. POISE-2 Trial randomized over 10,000 patients undergoing noncardiac surgery at risk for vascular complications to aspirin 200 mg or placebo [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. No protective effect on the incidence of MACE or death was seen in the aspirin group, however aspirin use was associated with an increased risk of major bleeding [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Notably, patients within 6 weeks of placement of a bare metal cardiac stent or 12 months of a drug eluting stent placement were excluded, as well as patients undergoing carotid endarterectomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The ACC/AHA does not recommend routine aspirin use prior to noncardiac surgery, although aspirin therapy may be appropriate for certain patient populations (e.g., high-risk CAD, cerebrovascular disease) if ischemic risks outweigh the risk of bleeding [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Statins:** There is growing evidence in the literature to suggest that perioperative statin therapy is safe and beneficial in reducing morbidity and mortality in the perioperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Statins work via several mechanisms: lowering lipids, enhancing nitric oxide–mediated pathways, reducing expression of cytokines and adhesion molecules, and lowering C-reactive protein levels with associated vasodilatory, anti-inflammatory, and antithrombotic effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The greatest benefit occurs in patients at higher risk for cardiovascular complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There is good evidence that the perioperative withdrawal of statins increases morbidity; both the ACC/AHA and the European Society of Cardiology (ESC) have made a class 1 recommendation that patients who are on a statin preoperatively should be restarted on a statin postoperatively as soon as possible [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Patients with noncoronary atherosclerosis should be treated with statin therapy for secondary prevention, independent of noncardiac surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The ESC also recommends that statins be started in high-risk surgery patients, optimally between 30 days and at least 1 week before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Guidelines from the ACC/AHA state that statin use is reasonable for patients undergoing vascular surgery with or without clinical risk factors, and statins may be considered for patients with at least one clinical risk factor who are undergoing intermediate-risk procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Angiotensin-Converting Enzyme Inhibitors and Angiotensin Receptor Blockers:** The perioperative management of angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors (ACEIs) and angiotensin II receptor blockers (ARBs) is controversial [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Intraoperative hypotension secondary to the continuation of ACEI/ARBs has led to many clinicians withholding therapy on the day of surgery, although outcomes data is inconsistent [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A recent meta-analysis evaluated 9 studies with over 6,000 patients on chronic ACEI/ARB therapy before noncardiac surgery and showed that withholding ACEI/ARB treatment the day of surgery was associated with significantly less intraoperative hypotension (OR, 0.63; 95% CI, 0.47–0.85) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There was, however, no difference in mortality (OR 0.97; 95% CI 0.62–1.52) or in the occurrence of MACE (OR 1.12; 95% CI 0.82–1.52) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The ACC/AHA acknowledges that there is a paucity of data regarding whether specific surgeries or patient subgroups are most likely to benefit from holding ACEI/ARB therapy preoperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Their recommendations are that continuation of ACEI/ARB therapy in the perioperative period is reasonable, however, if they are held prior to surgery, they should be restarted as soon as clinically feasible [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Medication management requires careful risk-benefit analysis for each patient. Continue beta-blockers in patients already receiving them for appropriate indications. For anticoagulants, decisions should be individualized based on thrombotic vs. bleeding risk. Aspirin is not routinely recommended except in specific high-risk patients. Statins should be continued perioperatively, with early postoperative resumption. For ACEIs/ARBs, either continuing or holding on the day of surgery is reasonable, though holding may reduce intraoperative hypotension.

## 5.3. PREVENTION OF PERIOPERATIVE PULMONARY ASPIRATION

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|ⓘ|Part A: Physiology of gastric emptying]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to aspiration risk management]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Patient safety strategies]]

**Exam Weighting:** High (Critical for patient safety)

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareExtrapolation from a primate study led to the statement in 1974 that patients who had a 25-mL residual gastric volume with a pH lower than 2.5 were at risk of aspiration, which might imply that some 40% to 80% of patients scheduled for elective surgery may be at risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, clinically significant pulmonary aspiration is actually very rare today in healthy patients undergoing general anesthesia, quoted as occurring in 1 in 3,000 to 1 in 6,000 anesthetics, though increasing to 1 in 600 for emergency anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Some patients who present for anesthesia are at increased risk for aspiration—the Australian Anesthetic Incident Monitoring Study found 133 cases of aspiration in 5,000 incidents reported, with the top 10 risk factors for aspiration shown below [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, almost 25% of the patients who aspirated had no risk factors [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Errors in judgment, faults in airway management technique, and inadequate patient preparation were felt to be the most common factors contributing to these events [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Top 10 Factors Predisposing to Aspiration:**
1. Emergency surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
2. Inadequate anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
3. Abdominal pathology [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
4. Obesity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
5. Opioid medication [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
6. Neurologic deficit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
7. Lithotomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
8. Difficult intubation/airway [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
9. Reflux [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
10. Hiatal hernia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.1.1. COMPREHENSIVE PREOPERATIVE ASSESSMENT [2017 ASA GUIDELINES]

**Patient Population:** These guidelines apply to healthy patients of all ages undergoing elective procedures where general anesthesia, regional anesthesia, or procedural sedation will be administered.

**High-Risk Conditions Requiring Modified Approach:**
The guidelines may not apply to or may need modification for patients with:
* **Esophageal disorders:** Significant uncontrolled reflux disease, hiatal hernia, Zenker's diverticulum, achalasia, stricture
* **Previous gastric surgery:** e.g., gastric bypass
* **Medical conditions:** Gastroparesis, diabetes mellitus, obesity
* **Medications:** Opioid use
* **Clinical scenarios:** Gastrointestinal obstruction, acute intra-abdominal processes, pregnancy, emergency procedures, enteral tube feeding
* **Airway considerations:** Patients where airway management might be difficult

**Assessment Components:**
* **Medical records review:** Check for history of gastroesophageal reflux disease, dysphagia symptoms, gastrointestinal motility disorders, metabolic disorders (e.g., diabetes mellitus)
* **Physical examination:** Include assessment of ASA physical status, age, sex, type of surgery, potential for difficult airway management
* **Patient interview:** Verify compliance with fasting requirements at time of procedure

⚠️ **Risk-Benefit Analysis:** When fasting guidelines are not followed, compare risks and benefits of proceeding, with consideration given to amount and type of liquids or solids ingested.

### 5.3.2. ASA FASTING GUIDELINES

The ASA published updated Practice Guidelines for Preoperative Fasting and Pharmacologic Intervention for the Prevention of Perioperative Aspiration in 2017 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The guidelines specifically address healthy patients of all ages scheduled for elective procedures in which general anesthesia, regional anesthesia, or sedation will be administered [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The purpose of the guidelines is not only to minimize the risk of pulmonary aspiration but also to avoid case delays as well as prolonged fasting leading to dehydration, hypoglycemia, and patient dissatisfaction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. More conservative fasting intervals may be clinically appropriate in patients with comorbidities that impact gastric emptying such as pregnancy, obesity, hiatal hernia, ileus or bowel obstruction, or patients with a known or anticipated difficult airway, or gastroesophageal reflux disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Recommendations for Clear Liquids: At Least 2 Hours**
The primary support for the task force recommendations comes from a meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials comparing fasting times for clear liquids of 2 to 4 hours versus more than 4 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Adult patients fasting for 2 to 4 hours had smaller gastric volumes and higher gastric pH values compared to those fasting more than 4 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The differences in gastric volumes were equivocal in children [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Therefore, the task force recommendations are unchanged for healthy patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Examples of clear liquids include, but are not limited to, water, fruit juices without pulp, carbonated beverages, clear tea, and black coffee [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Clear liquids do not include alcohol [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Of note, several European anesthesia societies and the Canadian Pediatric Anesthesia Society recommend a 1-hour clear fluid policy for healthy pediatric patients, as they feel evidence shows no increased risk of pulmonary aspiration and equivocal gastric emptying compared to 2-hour fasting [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Recommendations for Breast Milk: At Least 4 Hours**
The fasting recommendations for breast milk are also unchanged, as the task force found only equivocal findings regarding gastric fluid volume and pH from observational studies in infants [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Recommendations for Infant Formula, Nonhuman Milk, and Light Meal: At Least 6 Hours**
Again, among the observational and randomized control studies, the task force found no evidence to support any change in the previous recommendations for at least a 6-hour fast before elective procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. They noted that the amount and type of food must be considered, and recommended at least 8 hours before elective procedures for fried or fatty food that typically delays gastric emptying time [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Summary of Fasting Recommendations for all Ages to Reduce the Risk of Pulmonary Aspiration:**
* **Clear liquids:** 2 h [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Breast milk:** 4 h [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Infant formula:** 6 h [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Nonhuman milk:** 6 h [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Light meal:** 6 h [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Fatty or Fried Foods:** 8 h [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

### 5.3.3. PHARMACOLOGIC AGENTS TO REDUCE THE RISK OF PULMONARY ASPIRATION

Many different pharmacologic approaches have been used to decrease the volume and increase the pH of gastric fluid in an effort to reduce the risk of aspiration pneumonitis, such as histamine-2 (H-2) receptor antagonists, proton pump inhibitors (PPIs), antacids, antiemetics, and gastrokinetic agents [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The ASA task force reviewed the literature and surveyed both experts and ASA members to arrive at their 2017 recommendations for pharmacologic agents [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. They found that the literature is insufficient to evaluate or support the effect of administering any of these classes of drugs on the perioperative incidence of emesis/reflux or pulmonary aspiration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Therefore, they could not recommend the routine preoperative use of such drugs for patients who have no apparent increased risk for pulmonary aspiration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, the drugs were found to be effective for their intended use and are more cost-effective when prescribed for patients who have risk factors for pulmonary aspiration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Histamine-2 (H-2) Receptor Antagonists:** Meta-analyses of randomized placebo-controlled trials support the efficacy of the H-2 receptor antagonists cimetidine, ranitidine, and famotidine in reducing gastric volume and acidity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. They block the ability of histamine to induce secretion of gastric fluid with a high hydrogen ion concentration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Multiple-dose regimens may be more effective in increasing gastric pH than a single dose before operation on the day of surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

* **Cimetidine:** Cimetidine is usually administered in 150 to 300 mg doses orally or parenterally [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The gastric effects of cimetidine last as long as 3 or 4 hours; thus, this drug is suitable for operations of that duration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Side effects of cimetidine include inhibition of the hepatic mixed-function oxidase enzyme system [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. More seriously, life-threatening cardiac dysrhythmias, hypotension, cardiac arrest, and central nervous system depression have been reported after cimetidine administration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These side effects may be especially likely to occur in critically ill patients after rapid intravenous administration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Ranitidine:** Ranitidine is more potent, specific, and longer acting than cimetidine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The usual oral dose of 150 mg or 50 mg given parenterally will increase gastric fluid pH within 1 hour [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is as effective as cimetidine and produces fewer cardiovascular or central nervous system side effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The effects of ranitidine last up to 9 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Thus, it may be superior to cimetidine at the conclusion of lengthy procedures in reducing the risk of aspiration pneumonitis during emergence from anesthesia and extubation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
* **Famotidine:** Famotidine is another H-2 receptor blocker that is given preoperatively to raise gastric fluid pH, typically administered as 40 mg orally 1.5 to 3 hours preoperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The pharmacokinetics are similar to those of cimetidine and ranitidine, with the exception of having a longer serum elimination half-life than the other two drugs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Proton Pump Inhibitors (PPIs):** PPIs suppress gastric acid secretion in a dose-dependent manner by binding to the proton pump of the parietal cell [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Meta-analysis of placebo controlled RCTs support their efficacy in reducing gastric volume and acidity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For adult patients, oral doses of 40 mg to 80 mg must be given 2 to 4 hours before surgery to be effective, or omeprazole 40 mg intravenously 30 minutes before induction has been used [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The effect on gastric pH may last as long as 24 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Antacids:** Antacids are used to neutralize the acid already present in gastric contents [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Placebo controlled RCTs demonstrate their effectiveness [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A single dose of antacid given 15 to 30 minutes before induction of anesthesia is almost 100% effective in increasing gastric fluid pH above 2.5 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Although colloid antacid suspensions may be more effective in increasing gastric fluid pH, aspiration of particulate antacids may cause significant and persistent pulmonary damage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Nonparticulate antacids do not produce pulmonary damage themselves if aspiration should occur [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Consequently, the nonparticulate antacid, 0.3 M sodium citrate, is commonly used [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Withholding antacids because of concern about increasing gastric volume is not warranted, as it is more important to raise the pH [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Animal evidence showed increased mortality after aspiration of low volumes of acidic gastric fluid (0.3 mL/kg, pH 1) compared to aspiration of large volumes of buffered gastric fluid (1 to 2 mL/kg, pH > 1.8) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Gastrokinetic Agents: Metoclopramide:** Metoclopramide is a dopamine antagonist with antiemetic properties that stimulates upper gastrointestinal motility, increases gastroesophageal sphincter tone, and relaxes the pylorus and duodenum to reduce gastric volume [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A meta-analysis of randomized placebo-controlled trials supports the efficacy of metoclopramide to reduce gastric volume, but is equivocal regarding its effect on gastric acidity during the perioperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Thus it is helpful in patients with large gastric fluid volumes, such as parturients, patients scheduled for emergency surgery, obese patients, trauma patients, and those with gastroparesis secondary to diabetes mellitus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is not recommended for patients diagnosed with bowel obstruction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It may be administered orally or parenterally [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A parenteral dose of 5 mg to 10 mg is usually given 30 minutes before induction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Administration intravenously over 3 to 5 minutes usually prevents the abdominal cramping that can occur from more rapid administration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. An oral dose of 10 mg achieves onset within 30 to 60 minutes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The elimination half-life of metoclopramide is approximately 2 to 4 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The combination of metoclopramide with an H-2 receptor antagonist does not decrease the effect of either drug, and the effects may be additive [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

As reviewed earlier, the drugs used to decrease gastric fluid volume and acidity are effective and relatively free of side effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The use of these agents is warranted in patients with decreased gastric emptying, reflux, and those presenting for emergency procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, none of the drugs or combination of drugs is absolutely reliable in preventing the risk of aspiration in all patients all of the time [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Therefore, one must still employ anesthetic techniques to protect the airway during induction, maintenance, and emergence from anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Pulmonary aspiration risk management starts with appropriate fasting guidelines: 2h for clear liquids, 4h for breast milk, 6h for light meals/formula, and 8h for fatty foods. Pharmacologic agents (H2-blockers, PPIs, non-particulate antacids, metoclopramide) are effective for reducing gastric acidity and volume but aren't recommended routinely for patients without aspiration risk factors. For high-risk patients, consider combination therapy with different mechanisms of action.

# 6. PSYCHOLOGICAL PREPARATION/PREMEDICATION




## 6.1. PSYCHOLOGICAL PREPARATION

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to patient preparation]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Communication and trust-building]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareAnesthetic management for patients begins with preoperative psychological preparation and, if necessary, preoperative medication [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The anesthesiologist should assess the patient's mental and physical condition during the preoperative visit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Because it is actually the beginning of the anesthetic, the decision to administer preoperative medication, and which one, should be based on the same considerations as the choice of anesthesia, including considerations of the patient's medical problems, requirements of the surgery, and recovery goals [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Satisfactory preparation lessens the patient's (and family's) anxiety and smooths the anesthetic induction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. No consensus exists on the choice of preoperative medications and, historically, their use was dominated by tradition [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, the ever-increasing number of outpatient procedures has led to a significant decrease in preoperative sedation to facilitate rapid recovery from anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Psychological preparation of the patient involves a preoperative visit and interview with the patient and family members [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The anesthesiologist should explain anticipated events and the proposed anesthetic management in an effort to reduce anxiety [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A growing number of patients receive their preanesthetic evaluations by others in preoperative evaluation clinics, such that their initial encounter with the anesthesiologist may be in the immediate preoperative period outside of the OR [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Preoperative visits must be conducted efficiently, but they must also be informative and reassuring [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Most of the anesthesiologist's time is spent with the patient unconscious or sedated; therefore, time must be taken before the operation to earn the trust and confidence of that patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Studies show that, depending on the intensity of inquiry, 40% to 85% of patients are apprehensive before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Most patients expect their apprehension to be relieved before they arrive in the OR, but an informative and comforting preoperative visit may replace many milligrams of a sedative medication [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, more patients were adequately prepared for surgery after a preoperative interview than after 2 mg/kg of pentobarbital given intramuscularly 1 hour before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. After the patient interview, the use of preoperative medication in selected patients serves to achieve sedation or amnesia as well as provide any needed analgesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Preoperative depressant drugs though, should not be used as a substitute for a comforting and tactful preoperative visit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Psychological preparation through a thorough, informative preoperative visit can significantly reduce patient anxiety and may be more effective than pharmacological sedation. Take time to explain the anesthetic plan, build trust, and address concerns, especially since many patients are apprehensive before surgery.

## 6.2. PREMEDICATION

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#Perioperative Physiology|ⓘ|Part A: Pharmacology of premedication agents]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to premedication]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Patient comfort and safety]]

**Exam Weighting:** Medium

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareThe ideal drug or combination of drugs for preoperative pharmacologic preparation is as elusive as is the ideal anesthetic technique and is not based on comprehensive or definitive data [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In selecting the appropriate drug and dose for preoperative medication, the patient's psychological condition, physical status, age, and prior response to depressant drugs must be considered [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Some patients should not receive depressant drugs before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, those with little physiologic reserve, head injury, hypovolemia, or at the extremes of age may receive more harm than benefit from premedication [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Finally, the surgical procedure, expected duration, and postoperative discharge plan are important factors as well [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The goals to be achieved for each patient with preoperative medication should be tailored to the individual [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Some goals, such as relief of anxiety, apply to almost every patient, whereas a goal to dry airway secretions may be reserved for the patient with a potentially difficult airway who may require fiberoptic tracheal intubation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

The timing and route of administration of the preoperative medications are important [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Every attempt should be made to time the administration so that the premedication achieves its full effect before the patient's arrival in the OR [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. As a general rule, oral tablets should be given to the patient 60 minutes before arrival in the OR [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Intravenous agents, on the other hand, produce effects after a few circulation times [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The drug(s), doses, route of administration, and effects should be recorded on the anesthetic record [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Common Preoperative Medications, Doses, and Administration Routes:**
* **Lorazepam:** Oral, IV - 0.5–4 mg [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Midazolam:** IV - 1.0–2.5 mg doses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Fentanyl:** IV - 25–50 μg doses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Morphine:** IV - 1.0–2.5 mg doses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Meperidine:** IV - 10–25 mg doses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Cimetidine:** Oral, IV - 150–300 mg [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Ranitidine:** Oral - 150 mg, IV - 50 mg [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Metoclopramide:** IV - 5–10 mg [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Atropine:** IV - 0.2–0.4 mg [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Glycopyrrolate:** IV - 0.1–0.2 mg [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

### 6.2.1. BENZODIAZEPINES

Benzodiazepines are among the most popular preoperative medications because they produce anxiolysis, amnesia, and sedation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Because the site of action of benzodiazepines is located on the GABA receptor in the central nervous system, there is relatively little depression of the ventilatory or cardiovascular systems with dosages used for premedication [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Benzodiazepines have a wide therapeutic index and a low incidence of toxicity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Other than central nervous system depression, these drugs lack the side effects common to opioids, such as nausea and vomiting [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, benzodiazepines may not always produce a calming effect, but occasionally cause a paradoxical agitation manifested as restlessness and delirium [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Benzodiazepines are not analgesic agents [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Comparison of Pharmacologic Variables of Benzodiazepines:**
* **Diazepam:**
  * Dose equivalent (mg): 10 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Time to peak effect after oral dose (h): 1-1.5 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Elimination half-time (h): 20-40 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Clearance (mL/kg/min): 0.2-0.5 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Lorazepam:**
  * Dose equivalent (mg): 1-2 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Time to peak effect after oral dose (h): 2-4 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Elimination half-time (h): 10-20 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Clearance (mL/kg/min): 0.7-1.0 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Midazolam:**
  * Dose equivalent (mg): 3-5 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Time to peak effect after oral dose (h): 0.5-1 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Elimination half-time (h): 1-4 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Clearance (mL/kg/min): 6.4-11.1 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Midazolam:** Midazolam has predominantly replaced lorazepam and diazepam for preoperative medication and moderate sedation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is common to administer sedative doses intravenously just prior to transfer to the OR [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. As with other benzodiazepines, midazolam produces anxiolysis, sedation, and amnesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is two to three times more potent than diazepam because of its increased affinity for the GABA receptor [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The usual incremental dose is 1 mg to 2 mg titrated intravenously [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There is no irritation or phlebitis with injection of midazolam, as opposed to diazepam [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The incidence of side effects after administration is low, although depression of ventilation and sedation may be greater than expected, especially in elderly patients or when the drug is combined with other central nervous system depressants [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Compared to diazepam, intravenous midazolam has a rapid onset of 1 to 2 minutes, and more rapid recovery due to its lipid solubility, rapid redistribution to the peripheral tissues, and metabolic biotransformation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For these reasons, midazolam should usually be given within 1 hour of induction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The elimination half-life of midazolam is approximately 1 to 4 hours and may be extended in the elderly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Tests show that mental function usually returns to normal within 4 hours of administration, and amnesia may only last 20 to 30 minutes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. These properties make midazolam ideal for shorter procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Lorazepam and Diazepam:** Lorazepam is 5 to 10 times more potent than diazepam and can produce profound amnesia, anxiolysis, and sedation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Like diazepam, it has an extremely long half-life, but an even longer duration because of its affinity for the receptor [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Because of their duration, lorazepam and diazepam are not useful in instances in which rapid awakening is necessary, such as outpatient anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Their use may be more suited for those patients already taking chronic benzodiazepines for anxiety and who may need anxiolysis prior to arrival in the preoperative area [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Lorazepam intravenous administration is not associated with pain on injection or phlebitis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In addition to the intravenous route, lorazepam is reliably absorbed orally with demonstrated clinical effects 30 to 60 minutes after oral administration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Peak plasma concentrations may not occur until 2 to 4 hours after oral administration, so oral lorazepam must be given well before surgery so that the drug has time to be effective [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. With recommended doses, anterograde amnesia may be produced for as long as 4 to 6 hours without excessive sedation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Higher doses lead to prolonged and excessive sedation without more amnesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There are no active metabolites of lorazepam and because its metabolism is not dependent on microsomal enzymes, there is less influence from age or liver disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. As with diazepam, little cardiorespiratory depression occurs with lorazepam [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 6.2.2. DIPHENHYDRAMINE

Diphenhydramine is a histamine-1 receptor antagonist, which blocks the peripheral effects of histamine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It also has sedative, anticholinergic, and antiemetic activity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A dose of 50 mg will last 3 to 6 hours in an adult [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Diphenhydramine is not often used for preoperative sedation, but it is often used in combination with histamine-2 blockers and steroids for prophylaxis in patients with latex allergy as well as for prophylaxis before chemotherapy and radiologic studies using contrast [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 6.2.3. OPIOIDS

Morphine and meperidine were commonly used opioids in the past for intramuscular preoperative medication during a time when the majority of patients were admitted the night before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Currently, when analgesia is needed preoperatively, the administration of intravenous fentanyl, with its rapid onset and shorter duration, has become much more common [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In patients not experiencing pain, opioids may produce dysphoria [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Opioids given in premedication dosages do not produce sedation or amnesia and are often combined with a benzodiazepine for these effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Opioids are also useful to ameliorate the discomfort during regional anesthesia procedures or during the insertion of invasive monitoring catheters or large intravenous lines [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The analgesic properties and respiratory depressant effects of opioids usually go hand in hand, and the decrease in the CO2 drive at the medullary respiratory center may be prolonged [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Furthermore, there is a decrease in the responsiveness to hypoxia at the carotid body after injection of even low doses of opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The anesthesiologist may consider supplemental oxygen for the patient receiving opioid premedication [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. A common side effect of all opioids is nausea and vomiting, due to effects on both the chemoreceptor trigger zone and the vestibular system [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Fentanyl:** Fentanyl is a synthetic opioid agonist structurally similar to meperidine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is approximately 100 times more potent than morphine in its analgesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The lipid solubility of fentanyl is greater than that of morphine, which contributes to its rapid onset of action [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Peak plasma concentrations occur within 6 to 7 minutes following intravenous administration and its elimination half-time is 3 to 6 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The drug's much shorter duration of action is attributed to redistribution to inactive tissues, such as the lungs, fat, and skeletal muscle [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Metabolism occurs primarily by N-demethylation to norfentanyl, which is a less potent analgesic [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In doses of 1 to 2 μg/kg intravenously, fentanyl may be used to provide preoperative analgesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Fentanyl causes neither myocardial depression nor histamine release, but may be associated with ventilatory depression and profound bradycardia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Elderly and debilitated patients can have an increased sensitivity to its effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Synergistic effects with benzodiazepines warrant close observation when this combination is given in the preoperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Opioid-Dependent Patients and the Opioid Epidemic:** Opioid overdose has been a leading cause of death for Americans, and many people who ultimately abuse heroin or fentanyl started with prescription oral opioids [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Many addicts state their abuse began with their personal or an acquaintance's prescription, emphasizing the importance in avoiding overprescribing and identifying at-risk individuals [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Risk factors for opioid misuse include current opioid use, history of opioid use, current or past substance abuse, smoking, psychiatric illness such as anxiety or depression, chronic pain, younger age, and higher medical comorbidities [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For these individuals, a multimodal pain treatment program is beneficial, such as nonopioid medications, regional anesthesia and counseling [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The anesthesiologist can play a key role in setting expectations for analgesia postoperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Those individuals who are opioid dependent or have a past history of abuse require special consideration and planning which can start with the preoperative visit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Working with the patient's pain or addiction medicine specialist can help formulate a safe plan to avoid relapse [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Withdrawal symptoms produced by drug cessation preoperatively are an issue for the patient who is opioid dependent [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There should be an attempt to maintain opioid use at the usual level by continuing methadone or substituting other appropriate agents for methadone [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The anesthesiologist should be cautioned about using agonist–antagonist drugs in these patients for fear of precipitating withdrawal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 6.2.4. DEXMEDETOMIDINE

Dexmedetomidine is a highly selective alpha-2 adrenoreceptor agonist acting in the locus ceruleus that produces sedation similar to natural sleep and anxiolysis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Unlike opiates and benzodiazepines, it does so without respiratory depression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is thought to be neuroprotective and to provide analgesic and antiemetic effects [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, it can lead to hypotension and bradycardia due to its inhibition of norepinephrine release and sympathetic activity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It can be given in a variety of ways: oral, intranasal, intramuscular, and intravenously [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Intranasal doses range from 1 to 3 mcg/kg, and intravenous doses start at 0.5 to 1 mcg/kg given over 10 minutes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. It is not as fast acting as midazolam or fentanyl; when given intranasally it requires 30 minutes for effect and 15 minutes for intravenous administration, thus appropriate planning and timing are necessary [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Dexmedetomidine is eliminated through hepatic biotransformation with an elimination half-life of 2 to 3 hours, potentially delaying recovery for short procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

### 6.2.5. ANTICHOLINERGICS

Historically, anticholinergic drugs were widely used when inhalation anesthetics produced copious respiratory tract secretions and intraoperative bradycardia was a frequent danger [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The advent of newer inhalation agents has markedly decreased the routine use of anticholinergic drugs for preoperative medication [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Specific indications for an anticholinergic before surgery are (1) antisialagogue effect, (2) sedation and amnesia, and (3) vagolytic effect [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Comparison of Effects of Three Anticholinergic Drugs:**
* **Atropine:**
  * Increased heart rate: + + + [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Antisialagogue: + [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Sedation: + [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Glycopyrrolate:**
  * Increased heart rate: + + [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Antisialagogue: + + [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Sedation: 0 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
* **Scopolamine:**
  * Increased heart rate: + [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Antisialagogue: + [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]
  * Sedation: + + + [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]

**Antisialagogue Effect:** Anticholinergics have been prescribed in a selective fashion when drying of the upper airway is desirable [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, when awake fiberoptic tracheal intubation or bronchoscopic examination is planned, conditions will be more satisfactory for visualization when an anticholinergic drug has been administered [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Anticholinergics are also felt to increase the effectiveness of topical anesthesia in the airway by preventing a dilutional effect from secretions and facilitating contact of the local anesthetic with the mucosa [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Glycopyrrolate is the most potent antisialagogue, with less likelihood of increasing heart rate than atropine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Because glycopyrrolate is a quaternary amine, it does not easily cross the blood–brain barrier and does not produce sedation or amnesia as seen with scopolamine, a tertiary amine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Glycopyrrolate can be useful for counteracting the increased secretions caused by ketamine, or for producing a drier surgical field for intraoral surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Sedation and Amnesia:** Although midazolam has largely taken over as the preoperative sedative of choice, historically, scopolamine was often given intramuscularly in combination with an opioid [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Scopolamine may not be as effective as lorazepam or diazepam in preventing recall and causing amnesia, but it does have an additive amnestic effect when combined with benzodiazepines [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The combination of diazepam and scopolamine produces amnesia more reliably than diazepam alone [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. This additive amnestic effect may be useful for the unstable patient who cannot tolerate general anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Vagolytic Action:** The vagolytic action of the anticholinergic drugs is produced through the blockade of acetylcholine at the sinoatrial node [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Atropine given intravenously is more potent than glycopyrrolate in increasing heart rate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The vagolytic action of the anticholinergic drugs is useful in the prevention of reflex bradycardia that may result from traction on extraocular muscles or abdominal viscera, carotid sinus stimulation, or repeated doses of succinylcholine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

#### 6.2.5.1. SIDE EFFECTS OF ANTICHOLINERGIC DRUGS

**Central Nervous System Toxicity:** Scopolamine and atropine (tertiary amines) may cause central nervous system toxicity, known as central anticholinergic syndrome [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. This syndrome is most likely to occur after the administration of scopolamine, but can be seen after high doses of atropine and may include symptoms such as delirium, restlessness, confusion, and obtundation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Elderly patients and patients with pain appear to be particularly susceptible; the syndrome has been noted to be potentiated by inhalation anesthetics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The administration of 1 to 2 mg of physostigmine intravenously can successfully treat the syndrome [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Intraocular Pressure:** Mydriasis and cycloplegia from anticholinergic drugs may place patients with glaucoma at risk for increased intraocular pressure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Atropine and glycopyrrolate may be less likely to increase intraocular pressure than scopolamine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. In patients with glaucoma, it is generally safe to continue most glaucoma medications up until the time of surgery or use atropine or glycopyrrolate when necessary [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Hyperthermia:** The sweat glands of the body are innervated by the sympathetic nervous system, but use cholinergic transmission [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Therefore, administration of anticholinergic agents interferes with the sweating mechanism which may cause body temperature to increase, especially when using active warming devices in the OR [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The patient typically appears hot and dry [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].
