In children, the subsequent increase in temperature may exacerbate tachycardia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Premedication should be individualized based on patient factors, procedure type, and specific goals. Benzodiazepines (especially midazolam) are most commonly used for anxiolysis and amnesia. Opioids provide analgesia but can cause respiratory depression. Dexmedetomidine provides sedation without respiratory depression but may cause bradycardia. Anticholinergics have specific indications including antisialagogue effects for awake fiberoptic intubation and prevention of bradycardia.

# 7. ANTIBIOTIC PROPHYLAXIS




> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ|Part B: Clinical approach to antimicrobial prophylaxis]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#Medical Knowledge|ⓘ|Part C: Quality improvement and infection prevention]]

**Competencies:** Medical Knowledge, Patient CareThe goal of surgical antibiotic prophylaxis is to prevent surgical site infections and the direct consequences stemming from them such as morbidity and mortality, health care resource utilization and cost [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. At the same time, antimicrobial prophylaxis should have minimal adverse drug effects and impact on the patient's microbial flora [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Antimicrobial prophylaxis has become an important outcome measure such that properly timed and dosed administration impacts hospital reimbursement or even accreditation status [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Anesthesiologists frequently administer antibiotics to patients prior to surgery for contaminated and clean-contaminated procedures or for clean surgical procedures when infection would be catastrophic, such as for device implants [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Other indications for the use of prophylactic antibiotics include the prevention of endocarditis and the prevention of infection in immunocompromised patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

Cephalosporins, such as cefazolin, are the most popular antibiotics because they cover common skin microbes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. However, for intestinal surgery anaerobic and gram-negative coverage is needed, and those colonized with methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus require decolonization protocols and intraoperative vancomycin [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. The National Surgical Infection Project recommends that antibiotics be administered within 1 hour prior to incision [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. There are two exceptions to this policy: (1) vancomycin should be given 2 hours prior to incision and (2) when a tourniquet is used, the antibiotics should be administered prior to its inflation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Furthermore, if the surgical procedure is prolonged, it is recommended that the antibiotic be redosed when two half-lives have elapsed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. For example, cefazolin has a half-life of 2 hours and should be redosed if the surgical procedure extends past 4 hours [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Research on morbidly obese patients has shown that the dose required to achieve adequate tissue levels is twice that for normal-weight patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]]. Those with a proven allergy to penicillin, cephalosporins, and related compounds (β-lactam allergy) may receive either vancomycin or clindamycin [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Surgical antibiotic prophylaxis should be administered within 1 hour of incision (2 hours for vancomycin) and before tourniquet inflation when applicable. Cefazolin is most commonly used due to its coverage of skin flora. For prolonged procedures, redose when two half-lives have elapsed. Higher doses are needed for morbidly obese patients. For β-lactam allergies, vancomycin or clindamycin are alternatives.

**Summary of Patient Preparation:**

The proper preparation of a patient for surgery and anesthesia includes both medical optimization and psychological preparation. It begins with the preoperative visit to establish trust and rapport, explain proposed anesthetic techniques, and alleviate anxiety. Patients should receive detailed instruction about which medications to take before surgery, appropriate fasting guidelines, and realistic expectations about the perioperative course.

Physical preparation includes smoking cessation (ideally 4-8 weeks before surgery), management of chronic medications, prophylaxis against aspiration in high-risk patients, and appropriate premedication when indicated. Preoperative antibiotics should be administered within 1 hour of incision for procedures with infection risk.

These measures, when properly implemented, reduce perioperative complications, improve patient comfort and satisfaction, and optimize outcomes.
